\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}g^{2}C_{\rm A}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\Delta^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta^{\beta\sigma}(k)\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\nu\rho\sigma}(0,k,-k)\,,

(3.34)

where

\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)=2k_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}-k_{\alpha}g_{\mu\beta}-k_{\beta}g_{\mu\alpha}\,.

(3.35)

On the other hand,

({\tilde{a}}_{2})_{\mu\nu}
remains unchanged,

({\tilde{a}}_{2})_{\mu\nu}={\tilde{a}}_{2\mu\nu}
.
Since both contributions
are proportional to
g_{\mu\nu}
, we set
(
{\tilde{a}}_{1})_{\mu\nu}={\tilde{a}}_{1}g_{\mu\nu}

and
({\tilde{a}}_{2})_{\mu\nu}={\tilde{a}}_{2}g_{\mu\nu}
,
with

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{1}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\Delta^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta^{\beta\sigma}(k)\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}(0,k,-k)\,,

(3.36)

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{2}=

\displaystyle\,-g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{(d-1)}{d}\int_{k}\Delta^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(k)\,.

(3.37)

Now, assuming that the vertex
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is pole-free at
q=0
(
i.e.
, no Schwinger mechanism), the Taylor expansion of the Abelian STI of Eq. (
2.59
) yields the WI,

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=\frac{\partial\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{-1}(r)}{\partial r^{\alpha}}\,.

(3.38)

Note that the above formula
is valid also at tree level,
due to the fact that

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}
depends on
\xi_{Q}
,

i.e.
,

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=(q-r)_{\nu}g_{\alpha\mu}+(r-p)_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}+(p-q)_{\mu}g_{\nu\alpha}+\xi_{Q}^{-1}(g_{\alpha\nu}r_{\mu}-g_{\alpha\mu}p_{\nu})\,.

(3.39)

Combining the above with Eq. (
3.36
), and noting that

\Delta^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta^{\beta\sigma}(k)\frac{\partial\Delta^{-1}_{\rho\sigma}(k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}=-\frac{\partial\Delta^{\alpha\beta}(k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}\,,

(3.40)

we obtain

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{1}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\frac{\partial\Delta^{\alpha\beta}(k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\left\{\int_{k}\frac{\partial[\Delta^{\alpha\beta}(k)\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)]}{\partial k_{\mu}}-\int_{k}\Delta^{\alpha\beta}(k)\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}\right\}\,,

(3.41)

where an integration by parts was performed to obtain the last line.

At this point, it is straightforward to show that

\Delta^{\alpha\beta}(k)\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)=2(d-1)k_{\mu}\Delta(k^{2})\,,\qquad\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}=2(d-1)g_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3.42)

such that

{\tilde{a}}_{1}=g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{(d-1)}{d}\int_{k}\Delta^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(k)-g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{(d-1)}{d}\int_{k}\frac{{\cal F}_{\mu}(k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}\,,

(3.43)

where

{\cal F}_{\mu}(k):=k_{\mu}\Delta(k^{2})\,.

(3.44)

Finally, combining Eqs. (
3.43
) and (
3.37
), yields

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}(0)={\tilde{a}}_{1}+{\tilde{a}}_{2}=-g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{(d-1)}{d}\underbrace{\int_{k}\frac{{\cal F}_{\mu}(k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}}_{\text{seagull identity}}=0\,,

(3.45)

where we used the compact version of the seagull identity given by Eq. (
3.10
).

We next turn to the ghost-loop
diagrams
{\tilde{a}}_{3}
and

{\tilde{a}}_{4}
in
Fig.
2.5
, which comprise

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
;
their expressions for general

q
are given in Eq. (
2.73
).
Evidently, graph
{\tilde{a}}_{3}
is
q
-independent,
and directly proportional to

g_{\mu\nu}
. As for

{\tilde{a}}_{3}
,
after setting

q=0
in the corresponding expression in Eq. (
2.73
),
the result also
depends on
g_{\mu\nu}

alone. Specifically, we obtain

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{3}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k_{\mu}D^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma^{\mu}(-k,k,0)\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{4}=

\displaystyle\,g^{2}C_{\rm A}\int_{k}D(k^{2})\,.

(3.46)

Then, if the ghost-gluon vertex
{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
is pole-free, the Taylor expansion of Eq. (
2.59
) leads to the WI

{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\alpha}(r,-r,0)=\frac{\partial D^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{\alpha}}=2r_{\alpha}\frac{\partial D^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}\,,

(3.47)

which, when substituted into Eq. (
3.46
) (with
r\to-k
), yields

{\tilde{a}}_{3}=\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}\frac{\partial D(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}\,.

(3.48)

Hence, combining Eqs. (
3.46
) and (
3.48
),

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(0)={\tilde{a}}_{3}+{\tilde{a}}_{4}=\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\underbrace{\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\frac{\partial D(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{d}{2}\int_{k}D(k^{2})\right]}_{\text{seagull identity}}=0\,,

(3.49)

where we have used the version of the seagull identity given in
Eq. (
3.12
).

Note that
this demonstration does not assume any particular form for the gluon propagator,
\Delta(q^{2})
. In fact,
quite interestingly,
even if the gluon propagator were to be made massive by hand, the seagull identity would require that mass to vanish
[
54
]
.

4  Schwinger mechanism in
QCD: general notions

Schwinger’s fundamental observation on gauge invariance
and vector meson mass
[
90
,
91
]
may be summarized in a modern language as follows:
If the dimensionless vacuum polarization
of the vector meson develops a pole with positive residue
at zero momentum transfer, then
the vector meson acquires a mass, even if the gauge symmetry forbids a mass term at the
level of the fundamental Lagrangian.

To see in some detail how this general idea is realized,
it is convenient to introduce precisely
the dimensionless
vacuum polarization mentioned by Schwinger; we will denote
this function by
{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})
, and define it as

\Pi(q^{2})=q^{2}{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})
.
Then, from the
second relation
of Eq. (
2.22
), written
in Euclidean space,
we have that

\Delta^{-1}(q^{2})=q^{2}[1+{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})]
.

Then, the Schwinger condition that at
zero momentum transfer

{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})

develops a pole
with a positive residue,

c^{2}
,
means that

\lim_{q^{2}\to 0}{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})=c^{2}/q^{2}\,.

(4.1)

In what follows we will refer
to this type of pole as
a
massless pole

or a

Schwinger pole
.

Evidently, if
Eq. (
4.1
) holds, then

\,\,\lim_{q^{2}\to 0}\,\Delta^{-1}(q^{2})=\lim_{q^{2}\to 0}\,(q^{2}+c^{2})\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,\,\Delta^{-1}(0)=c^{2}\,,

(4.2)

where the residue of the pole acts as the effective
squared mass,
m^{2}
,
of the vector meson,

e.g.
, one carries out the identification

c^{2}=m^{2}
.
It is important to emphasize that in the
absence of interactions
the vector meson (or gauge boson) remains
massless, since

g=0
implies
{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})=0
.

The most celebrated example where this mechanism was first showcased is
the so-called “Schwinger model”, namely
QED
2
with massless fermions
[
91
,
241
,
242
]
.
Due to the particularities of the
two-dimensional Dirac matrices,
the one-loop vacuum polarization diagram
(
i.e.
, Fig.
3.2
with all its components set
to their tree-level values)
is the only possible quantum correction
that the photon propagator
may receive. Then, an elementary
calculation shows that
the photon acquires a mass,
given by the exact formula

m^{2}_{\gamma}=e^{2}/\pi
, where
e
is
the dimensionful electric charge in
d=2
.

It is important to emphasize that the
standard Higgs mechanism is a
very special
case of the Schwinger mechanism. In particular, in this case the
gauge boson mass,
M
, is given
by
M=gv/2
, where
v
is the vacuum expectation value of a fundamental scalar
field
\phi
; evidently, the gauge boson mass vanishes when
the gauge coupling is set to zero.
Since the Euclidean gauge boson
propagator becomes

\Delta^{-1}(q^{2})=q^{2}+M^{2}=q^{2}\left(1+\frac{g^{2}v^{2}}{4q^{2}}\right)\,,

(4.3)

it is clear that, in the terminology of the
Schwinger mechanism, the square of the vacuum expectation value of the scalar field plays the role of the residue of the pole.
Note, in addition, a pivotal
physical difference between the Higgs mechanism and the Schwinger mechanism
taking place in QCD:
while the Higgs mechanism is accompanied by a
fundamental scalar excitations, namely
the Higgs boson, the QCD spectrum remains
completely unaffected by the
action of the Schwinger mechanism.

Turning to Yang-Mills theories in
d=4
,
and in particular QCD,
the natural question that arises
is what makes the gluon vacuum polarization function
{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})

exhibit massless poles, given the absence of elementary scalar fields.
The starting observation for addressing this question
is that the
fully-dressed vertices
of the theory generate
massless scalar excitations

dynamically
. In particular, the required
Schwinger poles arise as composite bound state excitations, produced
through
the fusion of two gluons
or of a ghost-antighost pair
into a
color-carrying
scalar,

\Phi^{a}
,
of vanishing mass
[
92
,
93
,
138
,
31
,
139
,
96
,
97
,
98
,
140
,
141
,
142
]
.
Evidently, since these
excitations carry color, they do not appear as observable states.
The formation of these states
is controlled by special BSEs;
it may be understood as the limiting case of the production of a
bound state whose mass shrinks to zero when the
theory becomes sufficiently strongly coupled, as is the case of QCD.
Given that the fully-dressed vertices enter in the
diagrammatic expansion of the gluon self-energy
(see Fig.
2.4
), their poles
are finally transmitted
to
{\bf\Pi}(q^{2})
,
giving rise to Eq. (
4.1
),
and through it to an effective mass scale for the gluon
[
31
,
46
,
47
,
77
,
99
]
.

In what follows we will elaborate in detail on two main aspects associated with the realization of the Schwinger mechanism in QCD.
First, we will show how the emergence of massless poles in the fundamental vertices gives rise to a gluon mass, namely the way that the key sequence captured by Eqs. (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) proceeds within the intricate structure of the gauge sector of QCD. Second, we will address the equally fundamental issue of identifying the precise dynamics that drive the appearance of Schwinger poles in the vertices.

5  Schwinger poles: structure and action

The implementation of the Schwinger mechanism in QCD is intimately
connected with the appearance of
special irregularities in the fundamental
vertices of the theory, namely
of poles that manifest themselves as the incoming momenta tend to zero. In this section we
study in detail the nature of these poles and the way they operate at the level of the gluon propagator in order to generate the gluon mass scale.

5.1  Qualitative overview

The generation of a gluon mass scale in Yang-Mills theories
is intimately connected to
irregularities displayed
by the fundamental vertices
of the theory. In particular,
the vertices
possess special components
which are
composed by massless poles.
The effects of these components are transmitted to the gluon polarization function
through the corresponding SDE, which contains
these vertices
as its
main building blocks,
see Fig.
2.4
.
It is the collective effect of these poles that eventually
triggers the Schwinger mechanism
and gives rise to the desired gluon mass scale.

These observations prompt the separation of the
three-gluon and the ghost-gluon
vertices into a pole-free component,
which essentially describes the
physics in the absence of the
Schwinger mechanism,
and a pole part, which is purely
non-perturbative,
arising through the dynamical formation of massless bound-state excitations.

One prominent feature of these pole parts is that they are

completely longitudinal

[
92
,
93
,
81
,
31
,
46
,
94
,
47
,
95
,
96
,
97
,
98
,
54
,
55
,
140
,
143
,
141
,
142
,
77
,
99
,
100
]
.
For example, if we denote by
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
the pole part of the three-gluon vertex, the poles comprising it appear in the form
q_{\alpha}/q^{2}
,

r_{\mu}/r^{2}
,
p_{\nu}/p^{2}
,
and products thereof.
This particular property guarantees the decoupling of the poles from physical amplitudes, which otherwise would display strong divergences in certain kinematic limits.

The general tensorial structure of
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

is rather complicated, being
enforced both by the STIs satisfied by the three-gluon vertex and Bose symmetry.
Out of the entire pole structure of
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, the simple (order one) pole in the channel that carries the momentum that enters in the gluon SDE (denoted by
q
in Fig.
2.4
) is special, because its
residue function
, denoted by
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
, triggers the Schwinger mechanism.
The corresponding residue function
associated with the ghost-gluon vertex, to be denoted by

{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
,
acts in an exactly analogous
way.
Thus, considering
the
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}

component of the gluon
self-energy, which is free of seagull contributions, one may derive the equation that expresses the gluon mass scale as an integral involving precisely the pole
residues
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

and
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})

[
96
,
97
,
98
,
54
,
55
,
140
]
.

When the Schwinger mechanism is activated, the STIs obeyed by the vertices remain
intact
; however, they are now resolved with the nontrivial participation of the Schwinger poles.
In the soft-gluon limit (
q\to 0
), this observation leads to
a nontrivial modification
of the WI
satisfied by the pole-free parts of the vertices
[
95
,
98
,
54
,
142
,
77
,
99
]
,
which we coin “WI displacement”.
The pivotal observation
associated with this effect is that the displacement of the WI is controlled precisely by the corresponding
residue functions, which,
for this reason, are also denominated

displacement
functions

[
142
,
144
]
.

This WI displacement, in turn,
leads to the evasion of
the seagull cancellation
at the level of the
g_{\mu\nu}
component of the gluon propagator
[
96
,
97
,
98
,
54
,
55
,
140
]
.
In fact, one obtains

precisely
the
same expression for the
gluon mass scale derived
from the
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}

component, in absolute compliance with the
exact transversality of the gluon self-energy.

5.2  Fundamental QCD vertices with Schwinger poles

In what follows we will
consider the pole structure
of two of the QCD
vertices, namely the three-gluon
and ghost-gluon vertices,

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
and

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
, respectively,
introduced in
Subsection
2.2
. The four-gluon and quark-gluon vertices also
develop such poles
[
54
,
168
]
, but their
overall impact
is rather limited, and will be therefore
omitted in what follows.

Figure 5.1:
The diagrammatic representation of the three-gluon and ghost-gluon vertices introduced in Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.2
):

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
(first row) and
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
(second row).
The first term on the r.h.s. indicates the pole-free part,
\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
or
\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
,
while the second denotes the pole term
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
or
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
.

Given the key role played by the massless poles, it is natural at this point
to separate each vertex

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma
into two parts, as shown in
Fig.
5.1
:
the pole-free part, denoted
by
\Gamma
, and the part that
carries the Schwinger poles,
denoted by
V
.
In particular, for the three-gluon and ghost-gluon vertices, we write

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,,

(5.1)

and

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)+V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)\,.

(5.2)

A crucial restriction on the general form of
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
and
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
arises
from the requirement that
the massless poles be
longitudinally coupled.
This means that
poles in
q^{2}
,
r^{2}
, or
p^{2}

must be multiplied by

q^{\alpha}
,
r^{\mu}
, or

p^{\nu}
, respectively.
Similarly, double poles
are accompanied by two such
momenta;
for example, the double pole

1/q^{2}r^{2}
,
is multiplied by

q^{\alpha}r^{\mu}
.

The physical reason for imposing this
requirement is that, in this way,
the absence of strong divergences in
physical quantities, such as
S-matrix elements, is guaranteed.
Indeed,
the longitudinal nature of

{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
and
V_{\mu}(r,p,q)
annihilates them when
they get contracted by external conserved currents, or, equivalently, when they trigger the equations of motion (EoM) of the external particles.
For instance, for the three-gluon vertex in
Fig.
5.2
,
we have that (
q=p_{1}-p_{2}
)

q^{\alpha}\gamma_{\alpha}=\not{q}=(\underbrace{\not{p}_{1}-m}_{{\rm EoM}})-(\underbrace{\not{p}_{2}-m}_{{\rm EoM}})=0\,,

(5.3)

and similarly for
the other two legs.
Equivalently, one may say that
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}
must be annihilated when all its legs are contracted
by the corresponding projection tensors,
namely [see
Fig.
5.2
]

P^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(q)P^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}(r)P^{\nu^{\prime}}_{\nu}(p){\cal V}_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)=0\,,

(5.4)

and

P^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(q)V_{\alpha^{\prime}}(r,p,q)=0\,.

(5.5)

It is particularly important to emphasize that the longitudinal
nature of the Schwinger poles
is automatically enforced
within the bound state scenario
presented in Subsec.
7.2
.

Figure 5.2:
Cancellation of longitudinally coupled poles when contracted with conserved currents.

It is especially instructive to illustrate how non-longitudinal poles would
induce divergences to certain combinations of vertex
form factors, which are known
from lattice simulations
to be completely divergence-free.

To appreciate this point, consider the ghost-gluon vertex,
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
.
The tensorial decomposition of
\Gamma_{\alpha}
is given by

\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=r_{\alpha}B_{1}(r,p,q)+q_{\alpha}B_{2}(r,p,q)\,,

(5.6)

and if no restriction is imposed on the tensor structure of
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
, we have

V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left[r_{\alpha}C_{1}(r,p,q)+q_{\alpha}C_{2}(r,p,q)\right]\,.

(5.7)

Now, after amputating the external legs, the typical lattice “observable” associated with the Landau-gauge ghost-gluon vertex has the form
[
243
,
11
,
244
,
245
]

L_{\rm{gh}}(r,p,q)=\frac{\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha}(r,p,q)P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(q){\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}}(r,p,q)}{\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha}(r,p,q)P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(q)\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha^{\prime}}(r,p,q)}=\frac{\left[q^{2}r^{\alpha^{\prime}}-(q\cdot r)q^{\alpha^{\prime}}\right]{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}}(r,p,q)}{q^{2}r^{2}-(q\cdot r)^{2}}\,.

(5.8)

Then, assuming the general tensor structures of Eqs. (
5.6
) and (
5.7
), we have

L_{\rm{gh}}(r,p,q)=B_{1}(r,p,q)+\frac{1}{q^{2}}C_{1}(r,p,q)\,.

(5.9)

Hence,
L_{\rm{gh}}(r,p,q)
would contain a pole at
q=0
,
which, however, is not
observed in the available lattice data.
Thus,
C_{1}(r,p,q)

must be vanishing sufficiently fast
in the limit
q\to 0
for the pole to become evitable, or, equivalently,
to be absorbed into a redefinition of
the
B_{2}(r,p,q)

in Eq. (
5.6
).

Therefore,
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
is strictly longitudinal, in which case we can drop the index “2” in
C_{2}(r,p,q)
and write simply

V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=\frac{q_{\alpha}}{q^{2}}C(r,p,q)\,.

(5.10)

An analogous conclusion can be reached for the three-gluon vertex, whose typical lattice observables in Landau gauge have the form
[
243
,
186
,
187
,
246
,
188
,
23
,
247
,
248
,
249
,
250
,
251
]

L_{i}(r,p,q)=\frac{\lambda^{i}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(q)P^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(r)P^{\nu\nu^{\prime}}(p){\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)}{\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(q)P^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(r)P^{\nu\nu^{\prime}}(p)\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)}\,,

(5.11)

where the
\lambda_{\alpha\mu\nu}^{i}(q,r,p)
are suitable projectors that isolate specific form factors
or linear combinations thereof.
Then, the condition of Eq. (
5.4
) is tantamount to
the absence of poles in the lattice functions
L_{i}(r,p,q)
.

The most general form of
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
consistent with the condition that all poles are longitudinally coupled is given by
[
252
]

\displaystyle{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{\alpha}}{q^{2}}\left(g_{\mu\nu}V_{1}+p_{\mu}r_{\nu}V_{2}\right)\,\,+\,\,\frac{r_{\mu}}{r^{2}}\left(g_{\alpha\nu}V_{3}+q_{\nu}p_{\alpha}V_{4}\right)\,\,+\,\,\frac{p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}\left(g_{\alpha\mu}V_{5}+r_{\alpha}q_{\mu}V_{6}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{q_{\alpha}r_{\mu}}{q^{2}r^{2}}(q-r)_{\nu}V_{7}\,\,+\,\,\frac{r_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{r^{2}p^{2}}(r-p)_{\alpha}V_{8}\,+\frac{p_{\nu}q_{\alpha}}{p^{2}q^{2}}(p-q)_{\mu}V_{9}\,\,+\,\,\frac{q_{\alpha}r_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{q^{2}r^{2}p^{2}}\,V_{10}\,,

(5.12)

where
V_{i}\equiv V_{i}(q,r,p)
. Note that Bose symmetry imposes that the
V_{i}
have definite transformation properties under the interchange of momenta [see Eqs. (
6.32
) and (
6.33
)].

The Bose symmetry of the three-gluon vertex guarantees the presence of
Schwinger poles in all three momenta,
q
,
r
, and
p
, of the vertex

{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
.
Out of these three possibilities, the pole directly responsible for the emergence of a gluon mass scale is the one
that carries the external momentum
of the gluon SDE, denoted by
q

in diagrams (
a_{1}
) of Fig.
2.4
.
In fact, due to their longitudinal nature,
the poles in the other channels get annihilated
when contracted by the
internal Landau-gauge propagators
of (
a_{1}
).

The latter observation motivates us
to isolate the part of
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
that contains

only
a
single pole in
q
, and denote it by

V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
.
Thus, we have

{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+\cdots\,,\qquad V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\frac{q_{\alpha}}{q^{2}}C_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,,

(5.13)

where the ellipsis indicate
terms with at least one pole in the

p
or
r
channel, while
C_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is pole-free.

Now, the most general tensor structure of
C_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is given by
[
96
,
97
,
54
]

C_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=g_{\mu\nu}V_{1}(q,r,p)+p_{\mu}r_{\nu}V_{2}(q,r,p)+r_{\mu}r_{\nu}C_{3}(q,r,p)+p_{\mu}p_{\nu}C_{4}(q,r,p)+r_{\mu}p_{\nu}C_{5}(q,r,p)\,,

(5.14)

where the direct comparison between Eqs. (
5.13
) and (
5.12
) allowed us to unambiguously identify the
g_{\mu\nu}
and
p_{\mu}r_{\nu}
form factors as
V_{1}(q,r,p)
and
V_{2}(q,r,p)
, respectively.

Relating the
C_{i}
for
i=3,\,4,\,5
with the
V_{j}
is more subtle. In particular,
the
C_{i}
are comprised by
contributions of order
r^{2}
and/or
p^{2}

contained inside the
V_{i}
, with
i=7\,,9\,,10
; when inserted in Eq. (
5.12
), these terms furnish poles in
q
only. For instance, performing a Taylor expansion of
V_{7}(q,r,p)
around
r=0
,

\frac{V_{7}(q,r,p)}{q^{2}r^{2}}=\frac{V_{7}(q,0,-q)}{q^{2}r^{2}}+\frac{2(q\cdot r)}{q^{2}r^{2}}\left[\frac{\partial V_{7}(q,r,p)}{\partial p^{2}}\right]_{r=0}+\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left[\frac{\partial V_{7}(q,r,p)}{\partial r^{2}}\right]_{r=0}+\ldots\,,

(5.15)

the last term contains a pole only in
q
. Hence, matching tensor structures in Eqs. (
5.13
) and (
5.12
), yields

C_{3}(q,r,p)=-2\left[\frac{\partial V_{7}(q,r,p)}{\partial r^{2}}\right]_{r=0}+\ldots\,,

(5.16)

with ellipsis indicating contributions from
V_{7}(q,r,p)
,
V_{10}(q,r,p)
, and higher derivatives of
V_{7}(q,r,p)
.

When
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is contracted
by two internal propagators
in the Landau gauge
[as happens in graph (
a_{1}
) of Fig.
2.4
],
it suffices to consider

P^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}(r)P^{\nu^{\prime}}_{\nu}(p){\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=P^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}(r)P^{\nu^{\prime}}_{\nu}(p)V_{\alpha\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)\,.

(5.17)

In this case, only the form factors
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are relevant, since

\displaystyle P^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}(r)P^{\nu^{\prime}}_{\nu}(p)C_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,P^{\rho}_{\mu}(r)P_{\nu\rho}(p)V_{1}(q,r,p)+q_{\mu^{\prime}}q_{\nu^{\prime}}P^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}(r)P^{\nu^{\prime}}_{\nu}(p)V_{2}(q,r,p)\,,

(5.18)

where momentum conservation was used in passing from Eq. (
5.14
) to Eq. (
5.18
).

Note that, from the Bose symmetry of the full vertex,

V_{1}(q,r,p)=-V_{1}(q,p,r)\,,\Longrightarrow V_{1}(0,r,-r)=0\,;

(5.19)

the same result follows straightforwardly from the STI of Eq. (
2.33
), as we show in Subsec.
6.2
. Hence, performing a Taylor expansion around
q=0
,

V_{1}(q,r,p)=2(q\cdot r){\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})\,,\qquad{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2}):=\left[\frac{\partial V_{1}(q,r,p)}{\partial p^{2}}\right]_{q=0}\,.

(5.20)

A result analogous to Eq. (
5.19
) can be shown for the pole term,
C(r,p,q)
, of the ghost-gluon vertex (see App.
E
), namely

C(r,-r,0)=0\,,

(5.21)

which implies

C(r,p,q)=2(q\cdot r){\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})\,,\qquad{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2}):=\left[\frac{\partial C(r,p,q)}{\partial p^{2}}\right]_{q=0}\,,

(5.22)

for small
q
.

It is important to remark that, owing to Eqs. (
5.19
) and (
5.21
), the terms
V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
and
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
act as poles only in configurations where
q^{2}=0
and
q\neq 0
, corresponding to the momentum of an on-shell massless particle (the massless bound state). Instead, in the configurations where the momentum
q
itself goes to zero, the
V
s are irregular, in the sense that they do not have unique limits, but do not act as poles.

To illustrate this point, we consider the ghost-gluon pole term,
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
, and take the limit
q\to 0
through a path where
q^{2}\neq 0
. This can be achieved simply by choosing
q
and
r
to be spacelike Euclidean momenta, such that
q^{2}>0
and
r^{2}>0
. In this case, we can write
q=|q|\hat{q}
, where
|q|
and
\hat{q}
are magnitude of
q
and its unit vector, respectively, and similarly
r=|r|\hat{r}
. For concreteness, and without loss of generality, we may assume
\hat{q}=(1,0,0,0)
and
\hat{r}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0,0)
, where
\theta
is the angle between
q
and
r
. Then, the limit
q\to 0
corresponds to taking the absolute value,
|q|
, to zero.

Now, combining Eqs. (
5.10
) and (
5.22
), we obtain for
q\to 0
that

\lim_{q\to 0}V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=\lim_{q\to 0}2q_{\alpha}\frac{(q\cdot r)}{q^{2}}{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})=2|r|\hat{q}_{\alpha}\cos\theta\,{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})\,,

(5.23)

which does not act as a pole. In fact, the r.h.s. of Eq. (
5.23
) is strictly finite. Nevertheless,
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
is irregular; its value at vanishing
q
is not unique, but instead depends on
\hat{q}
and
\theta
, which specify the path through which the
q\to 0
limit is approached. As we will see in Subsec.
5.5
, this irregularity is enough to generate a gluon mass scale (see also the discussions in
[
162
,
141
]
). In fact, as we show there, the resulting gluon mass scale does
not
depend on the way that the limit
q\to 0
is implemented.

Note that the fact that the
V
s have no poles in
q=0
may be viewed as a consequence of the central assumption that the gluon mass generation leaves the gauge symmetry intact. Indeed, as shown in Subsec.
6.2
and App.
E
, the key conditions described by Eqs. (
5.19
) and (
5.21
) originate from the STIs of the theory. A related mechanism, but with the global color symmetry broken, has been discussed in
[
92
]
, and leads to stronger vertex irregularities, with the corresponding
V
s acting as poles in
q=0
.

There is an additional observation related to the momentum-dependent residues of Eqs. (
5.20
) and (
5.22
),
to be contrasted to the constant residue
g^{2}v^{2}/4

known from the Higgs mechanism, see
Eq. (
4.3
).
In particular, the latter
leads to a constant mass
M
for the corresponding gauge boson,

M^{2}=g^{2}v^{2}/4
, which, in turn,
makes the propagator display an “on-shell” pole at
q^{2}=-M^{2}
,
which would be clearly an undesirable feature
for the gluon. Fortunately, the momentum-dependence of the residues
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
and
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})

opens up the possibility of
having, instead of a constant mass, a
running
mass,

m^{2}(q^{2})

[
31
,
253
,
56
]
. Then, depending on the exact functional form of
m^{2}(q^{2})
,
the condition
q^{2}=-m^{2}(q^{2})
may be
impossible to satisfy for any real value of
q^{2}
; in App.
F
we discuss a particular realization of this general idea.

The
residue functions

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
and
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
are of central importance in the implementation of the Schwinger mechanism in QCD,
mainly due to the following three reasons:

(
a)
Up to numerical constants,
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
and
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
correspond to
the
BS amplitudes
that
control the formation of gluon–gluon
and ghost-antighost
colored
composite bound states, respectively; the details of their momentum dependence are determined by a set of coupled BS equations.

(
b)
As we will show in the next subsection, the gluon mass is determined by certain integrals that involve the functions
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
and
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
.

(
c)

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
leads to the
smoking-gun
displacements of the WIs; this
characteristic effect
has been confirmed
at a high level
of statistical significance,
through the appropriate combination of results obtained from several
lattice simulations
[
144
]
.

Note that
the function
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
will be
omitted from the numerical analysis,
because its effects are known to be
clearly subleading in comparison to those of
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

[
140
]
;
thus the aforementioned system gets reduced to a single integral equation describing
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
. Even so,

{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
will appear in various
intermediate demonstrations, because the simple tensorial structure of the ghost-gluon vertex facilitates the
illustration of certain conceptual points; for a
related study, see also
[
141
]
.

To conclude this initial discussion of the Schwinger poles, the following comments regarding the decomposition in Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) are in order.

(
i)
A
splitting analogous to Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.2
)
holds for the BFM vertices

\widetilde{{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(q,r,p)
,
with the corresponding
components
denoted by
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
,
\widetilde{{\cal V}}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
, and
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
.

(
ii)
The pole-free components
\Gamma

capture the full perturbative
structure of the corresponding vertices, while
the terms
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}
and
{V}_{\alpha}
are purely nonperturbative.

(
iii)

In general, the pole-free components
are not regular functions, even after a gluon mass scale
has been generated. Indeed, while some of the logarithms emerging from the evaluation of diagrams are “protected” by the presence of the gluon mass,
i.e.
, through the transition

\ln{q^{2}}\to\ln{(q^{2}+m^{2})}
,
others, originating from ghost loops, remain “unprotected”,

i.e.
, are of the type
\ln q^{2}

[
254
]
.

(
iv)
Note that, although only the term
V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
of the pole vertex
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
contributes to the gluon mass generation, all poles are required for enforcing the
STI of Eq. (
2.33
)
(and its permutations)
in the presence of
infrared finite gluon propagators (see Subsec.
6.4
).

(
v)

The vertex splitting of
Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.2
)
is akin to the act of singling out the pole of a complex
function
f(z)
, by setting

f(z)=\frac{g(z)}{z}+h(z)\,,

(5.24)

where
g(0)\neq 0
is the residue of the pole. Note that the above way of expressing
the function
f(z)

becomes
mathematically unique
only at
z=0
; for any
other value of
z
, pieces may be
moved around from
g(z)
to
h(z)

and vice versa. The same is true
with the separation of
Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), which becomes
unambiguous as the relevant momenta approach
zero.

5.3  Gluon mass scale from the residues of the Schwinger poles

In this subsection, we show how the presence of longitudinally coupled massless poles in the vertices leads to the generation of a gluon mass,
m^{2}
, and derive the relation between
m^{2}
and the functions
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
and
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
.

It is clear from Eq. (
2.21
) that the gluon mass scale,
m^{2}=-\Delta^{-1}(0)
(Minkowski space), is given by

m^{2}=-i\,\Pi(0)\,.

(5.25)

Then, since the self-energy is transverse,
\Pi_{\mu\nu}(q)=P_{\mu\nu}(q)\Pi(q)
, we can compute
\Pi(0)
in any one of the following two ways, which ought to yield the same result: (
i
) by computing the form factor of the
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}/q^{2}
component of
\Pi_{\mu\nu}(q)
and then taking the
q\to 0
limit; (
ii
) by determining the
g_{\mu\nu}
form factor of
\Pi_{\mu\nu}(q)
.
In this subsection we will present the relatively
straightforward
derivation through (
i
), while the conceptually more subtle derivation of
(
ii
) will be postponed for Subsec.
5.5
.

What is quite striking about these two derivations
is that the type of concepts invoked for arriving to the final common answer are completely different. One may appreciate
the subtlety already at this level:
given that the vertex poles are longitudinally
coupled, thus contributing to
the
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}/q^{2}
part of
\Pi_{\mu\nu}(q)
alone, it is not obvious
what gives rise to the

g_{\mu\nu}
component of the gluon mass.

It turns out that
it is physically far more transparent to carry out the calculation
in the context of the BFM Landau gauge, where
the vertices satisfy Abelian STIs, and the block-wise transversality allows the systematic treatment of
specific subsets of graphs.
At the end of the derivation,
the final answer will be easily converted
into the language of
the standard Landau gauge, with the aid of the appropriate BQIs.

The part of any self-energy diagram

({\tilde{a}}_{i})_{\mu\nu}
that contains the vertex

V
will be denoted by
({\tilde{a}}_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}})_{\mu\nu}
.
Evidently, since
the index
\nu
is saturated by the longitudinal pole,
proportional to
q_{\nu}
,
all such terms will be of the form

({\tilde{a}}_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}})_{\mu\nu}=\frac{q_{\mu}q_{\nu}}{q^{2}}{\tilde{a}}_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(q^{2})\,,\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{\tilde{a}}_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(q^{2})=\frac{q^{\mu}q^{\nu}}{q^{2}}({\tilde{a}}_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}})_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.26)

and we will be
determining

{\tilde{a}}_{i}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(0)
.

We consider first the
{\widetilde{\Pi}}_{\mu\nu}^{(1)}(q)
.
Since we are interested in the

q_{\mu}q_{\nu}/q^{2}

components, it is clear that graph
({\tilde{a}}_{2})_{\mu\nu}

gives no contribution.
Turning to

({\tilde{a}}_{1})_{\mu\nu}
,
and using the BFM analogues of Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.18
) into Eq. (
2.68
),
and finally employing Eq. (
5.26
),
we find

{\tilde{a}}_{1}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{2}g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{q^{\mu}}{q^{2}}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(q,k,-t)\Delta(k^{2})\Delta(t^{2})\left[P^{\alpha\rho}(k)P^{\beta}_{\rho}(t){\widetilde{V}}_{1}(q,k,-t)-q_{\rho}q_{\sigma}P^{\alpha\rho}(k)P^{\beta\sigma}(t){\widetilde{V}}_{2}(q,k,-t)\right]\,.

(5.27)

Next, in order to obtain the
expression for

{\tilde{a}}_{1}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(0)
,
we perform a Taylor expansion of Eq. (
5.27
) around
q=0
. In doing so, we note that the
{\widetilde{V}}_{2}
term in Eq. (
5.27
) is two orders higher in
q
than
{\widetilde{V}}_{1}
, and hence does not contribute. Then, using the BFM versions of Eqs. (
5.19
) and (
5.20
),
together with Eq. (
3.35
),
we find

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{1}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(0)=

\displaystyle\,g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{q^{\mu}}{q^{2}}\int_{k}(q\cdot k)\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)P^{\alpha\beta}(k)(t)\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2(d-1)g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{q^{\mu}q^{\rho}}{q^{2}}\int_{k}k_{\mu}k_{\rho}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})\,.

(5.28)

By Lorentz invariance, the integral in the last line must be proportional to
g_{\mu\rho}
,
and therefore

{\tilde{a}}_{1}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(0)=\frac{2(d-1)g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})\,.

(5.29)

So, finally we obtain

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}(0)=-\frac{2(d-1)g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})\,.

(5.30)

where the additional
minus sign comes
from the fact that

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}(0)
is multiplied by

-q_{\mu}q_{\mu}/q^{2}
.

A completely analogous procedure can then be employed to determine the contribution from the ghost loops,
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
, of Fig.
2.5
.
It is clear from
Eq. (
2.73
)
that the seagull diagram
({\tilde{a}}_{4})_{\mu\nu}
does not contribute to the

q_{\mu}q_{\nu}
component;
we therefore consider only diagram
({\tilde{a}}_{3})_{\mu\nu}
.
Using the BFM equivalents of Eqs. (
5.2
) and (
5.10
),
together with
Eq. (
5.26
), we get

{\tilde{a}}_{3}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(q^{2})=-g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{q^{\mu}}{q^{2}}\int_{k}t_{\mu}D(t^{2})D(k^{2}){\widetilde{C}}(-k,t,-q)\,.

(5.31)

Then, an expansion around
q=0
, using the BFM form of Eq. (
5.22
), entails

{\tilde{a}}_{3}^{{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(q^{2})=-2g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{q^{\nu}q^{\rho}}{q^{2}}\int_{k}k_{\mu}k_{\rho}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})=-\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\,,

(5.32)

such that

\widetilde{\Pi}^{(2)}(0)=\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\,.

(5.33)

In principle, the two-loop gluonic block,
\widetilde{\Pi}^{(3)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
, can also contribute to the gluon mass. However, in the absence of a pole in the four-gluon vertex, the only contribution,
(a_{5})_{\mu\nu}
, cancels in the process of renormalization that we present in later sections; hence, the term
\widetilde{\Pi}^{(3)}_{\mu\nu}(q)

will be neglected.

At this point, we can use
the BQI of Eq. (
2.61
), whose expression in terms of
\Pi(0)
reads

\Pi(0)=F(0)\widetilde{\Pi}(0)\,,

(5.34)

where we used the Landau-gauge relation of Eq. (
2.65
). Then, combining Eqs. (
5.30
), (
5.33
) and (
5.34
),

\Pi(0)=-\frac{2(d-1)g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})-\frac{1}{d-1}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\right]\,,

(5.35)

which, through Eq. (
5.25
), implies for the gluon mass

m^{2}=\frac{2(d-1)g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}iF(0)\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})-\frac{1}{d-1}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\right]\,.

(5.36)

Now, Eq. (
5.36
) still contains the BFM amplitudes
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})
and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})
. In order to express
m^{2}
exclusively in terms of quantum Green functions, we invoke the additional BQIs
[
99
]

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})=F(0)\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})\,,\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})=F(0){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})\,,

(5.37)

derived in App.
E
. Then, Eq. (
5.36
) is recast as

m^{2}=\frac{3}{2}ig^{2}C_{\rm A}\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2}){\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})-\frac{1}{3}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\mathcal{C}}(k^{2})\right]\,,

(5.38)

where we have finally specialized to the case
d=4
.

At this point we convert our results to Euclidean space,
employing the rules given in App.
A
.
Then, using the notation of Eqs. (
A.5
) and (
A.6
), and noting that
m^{2}\to m^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
, Eq. (
5.38
) becomes

m^{2}=-\frac{3\alpha_{s}C_{\rm A}}{8\pi}\left[\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!dy\,{\cal Z}^{2}(y){\mathbb{C}}(y)-\frac{1}{3}\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!dy\,F^{2}(y){\mathcal{C}}(y)\right]\,.

(5.39)

To conclude this analysis, let us note that the ghost contribution in Eq. (
5.39
) has been shown to be suppressed in comparison to the gluon
[
140
,
142
]
. Then, dropping
the second term in Eq. (
5.39
), we are left with

m^{2}=-\frac{3\alpha_{s}C_{\rm A}}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!dy\,{\cal Z}^{2}(y){\mathbb{C}}(y)\,.

(5.40)

The following comments are now in order.

(
{\it i}
)
It is clear from the above equation that, in order for
m^{2}
to
be positive,

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
must
be “sufficiently” negative
within the relevant region of
momentum.
Quite crucially,
the
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

in the lattice-based analysis presented in Subsec.
6.3

is negative-definite within the entire range of momentum,

i.e.
,
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})<0
for all values of
r^{2}
.
Moreover, as we will show in Subsec.
8.3
,
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
is guaranteed to be negative in the bound state realization of the Schwinger mechanism.
One may therefore recast
Eq. (
5.40
) in the
manifestly positive form

m^{2}=\frac{3\alpha_{s}C_{\rm A}}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!dy\,{\cal Z}^{2}(y)\lvert{\mathbb{C}}(y)\rvert\,.

(5.41)

(
{\it ii}
) In order for the result of the
integration in Eq. (
5.41
) to be
finite, the function
{\mathbb{C}}(y)

must drop in the UV faster than a
certain rate. In particular,
if we use the
anomalous dimension for

{\cal Z}(y)
, namely
[
255
,
106
,
177
,
256
,
228
,
116
]

{\cal Z}(y)\sim L_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle UV}}}^{-13/22}(y)\,,\qquad L_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle UV}}}(y):=c\ln(y/\Lambda^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle MOM}}})\,,

(5.42)

where
c=1/\ln(\mu^{2}/\Lambda^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle MOM}}})
, and
\Lambda_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle MOM}}}
is the (quenched) QCD mass-scale in the MOM scheme
[
257
,
181
,
258
,
259
]
, it follows that
{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})
must drop faster than
\ln^{2/11}(k^{2})/k^{2}
.
As we will see in Subsec.
8.6
,
the nonperturbative renormalization
of Eq. (
5.41
)
imposes a slightly more stringent condition on the
asymptotic behavior
of
{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})
, namely that it must
drop faster than
1/k^{2}\ln^{9/44}(k^{2})
;
this condition is
indeed fulfilled
by the
{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})
obtained
from the BSE that
controls amplitude
for Schwinger
pole formation.

5.4  Ward identity displacement: the key idea

In this subsection we focus on another important point of this approach,
namely
the displacement
that the Schwinger mechanism causes to the WIs satisfied by the pole-free parts of the three-gluon and ghost-gluon vertices
[
54
]
.

To fix the ideas in terms of an elementary example, we consider the BFM ghost-gluon vertex,
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
,
which has a simple tensorial
structure and satisfies the Abelian STI of Eq. (
2.59
).

Let us begin by reviewing the derivation of the standard WI in the absence of the Schwinger mechanism,
i.e.
, when

\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)

does not contain poles. In that case,
evidently
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(q,r,p)
, and
expanding Eq. (
2.59
) in a Taylor series around
q=0
, one obtains that, to order
q
, each side of that equation is given by

[{\rm l.h.s}]=q^{\alpha}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,-r,0)\,,\qquad\qquad[{\rm r.h.s}]=q^{\alpha}\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{\alpha}}\,.

(5.43)

Then, equating the coefficients of the first-order terms yields a WI similar to that of scalar QED in Eq. (
3.25
), namely

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,-r,0)=\,\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{\alpha}}=2r_{\alpha}\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}=-2r_{\alpha}\frac{\partial{D}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}{D}^{-2}(r^{2})\,.

(5.44)

The WI of Eq. (
5.44
) may also be expressed in terms of scalar form factors. For the case of
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,-r,0)
, which is described by a single form factor, namely

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,-r,0)=\widetilde{\cal A}(r^{2})r_{\alpha}\,,

(5.45)

Eq. (
5.44
) implies

\widetilde{\cal A}(r^{2})=2\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}\,.

(5.46)

Let us now turn on the Schwinger mechanism, and determine the effect of the corresponding pole
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
on the WI. To that end, we consider the full vertex,
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
, which, analogously to Eqs. (
5.2
) and (
5.10
), has the form

\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)+\frac{q_{\alpha}}{q^{2}}{\widetilde{C}}(r,p,q)\,.

(5.47)

Let us now assume that the Schwinger mechanism has become operational.
Then, the STIs satisfied by the fundamental vertices retain their standard
form, but are now resolved through the nontrivial participation of the massless poles

[
92
,
138
,
93
,
31
,
260
,
47
,
49
,
54
,
142
,
144
,
252
,
168
]
.
In particular,

\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
satisfies, as before, precisely Eq. (
2.59
), namely

\displaystyle q^{\alpha}\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\alpha}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)+\widetilde{C}(r,p,q)

(5.48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{D}^{-1}(p^{2})-{D}^{-1}(r^{2})\,.

Crucially, the contraction of
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
by
q^{\alpha}

cancels the massless pole in
q^{2}
, yielding a completely pole-free result.
Therefore, the WI obeyed by
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
may be derived as above, namely
by carrying out a Taylor expansion around
q=0
, and keeping terms at most linear in
q
. Following this procedure, we obtain

q^{\alpha}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,-r,0)=-\widetilde{C}(r,-r,0)+q^{\alpha}\left\{\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{\alpha}}-\left[\frac{\partial\widetilde{C}(r,p,q)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}\right\}\,.

(5.49)

It is clear at this point that the only zeroth-order contribution present in Eq. (
5.49
),
namely
\widetilde{C}(r,-r,0)
, must vanish,

\widetilde{C}(r,-r,0)=0\,.

(5.50)

Note, in fact, that this last property is a direct consequence of the antisymmetry of
\widetilde{C}(r,p,q)
under
r\leftrightarrow p
, namely

\widetilde{C}(r,p,q)=-\widetilde{C}(p,r,q)
, which is imposed by the general ghost-antighost symmetry of the vertex

B(q){\bar{c}}(r)c(p)
, where the
B(q)
denotes the background gluon field, introduced in subsection
2.4
.

Setting now

\left[\frac{\partial\widetilde{C}(r,p,q)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}\!\!\!\!\!=2r_{\alpha}\,{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})\,,\qquad{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2}):=\left[\frac{\partial\widetilde{C}(r,p,q)}{\partial p^{2}}\right]_{q=0}\,,

(5.51)

and implementing the matching of the terms linear in
q
, we arrive at the WI

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,-r,0)=2r_{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}-{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})\right]\,.

(5.52)

Evidently, the WI in Eq. (
5.52
) is
displaced
with respect to that of Eq. (
5.44
), by an amount proportional to the residue function
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})
;
given its new role, we will use for
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})
the equivalent name

displacement function
.

In complete analogy, the displaced version of Eq. (
5.46
) is given by

\widetilde{\cal A}(r^{2})=2\left[\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}-\,{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})\right]\,.

(5.53)

Similarly,
the Schwinger poles in
the BFM three-gluon vertex,
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, lead to
the displacement of the WI satisfied by the pole-free
component

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, whose
standard
WI has been reported
in Eq. (
3.38
).

To begin with, we use the BFM analogues of Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.14
) into the STI of Eq. (
2.58
) to write

q^{\alpha}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+{\widetilde{C}}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\Delta^{-1}_{\mu\nu}(p)-\Delta^{-1}_{\mu\nu}(r)\,,

(5.54)

where

{\widetilde{C}}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=g_{\mu\nu}{\widetilde{V}}_{1}(q,r,p)+p_{\mu}r_{\nu}{\widetilde{V}}_{2}(q,r,p)\,.

(5.55)

Then, we expand Eq. (
5.54
) in a Taylor series around
q=0
. From the zeroth order coefficients, we obtain

{\widetilde{C}}_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=0\,,

(5.56)

which leads directly to the BFM version of Eq. (
5.19
), and is guaranteed by Bose symmetry. On the other hand, the terms linear in
q
yield the nontrivial relation

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=\frac{\partial\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{-1}(r)}{\partial r^{\alpha}}-\left[\frac{\partial{\widetilde{C}}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}\,.

(5.57)

Clearly, the last term
represents the displacement of the naive WI of Eq. (
3.38
).
Then,
using Eq. (
5.22
),
the displacement function is given by

\left[\frac{\partial{\widetilde{C}}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}=2r_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})-2r_{\alpha}r_{\mu}r_{\nu}\left[\frac{\partial{\widetilde{V}}_{2}(q,r,p)}{\partial p^{2}}\right]_{q=0}\,.

(5.58)

From Eq. (
5.57
) one may derive
relations analogous to Eq. (
5.53
),
expressing the corresponding displacement
of the form factors comprising

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)
,
namely

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=2{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{1}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}+{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{2}(r^{2})(r_{\mu}g_{\nu\alpha}+r_{\nu}g_{\mu\alpha})+{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{3}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}r_{\mu}r_{\nu}\,.

(5.59)

The most relevant relation is the one
expressing the displacement of

{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{1}(r^{2})
.
In particular,
equating the
r_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}
coefficients on both sides of Eq. (
5.57
), we find

{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{1}(r^{2})=\frac{\partial\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}-{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}}(r^{2})\,.

(5.60)

The simple form of Eq. (
5.60
)
is particularly appropriate for illustrating
a conceptual point of major importance
for what follows. Let us assume that
both
{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{1}(r^{2})
and

\Delta(r^{2})
may be simulated on the lattice;
this is indeed possible for
\Delta(r^{2})
,
but, at present, not for
{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{1}(r^{2})
, being the form factor of a BFM vertex
[
261
,
262
]
.
Then, if the lattice results for
{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{1}(r^{2})
and

\Delta(r^{2})
were to be combined to
form Eq. (
5.60
),
crucial information on the
form of the function
{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}}(r^{2})

would emerge.
Within such a scenario, one could,
at least in principle,
confirm or rule out the action of the Schwinger mechanism, depending on the
outcome; for instance, in the limit
of vanishing error bars, if
{\widetilde{\cal A}}_{1}(r^{2})
and

\Delta(r^{2})
happened to satisfy precisely
Eq. (
5.60
), without displacement
[
i.e.
, for
{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}}(r^{2})=0
)],
the mechanism would be excluded.
This key observation will be explored in detail in Subsec.
6.3
, after the analogue of Eq. (
5.60
) has been derived for the
standard three-gluon vertex,
\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, which, in contradistinction to
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, has been
indeed simulated on the lattice.

5.5  Self-consistency, subtleties, and evasion of the seagull cancellation

The derivation of the
gluon mass formula in Eq. (
5.38
)
proceeded by considering
the
q^{\mu}q^{\nu}
component of the gluon propagator, since the Schwinger poles of the vertices contribute only to this particular tensorial structure.
To be sure, the transversality of the self-energy, encoded into Eq. (
2.32
), clearly affirms that the
g_{\mu\nu}

component must yield precisely the same answer. Nonetheless, the
explicit demonstration of this fact is
especially subtle
[
46
,
118
]
, hinging crucially on the notion of the WI displacement developed in the previous subsection.

As was done in the
case of the

q_{\mu}q_{\nu}
component in Subsec.
5.3
,
we will consider the blocks
of diagrams comprising

\widetilde{\Pi}_{1}^{\mu\nu}(q)
and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{\mu\nu}(q)
, given in Fig.
2.5
.
Evidently, the
block-wise transversality property of Eq. (
2.67
)
requires that their

g^{\mu\nu}
components
that survive in the limit

q\to 0

must coincide with
the results of
Eqs. (
5.30
) and (
5.33
),
respectively.

We start with the diagrams

({\tilde{a}}_{1})_{\mu\nu}
and

({\tilde{a}}_{2})_{\mu\nu}
;
after setting in them
q=0
and isolating the
g_{\mu\nu}
component, we recover precisely Eq. (
3.36
).
However, the key difference
is that now

{\widetilde{\Gamma}}^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}(0,k,-k)

is not given by the WI of
Eq. (
3.38
), but rather
by the displaced WI of
Eq. (
5.57
).
Thus, in this case we obtain

{\tilde{a}}_{1}=-\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\frac{\partial\Delta^{\alpha\beta}(k)}{\partial k_{\mu}}-\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\Delta^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta^{\beta\sigma}(k)\left[\frac{\partial{\widetilde{C}}_{\rho\sigma}(q,k,-q-k)}{\partial q_{\mu}}\right]_{q=0}\,.

(5.61)

Now, the first term in Eq. (
5.61
) is none other than Eq. (
3.41
), which we showed in Subsec.
3.6
to cancel exactly against
{\tilde{a}}_{2}
due to the seagull identity.
This cancellation proceeds exactly as before, and
we are thus left
with the second term,
i.e.
,

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}(0)={\tilde{a}}_{1}+{\tilde{a}}_{2}=-\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\Delta^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta^{\beta\sigma}(k)\left[\frac{\partial{\widetilde{C}}_{\rho\sigma}(q,k,-q-k)}{\partial q_{\mu}}\right]_{q=0}\,.

(5.62)

At this point we specialize to the Landau gauge, in which case the second term of Eq. (
5.58
)
gets annihilated
when inserted into
Eq. (
5.62
).
Then,
using Eq. (
3.35
), we obtain

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}(0)=-\frac{2(d-1)g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})\,,

(5.63)

which is exactly the same result obtained from the
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}
component of
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
,
i.e.
, Eq. (
5.30
).

The contribution of the ghost loops,
\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{\mu\nu}(q)
of Fig.
2.5
, can be computed through the same procedure. Setting
q=0
in the
({\tilde{a}}_{3})_{\mu\nu}
of Eq. (
2.73
), and isolating its
g_{\mu\nu}
form factor, leads us again to Eq. (
3.46
). Then, invoking therein the displaced WI of Eq. (
5.52
) (with
r\to-k)
, entails

{\tilde{a}}_{3}=\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}\frac{\partial D(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\,.

(5.64)

The first term in Eq. (
5.64
) is exactly the same as the r.h.s. of Eq. (
3.48
), which has been shown in Subsec.
3.6
to cancel against
{\tilde{a}}_{4}
, by virtue of the seagull identity. Hence,
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(0)
is equal to the second term in Eq. (
5.64
),
i.e.
,

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(0)={\tilde{a}}_{3}+{\tilde{a}}_{4}=\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\,,

(5.65)

which coincides precisely with Eq. (
5.33
), derived from the
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}
component of
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
.

We next turn to a natural question, which is often raised in connection with the longitudinal nature of the Schwinger poles.
In particular, the fact that the gluon self-energy is transverse allows one to freely contract by the projector
P_{\mu\nu}(q)
,
which would automatically annihilate the relevant
Schwinger poles from all vertices. The question then is:
in the absence of poles,
where does the gluon mass
come from?
The answer to this question is precisely the displacement of the WI.

We will now illustrate this point at the level
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
,
given by the sum of
({\tilde{a}}_{3})_{\mu\nu}
and
({\tilde{a}}_{4})_{\mu\nu}
in Eq. (
2.73
). Given
the transversality of
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
, we may set

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)={\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\alpha}(q)P^{\alpha}_{\nu}(q)
, and so
(
t=k+q
)

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)=g^{2}C_{\rm A}P^{\alpha}_{\nu}(q)\int_{k}t_{\mu}D(t^{2})D(k^{2})\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(-k,t,-q)+g^{2}C_{\rm A}\,P_{\mu\nu}(q)\int_{k}D(k^{2})\,.

(5.66)

Then, since
P^{\alpha}_{\nu}(q)\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(-k,t,-q)=P^{\alpha}_{\nu}(q){\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\alpha}(-k,t,-q)
,
we have

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)=g^{2}C_{\rm A}P^{\alpha}_{\nu}(q)\int_{k}t_{\mu}D(t^{2})D(k^{2}){\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\alpha}(-k,t,-q)+g^{2}C_{\rm A}\,P_{\mu\nu}(q)\int_{k}D(k^{2})\,.

(5.67)

At this point we can
isolate the
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}

component of both sides,
which now receives a contribution
proportional to
\int_{k}D(k^{2})
,
originating from graph
({\tilde{a}}_{4})_{\mu\nu}
,
namely

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(q^{2})=g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{q^{\mu}q^{\alpha}}{q^{2}}\int_{k}k_{\mu}D(t^{2})D(k^{2}){\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\alpha}(-k,t,-q)+g^{2}C_{\rm A}\,\int_{k}D(k^{2})\,.

(5.68)

The first integral on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
5.68
), after
setting
q=0

and using Eq. (
5.51
) (with
r\to-k
), becomes

\int_{k}k_{\mu}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\alpha}(-k,k,0)=\frac{g_{\mu\alpha}}{d}\int_{k}k^{\sigma}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\sigma}(-k,k,0)=\frac{2g_{\mu\alpha}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}\left[\frac{\partial{D}(k^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}+D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\right],

(5.69)

and so, substituting into
Eq. (
5.68
) we get

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(0)=\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\underbrace{\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\frac{\partial D(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{d}{2}\int_{k}D(k^{2})\right]}_{\rm seagull\,\,identity}+\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\,.

(5.70)

Therefore,

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(0)=\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\,,

(5.71)

which is exactly the result of Eq. (
5.33
).

Lastly, we consider the path dependence of the vertex irregularity, and show that the saturation value of
{\widetilde{\Pi}}(0)
is independent of the path through which the limit
q=0
is approached. For simplicity, we make this demonstration only for
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
.

Combining Eqs. (
5.23
) and (
5.52
), we see that the full vertex,
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(q,r,p)
, has the path dependent
q\to 0
limit

\lim_{q\to 0}\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=2r_{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial{D}^{-1}(r^{2})}{\partial r^{2}}-{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})\right]+2|r|\hat{q}_{\alpha}\cos\theta\,{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})\,,

(5.72)

where we use the momentum parametrization introduced above Eq. (
5.23
). Note that the term proportional to
r_{\alpha}
originates from the pole-free part,
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
, carrying a
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})
term only because of the displacement of the WI, whereas the direct contribution of
{\widetilde{V}}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
is proportional to
\hat{q}_{\alpha}
.

Now, in the
q\to 0
limit, the transverse projector becomes,

\lim_{q\to 0}P_{\mu\nu}(q)=g_{\mu\nu}-\hat{q}_{\mu}\hat{q}_{\nu}\,,

(5.73)

which is similarly path-dependent. Nevertheless,
P^{\alpha}_{\nu}(q)\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)=P^{\alpha}_{\nu}(q){\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
, still holds. That is, regardless of the path through which
q\to 0
, the residue function
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})
enters in the transversely projected vertex only through the displacement of the WI, and not by a direct contribution of
V_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
.

Then, inserting Eqs. (
5.72
) and (
5.73
) into Eq. (
5.66
), we find

\lim_{q\to 0}{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)=-2g^{2}C_{\rm A}\left(g^{\alpha}_{\nu}-\hat{q}_{\nu}\hat{q}^{\alpha}\right)\int_{k}k_{\alpha}k_{\mu}D^{2}(k^{2})\left[\frac{\partial D^{-1}(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}-{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\right]+g^{2}C_{\rm A}\,\left(g_{\mu\nu}-\hat{q}_{\mu}\hat{q}_{\nu}\right)\int_{k}D(k^{2})\,.

(5.74)

By Lorentz invariance, the integral in the first term can only be proportional to
g_{\alpha\mu}
, and we obtain straightforwardly

\lim_{q\to 0}{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)=\left\{\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\underbrace{\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\frac{\partial D(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{d}{2}\int_{k}D(k^{2})\right]}_{\rm seagull\,\,identity}+\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\right\}\left(g_{\mu\nu}-\hat{q}_{\mu}\hat{q}_{\nu}\right)\,.

(5.75)

Finally, invoking the seagull identity, together with
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)={\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(q)P_{\mu\nu}(q)
,

\lim_{q\to 0}{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)={\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(0)\left(g_{\mu\nu}-\hat{q}_{\mu}\hat{q}_{\nu}\right)=\left[\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{d}\int_{k}k^{2}D^{2}(k^{2}){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(k^{2})\right]\left(g_{\mu\nu}-\hat{q}_{\mu}\hat{q}_{\nu}\right)\,,

(5.76)

i.e.
, the only path dependence in
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}(q)
in the
q\to 0
limit is that of the transverse projector itself, whereas the limit of the scalar
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(2)}(0)
is uniquely and unambiguously given by Eq. (
5.33
).

6  Schwinger mechanism and the three-gluon vertex

In this section we study in detail the implications of the Schwinger poles on the structure of the three-gluon vertex.
In particular, we discuss in detail the displacement of the STI satisfied by the
three-gluon vertex,
and show how to determine the form of the displacement function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
using ingredients obtained from lattice QCD. Moreover, we show that
the use of an infrared finite gluon propagator in the
STI of the three-gluon vertex
imposes an extended pole structure for that vertex.

6.1  Qualitative overview

The three-gluon vertex
is of central importance to Yang-Mills theories,
attesting their
non-Abelian character through the
gauge boson self-interaction that it describes
[
2
,
175
,
176
]
. In fact, the most
celebrated perturbative feature
of these theories, namely
asymptotic freedom
[
3
,
4
]
, is intimately connected to the
action of this vertex. In addition, as has been explained in a series of works, this vertex is crucial to a wide array of non-perturbative phenomena, and in particular the
infrared
patterns associated with the gluon and ghost propagators. Given its significance, some key form factors of the three-gluon vertex have been studied extensively on the lattice, the most preeminent of them being the one associated with the soft-gluon limit, denoted by

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
.
The precise knowledge of
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})

from the lattice offers the
unique opportunity to confirm the action of the
Schwinger mechanism in QCD
in a model-independent way.

In particular, the
Schwinger mechanism induces a displacement to the
WI of the three-gluon vertex,
which affects the expected form of the
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
. Specifically,
in the absence of the
Schwinger mechanism,
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
is fully determined by
the quantities that appear on the r.h.s of Eq. (
2.33
) when the
limit
q\to 0
is taken.
Among these quantities,
the gluon propagator, its derivative, and the saturation value of the ghost dressing function, are known from the lattice. The only quantity not
known from the lattice
is a partial derivative of the ghost-gluon kernel, which can be determined from an appropriate SDE.
However, when the Schwinger mechanism is activated,
the WI-induced relation between

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
and the aforementioned quantities
is distorted by the presence of the nonvanishing
displacement function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
.
Thus, at least in principle, the difference
between the
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})

found on the lattice
and that predicted by the WI
provides the
size and shape of the key function

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
.
The detailed analysis of
[
144
]

reveals a clearly nonvanishing signal for
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
, with
a substantial deviation
from the null hypothesis value,
corresponding to
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})=0
.

Let us finally emphasize that the three-gluon vertex possesses a singularity content that is far richer than the simple pole associated with the function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
.
The additional structures are
mathematically indispensable
when the gluon propagator
entering on the r.h.s. of the STI in Eq. (
2.33
)
is of the massive type.
In particular,
the form factors accompanying mixed poles,

i.e.
, of the type

q_{\alpha}r_{\mu}/q^{2}r^{2}
,
are manifestly nonvanishing
[
252
]
; nonetheless,
in the Landau gauge,
they are completely transparent to the mass generation mechanism.

6.2  Ward identity displacement of the three-gluon vertex

From a conceptual standpoint, the WI displacement of the
BFM vertices

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
presented in
Subsec.
5.4

is of central importance
because it provides a concise way of deriving the SDE expressions for the gluon
mass scale, as shown
in Subsec.
5.5
.
However, it is particularly
important to determine
the corresponding WI displacement
satisfied by the
conventional three-gluon vertex

\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
.
As we will demonstrate, the WI displacement of
\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

is provided precisely by the function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
, which is thus found to
play a dual role: it is
both
the BS amplitude associated with the pole formation
and

the displacement function of the three-gluon vertex, as announced
in items (
a
) and (
c
) of Subsec.
5.2
.
This result is crucial, because it offers
an invaluable opportunity:
one may confirm the action of the Schwinger mechanism by combining appropriately Green functions simulated on the lattice.
In this subsection we will derive
the WI displacement of
{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, while
the lattice-based analysis will
be carried out in the next subsection.

We commence our
analysis with the STI satisfied by the vertex
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, namely Eq. (
2.33
).
A key ingredient of this STI is
the ghost gluon kernel,
appearing in two kinematic combinations,
H_{\sigma\mu}(p,q,r)
and
H_{\sigma\nu}(r,q,p)
. This kernels
contain massless poles
in the
r_{\mu}
and
p_{\nu}
channels, respectively, which,
however, are completely annihilated
by the transverse projectors in Eq. (
6.7
).
In addition, in the Landau gauge
that we employ, one may cast
the ghost-gluon kernel in the
special form
[
97
,
142
]
(see Fig.
6.1
)

H_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r)={\widetilde{Z}}_{1}g_{\nu\mu}+q^{\rho}K_{\nu\mu\rho}(p,q,r)\,;

(6.1)

note that the kernel
K
does not contain poles
in the soft-ghost limit,

q\to 0
, and

{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}
is the renormalization constant of the ghost-gluon vertex.

The
relation in Eq. (
6.1
)
is essentially a version of
Taylor’s theorem
[
102
]
, and can be derived as follows. First, note that the SDE for the ghost-gluon kernel can be expressed compactly as in Fig.
6.1
, which translates to
[
2
]

H_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r)={\widetilde{Z}}_{1}g_{\nu\mu}+\int_{k}t^{\rho}\Delta_{\rho}^{\sigma}(k)D(t^{2})A_{\sigma\nu\mu}(-k,t,p,r)\,,

(6.2)

with
t:=k+q
, and
A_{\sigma\nu\mu}(-k,t,p,r)
a kernel whose exact form is not relevant for the present derivation. Now, in the Landau gauge,
(k+q)^{\rho}\Delta_{\rho}^{\sigma}(k)=q^{\rho}\Delta_{\rho}^{\sigma}(k)
, which leads to Eq. (
6.1
) with the identification

K_{\nu\mu\rho}(p,q,r)=\int_{k}\Delta_{\rho}^{\sigma}(k)D(t^{2})A_{\sigma\nu\mu}(-k,t,p,r)\,.

(6.3)

Figure 6.1:
SDE for the ghost-gluon kernel,
H_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r)
, in compact form.

If we now combine
Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.18
), it is clear that

{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(p)\left[q^{\alpha}{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\right]={P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(p)[q^{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+C_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)]\,,

(6.4)

while from the STI of Eq. (
2.33
) we get

{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(p)\left[q^{\alpha}{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\right]={P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(p)\,F(q^{2})\,{R}_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r)\,,

(6.5)

where

{R}_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r):=\Delta^{-1}(p^{2})H_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r)-\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)\,.

(6.6)

Then, equating the right-hand sides of Eqs. (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) we find

q^{\alpha}\left[{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(p)\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\right]={P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(p)\left[\,F(q^{2})\,{R}_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r)-C_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\right]\,.

(6.7)

The next step is
to carry out the Taylor expansion of
both sides of Eq. (
6.7
) around
q=0
, keeping terms that are at most linear in the momentum
q
.

The computation of the l.h.s. of Eq. (
6.7
) is straightforward, yielding

[{\rm l.h.s}]=q^{\alpha}{\cal T}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}^{\mu\nu}(r)\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)\,,\qquad{\cal T}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}^{\mu\nu}(r):={P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(-r)\,.

(6.8)

Given that
\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)
depends on a single momentum (
r
), its
general tensorial decomposition is given by
1
1

1

The factor of 2 is motivated by the tree-level result
of Eq. (
3.35
), such that
{\cal A}_{1}^{(0)}(r^{2})=1
.

\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=2{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}+{\cal A}_{2}(r^{2})(r_{\mu}g_{\nu\alpha}+r_{\nu}g_{\mu\alpha})+{\cal A}_{3}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}r_{\mu}r_{\nu}\,.

(6.9)

Note that the form factors
{\cal A}_{i}(r^{2})
do not contain poles; however, in general they are not regular functions. In particular,

{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})
diverges logarithmically as
r\to 0
, due to the
“unprotected” logarithms that originate from the massless ghost loops appearing in the diagrammatic expansion of the vertex
[
254
,
186
]
. These mild divergences do not interfere in any way with the arguments that follow.

It is now elementary to derive from Eq. (
6.9
) that

{\cal T}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}^{\mu\nu}(r)\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)={\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})\lambda_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\alpha}(r)\,,\qquad\lambda_{\mu\nu\alpha}(r):=2r_{\alpha}P_{\mu\nu}(r)\,,

(6.10)

and, consequently,
Eq. (
6.8
) becomes

[{\rm l.h.s}]={\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})\,q^{\alpha}\lambda_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\alpha}(r)\,.

(6.11)

The computation of the r.h.s. of Eq. (
6.7
) is considerably more technical; the main
steps involved may be summarized as follows
[
142
]
.

(
i
) The action of the projectors
{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r){P}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(p)
on
C_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
activates
Eq. (
5.18
); consequently,
only the term
V_{1}(q,r,p)g_{\mu\nu}

contributes
to lowest order in
q
.

(
ii
)
From Eq. (
6.6
) follows immediately that
{R}_{\nu\mu}(-r,0,r)=0
; thus,
the vanishing of the zeroth order contribution in Eq. (
6.7
) imposes the condition

V_{1}(0,r,-r)=0\,,

(6.12)

in exact analogy to Eq. (
5.50
).
Quite interestingly, we have arrived once again at the result of Eq. (
5.19
), but through a completely different set of arguments; indeed,
while Eq. (
5.19
) is enforced by the Bose symmetry of the three-gluon vertex, Eq. (
6.12
) is a direct consequence of the STI
satisfied by this vertex.

(
iii
)
Clearly,
the Taylor expansion of
Eq. (
6.7
)
involves the differentiation of the ghost-gluon kernel,
which proceeds
by invoking Eq. (
6.1
);
specifically, to lowest order in
q
,
we encounter the partial derivatives

\left[\frac{\partial H_{\nu\mu}(p,q,r)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}\!\!\!\!\!\!=K_{\nu\mu\alpha}(-r,0,r)\,,\qquad\left[\frac{\partial H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}\!\!\!\!\!\!=K_{\mu\nu\alpha}(r,0,-r)\,.

(6.13)

Employing
the tensorial decomposition
[
180
]
,

K_{\mu\nu\alpha}(r,0,-r)=-\frac{{\cal W}(r^{2})}{r^{2}}g_{\mu\nu}r_{\alpha}+\cdots\,,

(6.14)

where the ellipsis denotes terms that
get annihilated upon contraction with the projector
{\cal T}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}^{\mu\nu}(r)
. Then, combining Eq. (
6.14
) with the elementary relation

{\cal T}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}^{\mu\nu}(r)K_{\nu\mu\alpha}(-r,0,r)=-{\cal T}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}^{\mu\nu}(r)K_{\mu\nu\alpha}(r,0,-r)
, we may finally
express the partial derivatives of Eq. (
6.13
) in terms of the function
{\cal W}(r^{2})
.

Taking the above three items into account, it is straightforward to show that the r.h.s. of Eq. (
6.7
) assumes the form

[{\rm r.h.s}]=q^{\alpha}\lambda_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\alpha}(r)\left[F(0)\left\{\widetilde{Z}_{1}\frac{d\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})}{dr^{2}}+\frac{{\cal W}(r^{2})}{r^{2}}\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})\right\}-{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})\right]\,.

(6.15)

The final step is to
equate the terms linear in
q
that appear in Eqs. (
6.11
) and (
6.15
), and thus to obtain
the WI

{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})=F(0)\left\{\widetilde{Z}_{1}\frac{d\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})}{dr^{2}}+\frac{{\cal W}(r^{2})}{r^{2}}\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})\right\}-{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})\,.

(6.16)

Thus, the inclusion of the term
V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
in the three-gluon vertex
leads ultimately to the displacement of the WI satisfied by the vertex form factor

{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})

by an amount given by the function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
.

6.3  The displacement function from lattice inputs

As stressed right after
Eq. (
5.60
),
the functional form of

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

may be determined
from the “mismatch” between quantities
entering in the WI of a given vertex.
In this subsection we turn to
what may be described
as an independent confirmation of the Schwinger
mechanism in QCD:
a non-vanishing
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
is uncovered
from the Euclidean version of
Eq. (
6.16
),
using inputs obtained almost exclusively from lattice simulations
[
144
]
.
The crucial conceptual advantage
of such a determination
is that the lattice is intrinsically “blind” to
field theoretic constructs such as the Schwinger mechanism; indeed, the lattice Green functions are generated
by means of the model-independent functional averaging over gauge-field configurations.
In that sense,
the emergence of a nontrivial signal for
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

strongly
supports the notion
that the Schwinger mechanism
is indeed operational in the gauge sector of QCD.

In order to employ profitably
Eq. (
6.16
), we must
establish an instrumental
relation between the form factor
{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})
and a special
projection of the three-gluon vertex, which has been studied extensively in a large number of lattice simulations
[
263
,
258
,
264
,
265
,
266
,
243
,
267
,
246
,
268
,
231
,
22
,
188
,
251
]
. In particular,
the lattice quantity
we consider is
the denominated “soft-gluon” form factor,
denoted by
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
,
which corresponds to a
particular case of
Eq. (
5.11
):
we set

\lambda_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\to\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, and then take the limit
q\to 0
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(q)P^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(r)P^{\nu\nu^{\prime}}(p){\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)}{\rule[0.0pt]{0.0pt}{12.80365pt}\;{\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(q)P^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(r)P^{\nu\nu^{\prime}}(p)\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)}}\rule[0.0pt]{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}\Bigg|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\!\!q\to 0\\
p\to-r\end{subarray}}\,.

(6.17)

Evidently,
when the
decomposition of

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)

given by Eq. (
5.1
) is substituted into Eq. (
6.17
),
the term
V_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)
drops out from Eq. (
6.17
) due to the validity of Eq. (
5.4
),
producing
the effective replacement
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)\to\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(q,r,p)
.

Figure 6.2:
Upper panel
: The gluon propagator (left) and the first derivative of its inverse (right).

Lower panel
: The ghost dressing function (left) and the soft gluon form factor
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
of the three-gluon vertex (right).
All items are taken from
[
23
]
, and have been cured from volume and discretization artifacts.
Note that
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
is markedly below unity in the infrared, displaying the characteristic zero crossing and the attendant logarithmic
divergence at the origin
[
254
,
186
,
187
,
269
,
197
,
270
,
251
]
.

Then, the numerator,
{\cal N}
, and denominator,
{\cal D}
,
of the fraction on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
6.17
), after employing
Eq. (
6.9
), become

{\cal N}=4(d-1)[r^{2}-(r\cdot q)^{2}/q^{2}]{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})\,,\qquad{\cal D}=4(d-1)[r^{2}-(r\cdot q)^{2}/q^{2}]\,.

(6.18)

Crucially, when the
ratio
{\cal N}/{\cal D}

is formed,
the path-dependent contribution contained in the square bracket cancels out, and Eq. (
6.17
) yields the final relation
[
23
]

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})={\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})\,.

(6.19)

Thus, we reach the important conclusion that the form factor
{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})
appearing in Eq. (
6.16
) is precisely the one measured on the lattice
in the soft-gluon kinematics.
Therefore, Eq. (
6.19
) will be employed in Eq. (
6.16
), in order to substitute
{\cal A}_{1}(r^{2})
in favor of
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
.

Next, we convert Eq. (
6.16
)
from Minkowski to Euclidean space, using the transformation rules given in
Eqs. (
A.2
), (
A.3
), and (
A.4
),
and solve for
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
to obtain
(suppressing the indices “
{\rm E}
”)

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})=\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})-L_{0}(r^{2})\,.

(6.20)

where

L_{0}(r^{2})=F(0)\left\{\frac{{\cal W}(r^{2})}{r^{2}}\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})+\widetilde{Z}_{1}\frac{d\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})}{dr^{2}}\right\}\,.

(6.21)

The quantity

L_{0}(r^{2})
introduced above
is precisely the value that

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
would have in the absence of the
Schwinger mechanism,
i.e.
, if
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

were to vanish identically.

As mentioned at the beginning of this subsection,
the idea now is to
use lattice inputs
for the quantities
appearing on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
6.20
).
In doing so, we note that

L_{0}(r^{2})
in Eq. (
6.21
) may not be
reconstructed in its entirety from lattice ingredients, because
the function
{\cal W}(r^{2})

has not been simulated
on the lattice.
In particular, we will
choose the inputs
in the following way.

(
{\it i}
)
For the gluon propagator
\Delta(r^{2})

we use the lattice data
of
[
23
]
, shown
on the upper left panel
of Fig.
6.2
;
the functional form of the fit (blue curve)
is given in Eq. (C11) of
[
142
]
.

(
{\it ii}
)
The derivative of the inverse propagator is
obtained by differentiating the fit
of
\Delta(r^{2})

from the previous step,
obtaining the curve shown on the upper right panel of
Fig.
6.2
.

(
{\it iii}
)
The
F(0)
is obtained from the lattice results of
[
142
]

for the ghost
dressing function.
Even though only the value of this
curve at the origin,
namely
F(0)=2.88

is required for the
computation in hand,
for future reference
we show the entire
curve
F(r^{2})
on the
lower left panel of
Fig.
6.2
.

(
{\it iv}
)
For the soft-gluon
form factor

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
we use
the lattice results of

[
188
]
,
shown on the lower right panel
of Fig.
6.2
.
In particular,
we employ the fit [blue curve]
given by Eq. (C12) in
[
142
]
.

(
{\it v}
)
All lattice curves
shown in Fig.
6.2

are renormalized within the asymmetric MOM scheme, see App.
B
.
In all cases the renormalization point has been chosen to be

\mu=4.3
MeV.

(
{\it vi}
)
The finite value of the ghost-gluon renormalization constant,
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}
, is determined nonperturbatively from the coupled system of SDEs governing the ghost propagator and ghost-gluon vertex, where
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}
appears explicitly. Specifically, this system is solved with lattice inputs renormalized in the asymmetric MOM scheme, for various values of
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}
until the
\chi^{2}
deviation between the solution for
F(q^{2})
and the lattice data of
[
23
]
is minimized. The procedure is detailed in Sec. 8 of
[
99
]
and yields
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}=0.9333\pm 0.0075
.

(
{\it vii}
)
The function
{\cal W}(r^{2})
is
determined from the SDE obeyed by the ghost-gluon kernel.
The computation, which is rather technical,
is described in App.
G
; the resulting curve for

{\cal W}(r^{2})
is shown as blue continuous on the left panel of Fig.
8.4
.

The inputs (
{\it i}
), (
{\it ii}
),
(
{\it iii}
), (
{\it vi}
), and (
{\it vii}
)
allow us to compute
L_{0}(r^{2})
,
which is to be compared to the
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})

of item (
{\it iv}
); the comparison is
shown on the left panel of Eq. (
6.3
).
Then, using directly Eq. (
6.20
),
a nontrivial result emerges for
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
, represented by the black curve
on the right panel of Fig.
6.3
.
The green error band surrounding this curve
represents the total propagation of the individual errors
assigned to the inputs employed in Eq. (
6.20
).
Note that the resulting curve is

negative
throughout the entire range of available momenta,
in agreement with the observation
in item (
{\it i}
) below
Eq. (
5.40
).

Figure 6.3:
Left:
Lattice data of
[
188
]
for
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
(points), the corresponding fit
(blue continuous), and the null hypothesis prediction,
