L_{0}(r^{2})
, of Eq. (
6.20
) (green dot-dashed).
Right:
Result for
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
(black continuous line) obtained from Eq. (
6.20
); the error
error bars correspond to
the combined error propagated from
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}
and
{\cal W}
. The green band is obtained
by implementing separate
\chi^{2}
fits to
the upper and lower bounds of the points;
its purpose is to provide a visual
impression of
the typical error associated with
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
.

The clear departure of the black curve from the line
{\mathbb{C}}=0
,
indicates that the null hypothesis
(absence of Schwinger mechanism) is strongly disfavored. In fact, as was explained in
[
144
]
,
even if the errors in
all data points
for
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
were
95\%
larger,
i.e.
, nearly doubled, we could still discard the null hypothesis at the
5\sigma
confidence level.

6.4  STI consistency and need for double Schwinger poles

It is clear from the discussion of the previous sections that the presence of massless poles in the vertices of the theory leads to the saturation
of the gluon propagator at the origin. Quite interestingly,
as was demonstrated
in
[
252
]
,
the reverse is also true:
if the gluon propagator saturates at the
origin, the STI of
Eq. (
2.33
) forces the three-gluon vertex to contain precisely this
kind of poles.

To begin our analysis, we decompose the ghost-gluon kernel,
H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)
, appearing in Eq. (
2.33
), into its most general Lorentz structure, namely
[
213
]

H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)=g_{\nu\mu}A_{1}+r_{\mu}r_{\nu}A_{2}+p_{\mu}p_{\nu}A_{3}+p_{\mu}r_{\nu}A_{4}+r_{\mu}p_{\nu}A_{5}\,,

(6.22)

where
A_{i}\equiv A_{i}(r,q,p)
. At tree level,
A_{1}^{(0)}=1
, while all other form factors vanish. Since the
H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)

in Eq. (
2.33
)
appears contracted by transverse projectors,
only the form factors
A_{1}
,

A_{3}
, and
A_{4}

contribute to the STI.

Note that while
H_{\mu\nu}(r,p,q)
may itself contain poles longitudinally coupled to its gluon leg, their residues are known to be negligible in comparison to those of the three-gluon vertex
[
140
,
142
,
252
]
. Hence, for simplicity, we will assume here that
H_{\mu\nu}(r,p,q)
is pole-free. For the complete analysis, where the poles of the ghost-gluon kernel are taken into account, see
[
252
]
.

Next, we decompose both sides of Eq. (
2.33
) in the same basis, and equate the coefficients of the independent tensor structures. Note that each side of the STI has two free Lorentz indices, and two independent momenta; hence, they can be decomposed in the same basis used for
H_{\mu\nu}(r,p,q)
in Eq. (
6.22
). In particular, for the contraction of the gluon momentum with the pole-free part of the vertex, we write

q^{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=S_{1}g_{\mu\nu}+S_{2}r_{\mu}r_{\nu}+S_{3}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}+S_{4}p_{\mu}r_{\nu}+S_{5}r_{\mu}p_{\nu}\,,

(6.23)

with
S_{i}\equiv S_{i}(q,r,p)
. At tree level,
S_{1}^{(0)}=p^{2}-r^{2}
,
S_{2}^{(0)}=1
,
S_{3}^{(0)}=-1
, and
S_{4}^{(0)}=S_{5}^{(0)}=0
. From Bose symmetry,
S_{1}
,
S_{4}
and
S_{5}
must be anti-symmetric under the exchange of
r\leftrightarrow p
, such that

S_{1}(0,r,-r)=S_{4}(0,r,-r)=S_{5}(0,r,-r)=0\,.

(6.24)

Then, since
\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is, by definition, pole-free, its contraction with
q^{\alpha}
vanishes when
q=0
; thus, from Eqs. (
6.23
) and (
6.24
),

\displaystyle S_{2}(0,r,-r)=-S_{3}(0,r,-r)\,.

(6.25)

Consider then the pole part; contracting Eq. (
5.12
) with
q^{\alpha}
, we obtain

q^{\alpha}{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=V_{1}g_{\mu\nu}+\frac{{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{2}}{r^{2}}r_{\mu}r_{\nu}+\frac{{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{3}}{p^{2}}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}+V_{2}p_{\mu}r_{\nu}+\frac{{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{5}}{r^{2}p^{2}}r_{\mu}p_{\nu}\,,

(6.26)

where the
{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{i}\equiv{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{i}(q,r,p)
are given by

\displaystyle{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{2}=

\displaystyle-V_{3}-(p\cdot q)V_{4}-2V_{7}\,,\qquad{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{3}=-V_{5}-(r\cdot q)V_{6}+2V_{9}\,,

\displaystyle{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{5}=

\displaystyle V_{10}+(p^{2}-r^{2})V_{8}-p^{2}\left[V_{3}+(q\cdot p)V_{4}+V_{7}\right]-r^{2}\left[V_{5}+(q\cdot r)V_{6}-V_{9}\right]\,.

(6.27)

With the above decompositions, we proceed to match the coefficients of the independent tensor structures on both sides of Eq. (
2.33
). We begin with the tensors
r_{\mu}r_{\nu}
and
p_{\mu}p_{\nu}
, whose coefficients give, respectively,

\displaystyle S_{2}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left\{F(q^{2})\left\{\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})\left[A_{1}(r,q,p)+(p\cdot r)A_{4}(r,q,p)\right]+r^{2}\Delta^{-1}(p^{2})A_{3}(p,q,r)\right\}\!-\!{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{2}\right\}\,,

\displaystyle S_{3}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\left\{F(q^{2})\left\{p^{2}\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})A_{3}(r,q,p)+\Delta^{-1}(p^{2})\left[A_{1}(p,q,r)+(p\cdot r)A_{4}(p,q,r)\right]\right\}+{\overline{V}}\vphantom{V}_{3}\right\}\,.

(6.28)

Now, consider the
S_{3}
in Eq. (
6.28
),
which is proportional to the factor
1/p^{2}
.
Since, by definition,
S_{3}
is pole-free, the expression in curly brackets must vanish when
p\to 0
, in order for the r.h.s. of Eq. (
6.28
) to be an
evitable
singularity. This condition then requires

V_{9}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{2}m^{2}F(q^{2})A_{1}(q^{2})\,,

(6.29)

where, in Minkowski space,
m^{2}=-\Delta^{-1}(0)
, and we define

V_{9}(q^{2}):=V_{9}(q,-q,0)\,,\qquad A_{1}(q^{2}):=A_{1}(0,q,-q)\,.

(6.30)

Evidently,
V_{9}(q^{2})
is non-zero if the gluon propagator
saturates at the origin.
Hence, from Eq. (
5.12
) follows that
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

contains a pole at
p=0
in
the tensor structure
p_{\nu}q_{\alpha}
.

A relation similar to Eq. (
6.30
) can be obtained for the
q=0
limit of
V_{9}(q,r,p)
. Specifically, it follows from the Bose symmetry of the pole vertex,
i.e.
,

{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=-{\cal V}_{\mu\alpha\nu}(r,q,p)=-{\cal V}_{\nu\mu\alpha}(p,r,q)\,,

(6.31)

that the form factors
V_{i}(q,r,p)
satisfy

\displaystyle V_{1,2}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,-V_{1,2}(q,p,r)\,,\quad

\displaystyle V_{7}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,V_{7}(r,q,p)\,,

\displaystyle V_{3,4}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,-V_{3,4}(p,r,q)\,,\quad

\displaystyle V_{8}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,V_{8}(q,p,r)\,,

\displaystyle V_{5,6}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,-V_{5,6}(r,q,p)\,,\quad

\displaystyle V_{9}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,V_{9}(p,r,q)\,,

(6.32)

while
V_{10}(q,r,p)
is totally anti-symmetric. Furthermore, Bose symmetry relates some form factors to each other by cyclic permutations of their arguments. Specifically,

\displaystyle V_{3,4}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,V_{1,2}(r,p,q)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle V_{8}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,V_{7}(r,p,q)\,,

\displaystyle V_{5,6}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,V_{1,2}(p,q,r)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle V_{9}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,V_{7}(p,q,r)\,.

(6.33)

Then, combining Eqs. (
6.32
) and (
6.29
), it follows that

V_{9}(0,q,-q)=V_{9}(q^{2})\,,

(6.34)

such that the tensor structure
p_{\nu}q_{\alpha}
of
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
has a pole also at
q=0
.

Now, neither
F(0)
nor
A_{1}(0)
vanish in Landau gauge. In particular, as was demonstrated in
[
180
]
, in this gauge,

A_{1}(0)={\widetilde{Z}}_{1}\,,

(6.35)

where we recall that the ghost-gluon renormalization constant,
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}
, is
finite
by virtue of Taylor’s theorem
[
102
]
. Hence, in the limit when both
q
and
p
vanish,
V_{9}(q,r,p)/q^{2}p^{2}
behaves as a double pole,

\lim_{q\to 0}\lim_{p\to 0}\frac{V_{9}(q,r,p)}{q^{2}p^{2}}=\lim_{q\to 0}\lim_{p\to 0}\frac{{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}F(0)m^{2}}{2q^{2}p^{2}}\,.

(6.36)

Similarly, requiring that the
S_{2}
of Eq. (
6.28
) must be pole-free at
r=0
,
we obtain an expression identical to Eq. (
6.29
), but with
V_{9}(q^{2})
substituted by
V_{7}(q,0,-q)
. Thus,
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
contains a pole at
r=0
in the structure
q_{\alpha}r_{\mu}
. Indeed, the same conclusion follows from Eq. (
6.29
) and the Bose symmetry relations of Eqs. (
6.32
) and (
6.33
). In fact, combining Eqs. (
6.32
) and (
6.33
) we get

V_{7}(0,q,-q)=V_{7}(0,q,-q)=V_{8}(q,-q,0)=V_{8}(q,0,-q)=V_{9}(q^{2})\,.

(6.37)

Hence, all of the mixed double poles of
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}
in Eq. (
5.12
) are characterized by the same function
V_{9}(q^{2})
. To appreciate its nature,
consider as an analogy a general function of two variables,
x
and
y
, of the form

f(x,y)=g(x,y)/xy
,
and assume
g(x,0)\neq 0

and
g(0,y)\neq 0
. Then,
f(x,y)

has simple poles as

x\to 0
and
y\to 0
. In particular, for

y\to 0
, the residue of the associated
pole is a function of
x
, given by
r(x)=g(x,0)/x
. The latter has itself a pole at
x=0
, with residue
g(0,0)\neq 0
.
Evidently,
g(x,0)
plays the role of
V_{9}(q^{2})
.

Figure 6.4:
Left:
Form factor
A_{1}(q^{2})
of the ghost-gluon scattering kernel in the soft-antighost limit, determined in
[
213
]
, and converted to the asymmetric MOM scheme through rescaling by a factor of
0.933

[
144
]
.
Right:
The function
V_{9}(q^{2})
, associated with the mixed double poles of the three-gluon vertex, determined through Eq. (
6.29
).

At this point, the function
V_{9}(q^{2})
can be determined numerically from Eq. (
6.29
), using the known forms of the gluon and ghost propagators, and the ghost-gluon form factor,
A_{1}(q^{2})
.

Evidently, since
V_{9}(q^{2})
is a component of the three-gluon vertex, it depends on the renormalization scheme and point chosen, and so do the
F(q^{2})
,
A_{1}(q^{2})
and
m^{2}
appearing in Eq. (
6.29
). Following the convention adopted throughout this work, we renormalize all ingredients in the asymmetric MOM scheme (see App.
B
), choosing
\mu=4.3
GeV for the renormalization point. For
F(q^{2})
we employ the fit to lattice data of
[
23
]
, shown as a blue curve on the lower left panel of Fig.
6.2
. Then, for
A_{1}(q^{2})
we use the SDE result of
[
213
]
, originally renormalized in the so-called “Taylor scheme”
[
102
,
259
,
271
,
272
,
23
]
, after rescaling by a factor of
0.933

[
144
]
to convert it to the asymmetric MOM scheme; the converted form is shown on the left panel of Fig.
6.4
. Finally, we use the value
m=354
MeV, deduced from the lattice determination of the gluon propagator in
[
23
]
. The resulting
V_{9}(q^{2})
is shown on the right panel of Fig.
6.4
;
note that it tends to zero at large momentum.
Comparing the result for
V_{9}(q^{2})
in Fig.
6.4
to the fit for
F(q^{2})
in Fig.
6.2
, it is clear
that the shape of
V_{9}(q^{2})
is dominated by
F(q^{2})
, whereas
A_{1}(q^{2})
contributes modestly, supplying a
12\%
enhancement at momenta
q\sim 1.5
GeV.

It is similarly possible to derive constraints on the remaining form factors
V_{i}
, by analyzing the tensor structures
g_{\mu\nu}
,
r_{\nu}p_{\mu}
and
r_{\mu}p_{\nu}
of Eq. (
2.33
). Specifically, one can show

(
i
) The STI constraints derived for
i=1,3,5
are equivalent to the
WI displacement presented in
Subsec.
6.2
.

(
ii
) In the case
i=2,4,6
, the Bose symmetry relations of Eq. (
6.32
) imply

V_{2}(0,r,-r)=V_{4}(q,0,-q)=V_{6}(q,-q,0)=0\,,

(6.38)

in analogy to Eq. (
5.19
). Then, the
r_{\nu}p_{\mu}
component of Eq. (
2.33
) leads to certain constraints
on the derivatives of the corresponding
V_{i}
, which we do not report here.

(
iii
) Finally, given that in Eq. (
5.12
) the
V_{10}(q,r,p)

is accompanied by a denominator
q^{2}r^{2}p^{2}
, one might expect that this combined term should act as a triple mixed pole. However, as was shown in
[
252
]
, this is
not the case. Instead, equating the
r_{\mu}p_{\nu}
component of the STI of Eq. (
2.33
), one may show that
V_{10}

vanishes rapidly at small momenta, thus reducing substantially the order of the pole associated with this form factor
[
252
]
.

7  Bound-state dynamics: key ingredients and renormalization

In this section we introduce the main dynamical quantities
associated with the formation of
the bound-state poles, derive the fundamental relation that expresses the gluon mass scale in terms of the transition amplitude, and discuss in detail the renormalization procedure.

7.1  Qualitative overview

The line of arguments employed up until this point has been based mostly on symmetries. In particular,
we have considered the modifications
induced to the STIs and WIs
by the inclusion of massless poles,
and how certain key cancellations are distorted, permitting the emergence of a gluon mass scale. In that sense, having
assumed
the formation of the massless poles, we have explored the consequences of their presence in the fundamental vertices of the theory.
Now, we must undertake the more demanding task of actually demonstrating that the dynamics of Yang-Mills theories indeed generates massless poles with the desired characteristics.

Focusing for concreteness on the case of the three-gluon vertex, the main dynamical ideas may be summarized as follows. A central component of the SDE that controls the evolution of the three-gluon vertex is the four-gluon kernel, namely the yellow ellipse denoted by
{\cal T}
in the second panel of Fig.
2.4
.
The kernel
{\cal T}
is one-particle-irreducible with respect to cuts in the direction of the momentum
q
; however, this restriction refers to the elementary fields of the theory (
i.e.
, gluons), but does not apply to the possible
bound states that may be produced
through the fusion of gluons.
Thus, a contribution such as the
one denoted by
{\cal M}

in Fig.
7.2

may arise when two gluons merge to form a

color-carrying

composite scalar,
\Phi^{a}
. Evidently, in order to act as a Schwinger pole, this scalar excitation must be massless.
Exactly similar arguments apply for the other
multi-particle kernels appearing
in the SDE of the three-gluon vertex;
however, their inclusion is not essential for the main bulk of the
underlying dynamics.

As is known from hadron physics, the formation of mesons out
of quarks is governed by integral equations known as BSEs. Similarly,
the emergence of the
\Phi^{a}
out of gluons
is controlled by a special equation of the BS type. Note however a crucial difference:
while the mesons are colorless and appear in the physical spectrum, the massless
excitations carry color and are therefore
not observable.

In general, the solution of the
BSE furnishes the associated “BS amplitude” or
“bound-state wave function”. In the
case of
\Phi^{a}
, the corresponding BS amplitude
is denoted by
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
.
Quite interestingly,
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})

coincides, up to a
constant, with the
displacement/residue
function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
.
The function
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
is the main ingredient
of the amplitude
I_{\mu}(q)=q_{\mu}I(q^{2})
, which
connects a gluon
A_{\mu}^{a}
with the scalar

\Phi^{a}
. It is relatively direct to establish the
profound connection between the gluon mass scale
and
I:=I(0)
, given by the compact and exact
formula
m^{2}=g^{2}I^{2}
.

The proper use of this last formula hinges crucially on one’s ability to carry out reliably the renormalization procedure.
In particular,
the equation that determines

I
must be
renormalized multiplicatively
by the
renormalization constant
Z_{3}
assigned to the three-gluon vertex. The correct implementation of this type of renormalization
is known to be particularly challenging.
In the present case it proceeds
by considering the SDE satisfied by the pole-free part of the three-gluon vertex, shown in Fig.
7.8
; in the soft-gluon limit
(
q\to 0
),
the only form factor that survives is the

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
introduced in the previous section.
Note that, in principle, the integrand of this SDE
involves the kernel

{\cal T}
; however, the contribution from
{\cal M}
does not belong to the
tensorial structure of the pole-free part of the vertex, in the soft-gluon limit.
As a result, it is only the kernel

{\cal K}
that enters in this SDE.
When the SDE is expressed in terms
of renormalized quantities,
the
Z_{3}
appears multiplying the tree-level contribution. Then, one “solves” this SDE formally for
Z_{3}

[
273
]
, which reads schematically

Z_{3}=\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})-\int_{k}\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})K(r,k)\,,

(7.1)

where the kernel
K(r,k)
is essentially

{\cal K}
multiplied by some additional
factors.
Then, the
Z_{3}
appearing in the equation of
I
is to be replaced by the expression on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
7.1
).

7.2  Schwinger poles as composite excitations

In the previous sections we have elucidated
how the presence of massless
poles in the three-gluon and ghost-gluon vertices
induces a mass scale at the level of
the gluon propagator.
Our arguments have been
predominantly based on
symmetries, and, in particular, the special way in which the
STI of Eq. (
2.33
) is resolved in terms of these poles. However, two fundamental questions remain to be addressed.
First, the identification of the precise dynamical mechanism that
gives rise to the formation of the
Schwinger poles.
Second, the determination
of
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

not through the indirect lattice
derivation presented in Subsec.
6.3
,
but rather directly, from the equation that governs its evolution.
In fact, as we will show in detail, the
structure of this equation
leads to a striking simplification, allowing us to carry out the multiplicative renormalization of the
mass formula given in Eq. (
5.40
)

exactly
.

The dynamical picture that
will be outlined in the
next sections may
be summarized as follows.
The Schwinger poles in the vertices are produced dynamically,
when elementary
Yang-Mills
fields
(
e.g.
, two gluons, two ghosts, or three gluons)
merge to form composite colored scalars with
vanishing masses
[
274
,
275
,
92
,
138
,
93
,
201
,
81
]
, to be denoted by

\Phi^{a}
. These nonperturbative processes are controlled by special bound state equations, analogous to the standard BSEs
[
96
,
139
,
97
,
55
,
140
,
141
,
143
,
142
,
100
]
; and the corresponding BS amplitudes are (up to finite multiplicative constants) the residue functions.

From now on we simplify the discussion, focusing exclusively on the three gluon vertex, which captures the bulk of the full effect
[
140
]
.
In this case, the Schwinger poles arise through the fusion of a pair of gluons; there are three main structures associated with this special process, which are diagrammatically depicted in Fig.
7.1
:

(
a
) The effective vertex describing the
interaction between
\Phi^{a}
and two gluons,
denoted by

B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=if^{abc}B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,.

(7.2)

(
b
) The propagator of the
massless composite scalar, having the form

D^{ab}_{\Phi}(q)=\frac{i\delta^{ab}}{q^{2}}\,.

(7.3)

(
c
) The transition amplitude,

I^{ab}_{\alpha}(q)=\delta^{ab}I_{\alpha}(q)
,
connecting a gluon

A^{a}_{\alpha}
with a scalar
\Phi^{b}
. Evidently,
Lorentz invariance imposes that

I_{\alpha}(q)=q_{\alpha}I(q^{2})\,,

(7.4)

where
I(q^{2})
is a scalar form factor.

Figure 7.1:
Left:
The effective vertex
B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, with Lorentz, color, and momentum conventions indicated. Center: The propagator of the massless composite scalar,
D_{\Phi}^{ab}(q)
.
Right:
Gluon-scalar transition amplitude,
I^{ab}_{\alpha}(q)
.

The emergence of these three
items, (
a
)-(
c
),
modifies
crucially the structure
of the four-gluon kernel,

{\cal T}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})
,
which enters in the
skeleton expansion of the three-gluon vertex SDE.
In particular,
as shown diagrammatically in Fig.
7.2
, we have that

{\cal T}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})={\mathcal{K}}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})+{\mathcal{M}}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\,,

(7.5)

where
{\mathcal{K}}

denotes the standard pole-free term, while
{\mathcal{M}}^{mnbc}

is given by

{\mathcal{M}}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=B^{mnx}_{\rho\sigma}(p_{1},p_{2},q)D^{xe}_{\Phi}(q)B^{ebc}_{\mu\nu}(p_{3},p_{4},-q)\,,

(7.6)

with
q=p_{1}+p_{2}=-p_{3}-p_{4}
.

We stress at this point that, despite appearances, the
kernel
{\cal T}

is one-particle irreducible,
because the propagator
D_{\Phi}(q)

in
{\mathcal{M}}

does not represent
a fundamental field of the Yang-Mills Lagrangian, but rather a composite
excitation, the collective result of countless
multi-gluonic
interactions.

Figure 7.2:
Decomposition of the four-gluon scattering kernel,
{\cal T}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})
, into a regular part,
{\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})
, and a massless pole part,
{\cal M}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})
, according to Eq. (
7.5
).

The massless pole in
{\mathcal{M}}
is eventually transferred to the gluon self-energy through
the full three-gluon vertex that appears
in diagram (
a_{1}
) of Fig.
2.4
.
Specifically, consider the
SDE for the three-gluon vertex, written from the
point of view of the gluon
leg carrying momentum

q
; this choice is dictated by the fact that

q
has been identified with the momentum carried by the gluon propagator
[see Fig.
2.4
], while the other two channels
are connected to propagators internal to the graph (carrying the integration momentum
k
).
The diagrammatic representation of the vertex SDE
is given in Fig.
7.3
, where we only
keep the
diagrams relevant to our purposes (first row),
while the omitted
ones have no bearing on any of our conclusions [see comments following Eq. (
7.9
)].

Figure 7.3:
First line
: SDE for the three-gluon vertex.
Second line
: The pole induced to the three-gluon vertex due to the
component
{\cal M}
of
{\cal T}
in Eq. (
7.5
) (see also Fig.
7.2
).

The inclusion of the term
{\mathcal{M}}^{mnbc}
into the vertex SDE gives rise to a pole term,
V_{\alpha\mu\nu}
, for
the three-gluon vertex
(second row).
In fact, the term that this procedure generates is
precisely the

V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

introduced in Eq. (
5.13
).

Specifically, as may be read off from Fig.
7.3
,

V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=I_{\alpha}(q)\left(\frac{i}{q^{2}}\right)iB_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,.

(7.7)

Then, by virtue of Eq. (
7.4
), this term becomes

V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=-\left(\frac{q_{\alpha}}{q^{2}}\right)I(q^{2})B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,.

(7.8)

Since
V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
satisfies

P^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}(q)V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=0
,
we conclude that
the bound-state realization of the Schwinger poles
enforces their longitudinal
nature

dynamically
.

The central result of this
construction is obtained
when the component

V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

of the three-gluon vertex

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

is inserted into the
SDE that determines
the momentum evolution of the
gluon propagator
[see Fig.
2.4
]:

V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

provides the massless pole
required for the activation of the Schwinger mechanism.

To see this precisely,
let us focus on the
part of the gluon propagator proportional to
q_{\mu}q_{\nu}
. Then,
we obtain immediately the characteristic diagram shown in Fig.
7.4
,
composed by the “square” of
the transition amplitude

I_{\alpha}(q)
. When the
limit
q\to 0
is taken,
one arrives at the
fundamental result
[
92
,
96
,
97
]

m^{2}=g^{2}I^{2}\,,

(7.9)

where the short-hand notation
I:=I(0)
has been introduced.

Figure 7.4:
Relation between the gluon mass and the transition amplitude,
I_{\alpha}(q)
.

It is important to stress that
in Figs.
7.3
and
7.4
all diagrams
containing ghosts-gluon or
four-gluon vertices have been omitted.
The omission of these graphs
has no impact on our qualitative conclusions for the
following two reasons. First, as we will see in Subsec.
8.4
, the renormalization of Eq. (
7.9
) induces an extensive cancellation, which would eliminate all these graphs, provided that the ghost-gluon and four-gluon vertices contain no Schwinger poles.
In omitting these terms we essentially
assume that the pole residue of the four-gluon vertex is numerically subleading, as is known to happen in the case of the ghost-gluon vertex
[
140
]
.
Second, the main function of the
vertex SDE is to participate in the
aforementioned cancellation, where
the graph retained in Fig.
7.4

is precisely what one needs. In fact, while the quantity
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
is a key component
in some of the fundamental formulas, its form will not be determined from the vertex SDE, but rather from extensive lattice simulations of the three-gluon vertex
[
186
,
187
,
246
,
188
,
23
,
247
,
248
,
249
,
250
,
251
]
.

The bound-state origin of the Schwinger poles may be used to explain the
structure of not only the component

V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
,
but of the entire vertex

{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
,
given Eq. (
5.12
).
Indeed, as may be clearly seen
in Fig.
7.5
, in compliance
with the Bose symmetry of
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, Schwinger poles appear also in the
channels carrying momenta
r
or
p
,
being
proportional to

I_{\mu}(r)
or

I_{\nu}(p)
. These structures, in turn, give rise
to mixed poles, precisely as seen in
Eq. (
5.12
).
In fact,
the effective amplitudes

B_{\mu\nu}
,

B_{\mu}
, and
B
in
Fig.
7.5

may be expressed in terms
of the form factors
V_{i}

in Eq. (
5.12
),
through the
direct matching of the various
tensorial structures,
namely

\displaystyle I(q^{2})B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,-\left[g_{\mu\nu}V_{1}(q,r,p)+p_{\mu}r_{\nu}V_{2}(q,r,p)\right]\,,

\displaystyle I(q^{2})I(r^{2})B_{\nu}(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,-(r-q)_{\nu}V_{7}(q,r,p)\,,

\displaystyle I(q^{2})I(r^{2})I(p^{2})B(q,r,p)=

\displaystyle\,\,iV_{10}(q,r,p)\,.

(7.10)

Figure 7.5:
The general structure
of the three-gluon vertex after the
activation of the Schwinger
mechanism. Note, in particular,
that the term
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
contains single poles, such as
q_{\alpha}/q^{2}
, as well as mixed poles of the forms
q_{\alpha}r_{\mu}/q^{2}r^{2}
and
q_{\alpha}r_{\mu}p_{\nu}/q^{2}r^{2}p^{2}
. The term “(perms)” denotes the permutations of the external legs that lead to a Bose-symmetric
{\cal V}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
.

Let us next consider
the behavior of the
two main building blocks,
namely of the
effective vertex
B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

and the transition amplitude
I(q^{2})
,
as
q\to 0
.

The general tensorial
decomposition of
B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is given by

B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=B_{1}\,g_{\mu\nu}+B_{2}\,r_{\mu}r_{\nu}+B_{3}\,p_{\mu}p_{\nu}+B_{4}\,r_{\mu}p_{\nu}+B_{5}\,p_{\mu}r_{\nu}\,,

(7.11)

where
B_{i}:=B_{i}(q,r,p)
are scalar form factors.

The Bose symmetry of the full vertex

B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

under the
simultaneous
exchange

(r,b,\mu)\leftrightarrow(p,c,\nu)
means that

B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=B^{acb}_{\nu\mu}(q,p,r)
.
Since
f^{abc}
has been factored out in
Eq. (
7.2
), we have that

B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=-B_{\nu\mu}(q,p,r)
. Then, setting
q=0
(
p=-r
) in this last relation, we find

B_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=-B_{\nu\mu}(0,r,-r)
.
So, since

B_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=B_{1}(0,r,-r)\,g_{\mu\nu}+C_{1}(0,r,-r)r_{\mu}r_{\nu}\,,

(7.12)

where
C_{1}:=B_{2}+B_{3}-B_{4}-B_{5}
,
we conclude that

B_{1}(0,r,-r)=0=C_{1}(0,r,-r)\,\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,B_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=0\,.

(7.13)

Let us proceed by
considering the Taylor expansion
of
B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
around
q=0
,

B_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=q^{\alpha}{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)+\ldots\,,

(7.14)

where
the ellipsis denotes
terms of higher order in
q
, and
the shorthand notation

{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r):=\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{\alpha}}B^{\mu\nu}(q,r,\,-r-q)\right]_{q=0}\,,

(7.15)

has been introduced,
supplemented by the diagrammatic representation
shown in Fig.
7.6
.
Note that
when taking the limit indicated in Eq. (
7.15
),
the momentum

r
is treated as independent of
q
;
then,
due to the conservation of four-momentum,
p
depends on
q
,
since
p=-q-r
,
and
therefore,

\partial p^{\mu}/\partial q^{\alpha}=-g^{\mu}_{\alpha}
.

We next consider the contraction of
{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)
by
P_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r)P_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(r)
; in the Landau gauge, this contraction occurs naturally in the diagrams where
B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is inserted, because the two gluon propagators to which it is attached are completely transverse.
Since
the tensorial decomposition of

{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)
is given by

{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)={\cal B}_{1}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}+{\cal B}_{2}(r^{2})\left[r_{\mu}g_{\alpha\nu}+r_{\nu}g_{\mu\alpha}\right]+{\cal B}_{3}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}r_{\mu}r_{\nu}\,,

(7.16)

only the first term
in Eq. (
7.16
) survives this contraction,
namely

{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)P_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r)P_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(r)={\cal B}_{1}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}P_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(r)\,.

(7.17)

It is then straightforward to establish that

r_{\alpha}{\cal B}_{1}(r^{2})=\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{\alpha}}B_{1}(q,r,\,-r-q)\right]_{q=0}=2r_{\alpha}\underbrace{\left[\frac{\partial{B}_{1}(q,r,p)}{\partial p^{2}}\right]_{q=0}}_{\mathbb{B}(r^{2})}\,,

(7.18)

or

{\cal B}_{1}(r^{2})=2\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,,

(7.19)

from which follows that

{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=2\,\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,r_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}+\ldots\,,

(7.20)

and then, from Eq. (
7.14
),

B_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=2\,(q\cdot r)\,\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,g_{\mu\nu}+\ldots\,,

(7.21)

where the ellipses denote
terms that get annihilated upon the
aforementioned contraction.
The diagrammatic representation of
Eq. (
7.20
)
is shown in
Fig.
7.6
, and will be used in the analysis that
follows.

Figure 7.6:
Diagrammatic representation of Eq. (
7.20
), corresponding to the first nonvanishing term in the Taylor expansion of
B_{\mu\nu}^{abc}(q,r,p)
around
q=0
.

Finally, combining Eqs. (
5.20
), (
7.7
) and (
7.21
), we obtain a relation between the displacement function,
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
, and the BS amplitude,
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, namely

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2}):=-I\,\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,,

(7.22)

which is the result announced in item (
a
) of Sec
5.2
.

We next
turn to the
transition amplitude

{I}^{\alpha}(q)
.
In view of the central relation given by
Eq. (
7.9
), we must
determine
the quantity
I
in terms of
the basic elements entering in its diagrammatic definition, shown on
item (
iii
) of
Fig.
7.1
.

In particular, the
transition amplitude

{I}^{\alpha}(q)

for general momentum
q
is given by

I^{\alpha}(q)=-\frac{iC_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}(q,k,-t)\Delta_{\beta\mu}(k)\Delta_{\lambda\nu}(t)B^{\mu\nu}(-q,-k,t)\,,

(7.23)

where Eq. (
7.2
) was employed,
t:=k+q
,
and the symmetry factor

\frac{1}{2}
has been included.

Then, from Eq. (
7.4
),
it is elementary to
deduce that

I=\frac{1}{4}\left[\frac{\partial I^{\alpha}(q)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}\,,

(7.24)

which has the diagrammatic representation shown in Fig.
7.7
. Thus, from Eq. (
7.23
) we obtain

\displaystyle 4I=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{iC_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\Gamma_{\!0}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}(q,k,-t)\Delta_{\beta\mu}(k)\Delta_{\lambda\nu}(t)\right]_{q=0}\!\!\!\!\!\!B^{\mu\nu}(0,-k,k)

\displaystyle\,-\frac{iC_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}(0,k,-k)\Delta_{\beta}^{\mu}(k)\Delta_{\lambda}^{\nu}(k)\,{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,-k,k)\,,

(7.25)

and, since
B_{\mu\nu}(0,-k,k)=0

[see Eq. (
7.13
)], we simply have

4I=-\frac{iC_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}(0,k,-k)\Delta_{\beta}^{\mu}(k)\Delta_{\lambda}^{\nu}(k)\,{\cal B}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,-k,k)\,.

(7.26)

Eq. (
7.26
) may be further evaluated by
using that

\Gamma_{\!0}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}(0,k,-k)=2k^{\alpha}g^{\beta\lambda}-k^{\lambda}g^{\alpha\beta}-k^{\beta}g^{\alpha\lambda}\,,

(7.27)

and then Eq. (
7.20
) (with
r\to-k
),
to obtain the final expression

I=-\frac{3iC_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\,.

(7.28)

Figure 7.7:
Diagrammatic definition of the scalar form factor,
I
, of the transition amplitude.

7.3  SDE of the pole-free vertex in the soft-gluon limit

It turns out that
the soft-gluon limit of the SDE that controls the pole-free component,

\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}^{abc}(q,r,p)
,
of the three-gluon vertex is of central importance for the
determination of the gluon mass scale.
Indeed, as we will see in detail
in Subsec.
8.4
,
its judicious use
leads to substantial simplifications
at the level of the
renormalized gluon mass equation.
In order to clarify this important facet,
we consider the SDE
represented in Fig.
7.8
,
composed only by the tree-level contribution

(a_{1})
and the diagram (
a_{2}
);
diagrams where the
gluon with momentum
q
couples to a
ghost-gluon or a four-gluon vertex are omitted.
The reason why diagram (
a_{2}
) is singled out
is because its
kernel
{\cal K}
appears also in
the BSE for
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, shown in
Fig.
8.1
;
as we will see, this fact is instrumental for the implementation of the renormalization procedure illustrated in Subsec.
8.4
.

Figure 7.8:
SDE for the regular part of the three-gluon vertex,
\Gamma^{abc}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
,
after the implementation of the skeleton expansion.

In the standard version of this SDE,
the three-gluon
vertex
in diagram
(a_{2})

is kept at tree level.
The form employed here,
with the three-gluon vertex
fully-dressed,
corresponds to the
version of this SDE
after the skeleton expansion has been implemented
[
273
,
106
]
.
Equivalently, this version of the SDE corresponds to the
equation of motion
for the vertex
obtained within the formalism of the
3-particle irreducible
effective action
at three-loops
[
202
,
206
,
207
,
209
,
214
]
.
Consequently, the
diagrammatic expansion of the
kernel
\cal K
, given in
Fig.
7.9
,
does not contain certain classes of diagrams
(
e.g.
, ladder graphs) in
order to avoid overcounting. In fact,
with the exception of
the ghost loops, the diagrams of Fig.
7.9
comprise exactly the kernel of the standard glueball BSE
[
63
,
66
,
68
]
.

The main advantage of this SDE
is that the additional fully-dressed vertex
absorbs the vertex renormalization

Z_{3}
, defined in Eq. (
2.35
),
which otherwise would be
multiplying the tree-level vertex. This is
technically very advantageous, because,
as we will see in the
next subsection,
the renormalization may be carried out subtractively
rather than multiplicatively.

We now set
\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}^{abc}(q,r,p)=f^{abc}\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
,
and evaluate the soft gluon limit of the SDE by substituting

q=0
into
Fig.
7.8
.
In particular,
we find

f^{abc}\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=f^{abc}\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)\,+\,f^{amn}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\alpha\gamma\delta}(0,k,-k)\Delta^{\gamma\rho}(k)\Delta^{\delta\sigma}(k){\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,k,r,-r)\,.

(7.29)

Figure 7.9:
Skeleton expansion of the scattering kernel,
{\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})
. The one-gluon exchange is highlighted and the ellipsis denotes contributions with two or more loops.

We next
consider the contraction of

\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)

by
P_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r)P_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(r)
;
the resulting projection is described in terms of a single form factor,
denoted by
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
, to wit
[
23
,
142
]

P_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r)P_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(r)\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=2\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})r_{\alpha}P_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(r)\,.

(7.30)

Note, in fact, that,
in the Landau gauge that we employ, the
\Gamma_{\alpha\gamma\delta}(0,k,-k)
on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
7.29
) is automatically contracted by two transverse projectors, thus triggering Eq. (
7.30
) therein. Therefore,
contracting both sides of Eq. (
7.29
)
by
P^{\mu}_{\mu^{\prime}}(r)P^{\nu}_{\nu^{\prime}}(r)
,
we obtain

f^{abc}\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})r_{\alpha}P_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(r)=f^{abc}r_{\alpha}P_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(r)-f^{amn}\int_{k}k_{\alpha}\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})\Delta^{2}(k^{2})P^{\rho\sigma}(k){\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,k,r,-r)P^{\mu}_{\mu^{\prime}}(r)P^{\mu}_{\mu^{\prime}}(r)\,.

(7.31)

The last step is to contract Eq. (
7.31
) by
f^{abc}r^{\alpha}g^{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}
, in order to eliminate the color and Lorentz indices.
Introducing
the strong charge
\alpha_{s}=g^{2}/4\pi
,
using that
P^{\mu}_{\mu}(r)=3
, and that for SU(
N
),

f^{abc}f^{abc}=C_{\rm A}(N^{2}-1)
, we finally arrive at

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})=\,1+\alpha_{s}\!\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\,{K}(r,k)\,\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})\,,

(7.32)

where the kernel
K(r,k)
is defined as

\alpha_{s}K(r,k):=-\frac{(r\cdot k)}{c\,r^{2}k^{2}}f^{abc}f^{amn}P^{\mu\nu}(r)P^{\rho\sigma}(k)\,{\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,k,r,-r)\,,

(7.33)

The numerical factor

c:=3C_{\rm A}(N^{2}-1)

arises from the projections of Lorentz and color indices;
for
N=3
,
c=72
.

7.4  Renormalization

We now turn to a
pivotal aspect of this analysis, and
illustrate in detail the renormalization of the main dynamical
components that arise from the activation of the Schwinger mechanism. In particular, we will elaborate on
the renormalization properties of the
key
quantities
I
and
B_{\mu\nu}
.

We begin by recalling that the gluon propagator and three-gluon vertex are renormalized by the constants
Z_{A}
and
Z_{3}
, defined in Eq. (
2.35
), which are related to the
gauge coupling renormalization constant,

Z_{g}
, through
Eq. (
2.36
).
Moreover, we will introduce two additional renormalization constants, to be denoted by
Z_{I}
and
Z_{B}
, which renormalize the quantities
I
and
B^{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, respectively.
In particular
[
276
]
,

I_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}=Z^{-1}_{I}I\,,\quad\quad B_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}^{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=Z^{-1}_{B}B^{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,.

(7.34)

The partial derivatives of

B^{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
also renormalize
in the same way, namely

{\cal B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}^{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=Z^{-1}_{B}{\cal B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}^{\alpha\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)\,,\quad\quad\mathbb{B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(r^{2})=Z^{-1}_{B}\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,.

(7.35)

Since both
I
and

B^{\mu\nu}
are comprised by the fundamental QCD components, it is natural to expect
that both
Z_{I}
and
Z_{B}
can eventually
be expressed in terms of the

Z_{A}
and
Z_{3}
introduced in
Eq. (
2.35
). In fact, it is straightforward to deduce that
[
276
]

Z_{I}=Z^{-1}_{3}Z_{A}\,,\quad\quad\quad Z_{B}=Z^{-1}_{A}\,.

(7.36)

To see how the relations in Eq. (
7.36
) arise, let us
first consider Eq. (
7.9
).
Since
m^{2}:=\Delta^{-1}(0)
,
from the first relation in Eq. (
2.35
) we have that
[
276
,
98
]

m^{2}=Z^{-1}_{A}m^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,.

(7.37)

On the other hand, combining
Eq. (
7.9
), the first relation in Eq. (
7.34
),
and Eq. (
2.36
), we find

m^{2}=g^{2}I^{2}=Z^{2}_{g}\,Z^{2}_{I}\,\underbrace{g^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}I^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}}_{m^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}}=Z^{2}_{3}\,Z^{-3}_{A}\,Z^{2}_{I}\,m^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,.

(7.38)

Then, the direct comparison of Eqs. (
7.37
) and (
7.38
)
leads immediately to the first relation of
Eq. (
7.36
).

The second relation of Eq. (
7.36
) may be obtained
from Eq. (
7.22
), by recognizing
that,
since
V_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is a component of the three-gluon vertex

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma^{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
,
it is renormalized
according to Eq. (
2.35
), namely

V^{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=Z^{-1}_{3}\,V^{\alpha\mu\nu}_{\!\!{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(q,r,p)\,,

(7.39)

and, consequently,

{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})=Z^{-1}_{3}\,{\mathbb{C}}_{\!{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})\,.

(7.40)

On the other hand, from Eq. (
7.22
), using Eq. (
7.34
) and the first relation of
Eq. (
7.36
), we
get

{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})=-Z_{I}Z_{B}\underbrace{I_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\mathbb{B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})}_{-{\mathbb{C}}_{\!{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})}=Z^{-1}_{3}Z_{A}Z_{B}\,{\mathbb{C}}_{\!{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})\,.

(7.41)

Then, the comparison between
Eqs. (
7.40
) and (
7.41
) yields directly
the second relation of Eq. (
7.36
).

We now address the renormalization of the kernel

{\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(q,r,p,t)
, which appears in
Eq. (
7.29
), and later on in
Eq. (
8.8
).
We start by recognizing that

{\cal K}
is a part of the
four-gluon amplitude

{\cal G}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(q,r,p,t)
,
which, in terms of gauge fields is given by

{\cal G}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(q,r,p,t)=\langle 0|T[\widetilde{A}^{m}_{\rho}(q)\widetilde{A}^{n}_{\sigma}(r)\widetilde{A}^{b}_{\mu}(p)\widetilde{A}^{c}_{\nu}(t)]|0\rangle
, but with the external
legs amputated,
i.e.
,

{\cal G}(q,r,p,t)=\Delta(q^{2})\Delta(r^{2})\Delta(p^{2})\Delta(t^{2}){\cal K}(q,r,p,t)+\cdots

(7.42)

where the ellipsis indicates
the diagrams excluded when passing from the SDE to the BSE kernel,
as explained in Subsec.
7.3
.
Since,
A^{a\mu}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}=Z^{1/2}_{A}A^{a\mu}
, the above definition of
{\cal G}
in terms of gauge fields implies that
{\cal G}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}=Z^{-2}_{A}\,{\cal G}
.
Therefore,
from Eqs. (
7.42
) and (
2.35
), we get

{\cal K}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(q,r,p,t)=Z^{2}_{A}\,{\cal K}(q,r,p,t)\,,

(7.43)

and, since

{\cal K}\sim\alpha_{s}K
, we
arrive at

{K}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(q,r,p,t)=Z^{2}_{A}Z^{2}_{g}\,{K}(q,r,p,t)\,.

(7.44)

With the aid of the above relations, it is easy to prove that
the combinations

\Delta{\mathbb{B}}
,

\Delta^{2}{\cal K}
,
and
\alpha_{s}\Delta^{2}K

are
renormalization-group invariant (RGI),
i.e.
,

\Delta\mathbb{B}=\Delta_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\mathbb{B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,,\qquad\qquad\Delta^{2}{\cal K}=\Delta^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,{\cal K}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,,\qquad\qquad\alpha_{s}\Delta^{2}K=\alpha_{s}^{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\Delta^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}K_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,.

(7.45)

As a self-consistency check,
note that, by virtue of
the renormalization rule

B_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}^{\mu\nu}=Z_{A}B^{\mu\nu}
,
given by
Eqs. (
7.34
) and (
7.36
),
the kernel
{\mathcal{M}}

defined in
Eq. (
7.6
)
renormalizes as

{\cal M}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}=Z^{2}_{A}\,{\cal M}
,

i.e.
, exactly as
the kernel
{\mathcal{K}}
in
Eq. (
7.43
);
this is precisely as expected, given that both
{\mathcal{K}}
and

{\mathcal{M}}
are parts
of the same four-gluon kernel, see
Eq. (
7.5
) and Fig.
7.3
.

Capitalizing
on the above results,
we may now derive a useful
expression for the
I_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}
,
using Eq. (
7.26
) as our
point of departure.
Specifically, substituting the bare quantities
entering in Eq. (
7.26
)
by renormalized ones, we have

4Z_{I}I_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}=-\frac{iC_{\rm A}}{2}Z^{2}_{A}Z_{B}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}(0,k,-k)\Delta_{{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,\beta}^{\mu}(k)\Delta_{{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,\lambda}^{\nu}(k)\,{\cal B}_{\!{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,\alpha\mu\nu}(0,-k,k)\,,

(7.46)

and, after
employing Eq. (
7.36
),

4I_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}=-\frac{iC_{\rm A}}{2}Z_{3}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}(0,k,-k)\Delta_{{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,\beta}^{\mu}(k)\Delta_{{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,\lambda}^{\nu}(k)\,{\cal B}_{\!{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,\alpha\mu\nu}(0,-k,k)\,,

(7.47)

or, from
Eq. (
7.28
),

I_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}=-\frac{3iC_{\rm A}}{2}Z_{3}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}^{2}(k^{2})\mathbb{B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})\,.

(7.48)

We conclude this subsection with
the renormalization of the SDE given in Eq. (
7.32
).
By virtue of
Eq. (
7.30
),
it is clear that

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(r^{2})=Z_{3}\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
;
then, using
Eq. (
7.45
),
it is
straightforward to show that
the renormalized version of Eq. (
7.32
) reads

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(r^{2})=\,Z_{3}+\alpha_{s}^{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\!\!\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})\,{K}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(r,k)\,\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})\,.

(7.49)

As emphasized in
Subsec.
7.3
,
the renormalization required for Eq. (
7.49
)
is subtractive.

Note finally that,
in order to simplify the notation,
in what follows
we will drop the indices “R”
from all renormalized
equations.

8  Emergence of the gluon mass scale

In this section we set up and solve the BSE that determines the formation of the
Schwinger poles, and illustrate the profound role that the Fredholm alternative theorem plays
when implementing multiplicative
renormalization. In addition, we carry out the numerical analysis and obtain the values of the gluon mass scale and the displacement function.

8.1  Qualitative overview

In order to explore the practical implications of the formalism developed thus far, we must solve the set of equations that determine the quantities
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
,
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
, and
I
, defined in the previous section.
In the process of solving these equations one discovers a striking cancellation, which is a direct consequence of the so-called Fredholm alternative theorem.

In order to illustrate this point, note that
the equations
determining
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
and

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
may be written in a unified way, as distinct components of a more general equation.
Specifically, the inhomogeneous BSE, which coincides with the renormalized SDE equation for the full three-gluon vertex, can be written schematically as

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma=Z_{3}\Gamma_{\!0}+\int_{k}{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma\Delta^{2}{\cal K}+ID_{\Phi}\Omega D_{\Phi}B\,,

(8.1)

where the third term on the r.h.s.
represents the contribution of the bound state
\Phi
, contained
in the part
{\cal M}
of the complete kernel

{\cal T}={\cal K}+{\cal M}
, shown in Fig.
7.2
.

We next consider the above equation at small
q
, where the vertex irregularity appears. In that limit we get

\displaystyle\left[r_{\alpha}\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})-q_{\alpha}\frac{(q\cdot r)}{q^{2}}I\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\right]P_{\mu\nu}(r)=

\displaystyle\,Z_{3}r_{\alpha}P_{\mu\nu}(r)+\int_{k}\left[k_{\alpha}\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})-q_{\alpha}\frac{(q\cdot k)}{q^{2}}I\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\right]\Delta^{2}{\cal K}P_{\mu\nu}(r)

\displaystyle\,-q_{\alpha}P_{\mu\nu}(r)\frac{(q\cdot r)}{q^{2}}I\omega\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,.

(8.2)

Importantly, the term
\omega
in the last line is an integral quadratic in
\mathbb{B}
, which becomes a constant (
r
-independent) in the limit
q\to 0
.

The above expression can then be separated into two scalar equations that must be satisfied simultaneously. First, one obtains an inhomogeneous equation for
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
, namely Eq. (
7.49
), by isolating the
r_{\alpha}
component. This can be achieved either by acting with suitable projectors, or by choosing
q
to be orthogonal to
r
(
i.e.
, noting that
q\cdot r=qr\cos\theta
and choosing the independent angle
\theta=\pi/2
), and contracting by
r^{\alpha}/r^{2}
. On the other hand, isolating the massless pole terms yields a homogeneous equation for
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
. Hence, we obtain a system of equations of the form

\displaystyle\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})=

\displaystyle\,Z_{3}+\int_{k}\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})K(r,k)\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}(r^{2})=

\displaystyle\,\int_{k}\mathbb{B}(k^{2})K(r,k)+\omega\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,.

(8.3)

Crucially, due to the fact that
\omega
is quadratic in
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, the homogeneous equation
is cubic in
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
.
However, since
\omega
is constant, it can be reabsorbed into an effective redefinition of the coupling, affecting only the homogeneous equation, which can then be recast as

\mathbb{B}(r^{2})=\tau^{-1}\int_{k}\mathbb{B}(k^{2})K(r,k)\,,

(8.4)

with
\tau=1-\omega
. As a consequence, the homogeneous BSE, even though
cubic in nature, may still be solved
as an eigenvalue problem, which is the standard treatment of homogeneous linear BSEs.

The first consequence of the presence of the cubic term is that the scale of the solution obtained for
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})

[and, therefore, the scale of
the displacement/residue function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
] is completely fixed.
The second consequence is considerably more
far-reaching: the gluon mass scale is directly proportional to
\omega
; in other words, in the absence of the
cubic term (
i.e.
,
\omega=0
), the gluon mass scale vanishes identically.

To see how this second consequence comes about, remember that
the renormalization of the
equation that controls the term
I
proceeds through the use of Eq. (
7.1
). When
\omega=0
,
we have that
{\cal T}
=
{\cal K}
;
therefore,
when the r.h.s. of Eq. (
7.1
) is
inserted in the equation for
I
, it
may be re-interpreted as the BSE for

\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, namely
B(r^{2})-\int_{k}B(k^{2})K(r,k)=0
.
This means that, in the absence of the
nonlinear term, the
I
vanishes, and so does the gluon mass.

As alluded above, the deeper reason for this cancellation is the Fredholm alternative theorem, which is known to relate the solutions of a homogeneous and inhomogeneous integral equation with the same kernel. In our case, the homogeneous equation is the BSE for
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, while the inhomogeneous one is the SDE for
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
,
i.e.
, the system comprised by Eq. (
8.3
).
In the absence of the nonlinear term
\omega
, the kernels of the BSE and the SDE are identical; then, the condition for having nontrivial solutions for both equations imposed by the theorem is that the expression for
I
should vanish,
i.e.
, no gluon mass scale. Instead, the presence of the cubic term (
\omega\neq 0
)
invalidates the assumption of equal kernels,
required for the theorem to apply, and therefore, the adverse condition
I=0
is
evaded.

Once renormalization has been duly implemented, and the extensive cancellations
induced by the aforementioned theorem have been carried out, one arrives at a set of manifestly
finite equations. These equations must be then treated numerically, in order to obtain the value of the gluon mass scale

m
and the shape and size of the
displacement function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
.
The benchmark values for these
quantities are the saturation point
of the gluon propagator computed on the
lattice [
m^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle lat}}}=\Delta{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle lat}}}^{-1}(0)
], and the

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
obtained from the
lattice-based analysis of
subsection
6.3
.
In order to approach
these reference values,
one implements variations
around the form of the
four-gluon kernel
{\cal K}
obtained
within the “one-gluon exchange” approximation. In this way,
the benchmark value

m_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle lat}}}=354
MeV is reached within
about
4\%
. On the other hand, the
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
so obtained displays
the same qualitative trend as the
benchmark curve, but
differs considerably in size.

8.2  Bethe-Salpeter equation for Schwinger pole formation

We now turn to one of the main dynamical issues associated with the generation of a
gluonic mass scale,
namely the
BSE satisfied by the amplitude

\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
.
The structure of this
BSE turns out to be decisive for the
success of the entire endeavor, not only because it admits
nontrivial solutions for
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, but also because it
is instrumental for the successful and concise implementation of the renormalization program.
In this presentation we follow the
construction
developed in
[
100
]
, where the non-linear structure
of the BSE is fully retained
; for earlier, linearized versions
of the same equation
see
[
96
,
97
,
98
,
140
,
142
]
.

The diagrammatic form of the BSE
for the effective vertex
B_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

is shown in the left part of Fig.
8.1
,
where the four-gluon kernel

{\cal T}
is depicted in Fig.
7.2
.
In particular, from the
first equality of Fig.
8.1

we have

B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=(G_{\cal T})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,,

(8.5)

where

(G_{\cal T})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\int_{k}B^{axe}_{\alpha\beta}(q,k,-t)\Delta_{xm}^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta_{en}^{\beta\sigma}(t){\cal T}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,t,r,p)\,,

(8.6)

with
t:=k+q
.
Then,
after using Eqs. (
7.5
) and (
7.6
), two
distinct terms appear, namely

(G_{\cal T})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=(G_{\cal K})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+(G_{\!{\cal M}})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,,

(8.7)

with

\displaystyle(G_{\cal K})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{k}B^{axe}_{\alpha\beta}(q,k,-t)\Delta_{xm}^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta_{en}^{\beta\sigma}(t){\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,t,r,p)\,,

\displaystyle(G_{\!{\cal M}})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega^{ad}(q)D^{ds}_{\Phi}(q)B^{sbc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)\,,

(8.8)

where (symmetry factor
\frac{1}{2}
included)

\Omega^{ad}(q)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{k}B^{axe}_{\alpha\beta}(q,k,-t)\Delta_{xm}^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta_{en}^{\beta\sigma}(t)B^{dnm}_{\sigma\rho}(-q,t,-k)\,.

(8.9)

Note that, while the term
(G_{\cal K})
is linear in

B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}
, the term
(G_{\!{\cal M}})
is cubic.

The first key step
in the treatment of this BSE is to
show that
the product
\Omega^{ad}(q)D^{ds}_{\Phi}(q)
appearing in
(G_{\!{\cal M}})

is finite and
nonvanishing as

q\to 0
. This is
indeed so, because, in that limit,

\Omega^{ad}(q)\sim q^{2}\delta^{ad}
, thus canceling exactly the massless pole contained in

D^{ds}_{\Phi}(q)
.

To demonstrate this
important result,
set first

\Omega^{ad}(q)=\delta^{ad}\Omega(q^{2})
in
Eq. (
8.9
),
and carry out the color algebra
to obtain

\Omega(q^{2})=\frac{C_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}B_{\alpha\beta}(q,k,-t)P^{\alpha\rho}(k)P^{\beta\sigma}(t)B_{\rho\sigma}(-q,-k,t)\Delta(k^{2})\Delta(t^{2})\,.

(8.10)

Then, taking the limit
q\to 0

of Eq. (
8.10
) using Eq. (
7.21
),
we find

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 0}\Omega(q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}{B}_{\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)P^{\alpha\rho}(k)P^{\beta\sigma}(k){B}_{\sigma\rho}(0,k,-k)\Delta^{2}(k^{2})

(8.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 6C_{\rm A}\int_{k}(q\cdot k)^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\mathbb{B}^{2}(k^{2})\,,

and consequently

\lim_{q\to 0}\Omega(q^{2})=q^{2}{\widetilde{\omega}}\,,\qquad\qquad{\widetilde{\omega}}:=\frac{3C_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\mathbb{B}^{2}(k^{2})\,.

(8.12)

Armed with this result, it is
straightforward to establish that

(G_{\!{\cal M}})^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=\omega\,{B}_{\mu\nu}^{abc}(0,r,-r)\,,\qquad\omega:=i\,{\widetilde{\omega}}\,.

(8.13)

Evidently, the term in Eq. (
8.13
) may be carried to the l.h.s of Eq. (
8.5
), and combine directly
with the term
B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, in the same kinematic limit.
In particular,
after introducing the
new variable,

\tau:=1-\omega\,,

(8.14)

Eq. (
8.5
) becomes

\tau\,{B}^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=\int_{k}B^{axe}_{\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\Delta_{xm}^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta_{en}^{\beta\sigma}(k){\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,k,r,-r)\,.

(8.15)

It is clear now that, because of the validity of Eq. (
7.13
), Eq. (
8.15
) yields to lowest order a trivial result (
0=0
). Therefore, in order
to obtain nontrivial
dynamical information from Eq. (
8.15
), one must equate the terms linear in
q
on both of its sides.
In particular,
using Eq. (
7.14
) for the
B_{\mu\nu}(0,r-r)
and

B_{\alpha\beta}(0,k-k)
,
one obtains
the equationEq. (
8.15
)

{\cal B}^{abc}_{\lambda\mu\nu}(0,r,-r)=\tau^{-1}\int_{k}{\cal B}^{axe}_{\lambda\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\Delta_{xm}^{\alpha\rho}(k)\Delta_{en}^{\beta\sigma}(k){\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,k,r,-r)\,,

(8.16)

which admits the diagrammatic representation given in the second line of Fig.
8.1
.
Note that,
in passing from Eq. (
8.15
) to
Eq. (
8.16
),
we have assumed that
\tau\neq 0
; of course, if

\tau=0
, the l.h.s.
of Eq. (
8.15
) vanishes, and the remaining equation is no longer of the BSE type.

We now contract both sides by

P_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r)P_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(r)

and employ Eq. (
7.17
), to get

f^{abc}\,\mathbb{B}(r^{2})\,r_{\lambda}P_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(r)\,=-\tau^{-1}f^{amn}\int_{k}\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\,\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\,k_{\lambda}P^{\sigma\rho}(k)\,{\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,k,r,-r)P_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mu}(r)P_{\nu^{\prime}}^{\nu}(r)\,.

(8.17)

Then, contracting both sides by

f^{abc}\,r^{\lambda}g^{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}
, and using that

P_{\mu}^{\mu}(r)=3
,
we arrive at the
final BSE
for the central quantity
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, namely

\mathbb{B}(r^{2})=\tau^{-1}\alpha_{s}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2}){K}(r,k)\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\,;

(8.18)

note that
K(r,k)

is
precisely
the kernel
introduced in Eq. (
7.33
).

Our final important
observation regarding
Eq. (
8.18
) is that it
is RGI.
This property may be
easily established,
by observing that,
due to the first relation in Eq. (
7.45
),

{\widetilde{\omega}}
itself, given by Eq. (
8.12
),
is RGI, and so is the
\tau
in
Eq. (
8.14
),
i.e.
,

\omega=\omega_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,,\qquad\tau=\tau_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\,.

(8.19)

Then, the BSE in Eq. (
8.18
)
is RGI by virtue of the third relation in Eq. (
7.45
),
and the fact that the

Z_{B}
, introduced to renormalize
the
\mathbb{B}
,
cancels on both sides.
Consequently, one may
substitute into Eq. (
8.18
) directly bare for renormalized quantities, without
any renormalization constants
appearing in the final expression, to wit

\mathbb{B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(r^{2})=\tau_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}^{-1}\alpha_{s}^{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}^{2}(k^{2}){K}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(r,k)\mathbb{B}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(k^{2})\,.

(8.20)

As before, the indices “R” will be
omitted in what follows.

Figure 8.1:
BSE for the effective vertex
B^{abc}_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
(left), and its
q=0
limit (right).

8.3  Dynamical scale-fixing of the BSE solutions

In order to proceed further with our analysis, certain key equations, derived
in Minkowski space,
must be converted
to Euclidean space.
This conversion is accomplished
following the set of rules given in the App.
A
;
applying them to Eqs. (
7.48
), (
8.12
), and (
8.13
), the expressions for
\omega_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
and
I_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
read

\displaystyle\omega_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{3C_{\rm A}}{2}\int_{k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}\Delta^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\mathbb{B}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,

(8.21)

\displaystyle I_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{3C_{\rm A}Z_{3}}{2}\int_{k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}\Delta^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\mathbb{B}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,.

(8.22)

The Euclidean versions of
Eqs. (
7.49
) and (
8.20
) may be derived
in a similar way. In doing so,
note that the kernel
K

must be cast into the form

K(r,k)=iK_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}},k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}})\,,

(8.23)

where

i\,\alpha_{s}K_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}},k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}):=\frac{(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}\cdot k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}})}{c\,r^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}k^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}f^{abc}f^{amn}\left[P^{\mu\nu}(r)P^{\rho\sigma}(k)\,{\cal K}^{mnbc}_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-k,k,r,-r)\right]_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}\,,

(8.24)

and
\left[\ldots\right]_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
denotes the transformation of the expressions in square brackets to Euclidean space.
The origin of the factor “
i
”
in Eq. (
8.24
)
is easily understood at the level of the one-gluon exchange diagram
(b_{1})

in Fig.
7.9
, being included in the definition of the gluon propagator, see item (
i
) of Subsec.
2.2
.

Then, with
\tau_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}:=1-\omega_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
, we have

\mathbb{B}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\tau_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{-1}\alpha_{s}\int_{k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}\Delta_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}){K}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}},k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}})\mathbb{B}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,

(8.25)

whereas the equation for
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r)
becomes

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{\!{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\,Z_{3}+\alpha_{s}\!\int_{k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}\Delta_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,{K}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}},k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}})\,\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{\!{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}(k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,.

(8.26)

Finally, the expressions for the Euclidean displacement function and mass retain the same form as in Eqs. (
7.9
) and (
7.22
), but now carry indices “E”,
i.e.
,

{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}):=-I_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}\,\mathbb{B}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,

(8.27)

and

m^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}=g^{2}I^{2}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}\,.

(8.28)

In order to simplify the
notation, in what follows the index “E”
will be dropped.

Let us now focus on
the BSE of Eq. (
8.25
).
Strictly speaking, it
is a non-linear
integral equation, due to the quadratic
dependence of
\tau
on the
unknown function
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
.
Nonetheless, the fact that
\tau
is a constant, independent of the variable
r
, allows one to convert the BSE into an eigenvalue problem, as is
typical for linear homogeneous equations. There is, however, a crucial, and very welcome difference:
the solutions do not suffer from a scale indeterminacy, as happens in the case of linear equations, where the multiplication of a given solution by an arbitrary number is also a solution.
The reason for this
decisive difference is
precisely the presence of the parameter
\tau
,
which fixes the scale, up to an overall sign.

In order to fully
appreciate the function of
\tau
in this context,
we temporarily set
\tau=1

inside Eq. (
8.25
). Then, suppose that
the kernel
K
is such that
the eigenvalue that yields
a nontrivial
solution for
\mathbb{B}(k^{2})
requires
that
\alpha_{s}\to\alpha_{\star}
, where

\alpha_{\star}
differs from the value predicted for

\alpha_{s}
within the renormalization scheme employed.

Let us now restore
\tau
at the level of Eq. (
8.25
); it is then clear that
\tau
has to compensate exactly
for the difference between
\alpha_{s}
and
\alpha_{\star}
.
Specifically, one must have

\tau=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\alpha_{\star}}\,,

(8.29)

which converts Eq. (
8.25
) into the familiar form

\mathbb{B}(r^{2})=\alpha_{\star}\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2}){K}(r,k)\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\,.

(8.30)

To understand how the presence of the parameter

\tau
in Eq. (
8.30
)
leads to the determination
of the scale of
\mathbb{B}(k^{2})
,
note that, by combining Eqs. (
8.14
) and (
8.29
),
we obtain the condition

\omega=1-\alpha_{s}/\alpha_{\star}\,.

(8.31)

Since the value of
the l.h.s. of Eq. (
8.31
)
is fixed, the parameter

\omega

is completely determined.
Therefore,
the size (scale) of the
\mathbb{B}(k^{2})

entering in the definition of
Eq. (
8.21
) is constrained:
it has to be chosen such that both sides
of Eq. (
8.21
) become equal.

To see how this works, let
\mathbb{B}_{0}(k^{2})
represent a solution of Eq. (
8.20
) with a scale set arbitrarily,
e.g.
, by imposing that the global maximum of
\mathbb{B}_{0}(k^{2})
is at
1
.
In addition, denote by
\omega_{0}

the value of
\omega
when
\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\to\mathbb{B}_{0}(k^{2})
is substituted in Eq. (
8.21
).
The

\mathbb{B}
that is compatible with Eq. (
8.31
)
is related to the
\mathbb{B}_{0}
by a multiplicative constant,
\sigma
,
i.e.
,
\mathbb{B}(k^{2})=\sigma\,\mathbb{B}_{0}(k^{2})
,
whose value is determined from the equation

\sigma^{2}\omega_{0}=1-\alpha_{s}/\alpha_{\star}=\omega\,,

(8.32)

or, equivalently,

\sigma=\pm\sqrt{\frac{1-\alpha_{s}/\alpha_{\star}}{{\omega}_{0}}}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{\omega}{{\omega}_{0}}}\,.

(8.33)

Note that, since
{\omega}
is quadratic in
\mathbb{B}(k^{2})
, the sign of
\sigma
, and hence the sign of
\mathbb{B}
itself, is left undetermined. However, as we can see from Eqs. (
8.22
) and (
8.27
),

{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})
is also quadratic in
\mathbb{B}(k^{2})
, and therefore
does not get affected by the sign ambiguity of Eq. (
8.33
). In fact, the overall sign of
{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})

turns out to be negative for the entire range of Euclidean momenta, in agreement with the sign found in the lattice extraction of
{\mathbb{C}}(k^{2})

presented in
[
142
,
144
]

[see also comments below
Eq. (
5.40
), item (
{\it i}
)].

8.4  An exceptional cancellation

Let us assume that
the BS amplitude
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})

has become available by solving
the BSE in Eq. (
8.30
),
and one would like to proceed
with the determination of
the gluon mass scale,
by employing
Eq. (
8.28
) in conjunction with
Eq. (
8.22
). At that point,
one is faced with the typical difficulty
associated with the implementation of multiplicative renormalization:
on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
8.22
)
the integral is multiplied
by the renormalization constant
Z_{3}
.

It turns out
that a subtle set of circumstances
allows one to implement the
multiplicative renormalization
at the level of Eq. (
8.22
)
exactly. The final result
amounts to
the
effective

replacement
Z_{3}\to\omega\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})
,
i.e.
,

I=\frac{3C_{\rm A}}{2}\omega\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\,.

(8.34)

In what follows we will present a detailed
derivation of the above important
result.

The first main observation is that, thanks to Eq. (
7.49
), the
Z_{3}
in Eq. (
7.48
) may be substituted by the combination

Z_{3}=\,\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})-\alpha_{s}\!\!\int_{\ell}\ell^{2}\Delta^{2}(\ell^{2})\,{K}(k,\ell)\,\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(\ell^{2})\,.

(8.35)

This procedure is typically employed
when dealing with multiplicative renormalizability at the level of the SDEs, see,
e.g.
,
[
273
]
,
and is intimately connected with the “skeleton expansion” of the SDE kernel
[
273
,
106
]
.

The second observation is specific to the particular form of the BSE in Eq. (
8.25
):
when the substitution of Eq. (
8.35
)
is carried out,
the BSE of Eq. (
8.25
) is formed inside
Eq. (
8.22
),
leading to a crucial
cancellation, and, finally, to
Eq. (
8.34
).

The essence of this cancellation may be captured
by means of a simple toy example. In particular, consider two functions,
g(x)
and
f(x)
,
satisfying the system of integral equations

g(x)=z+\int\!\!dy\,\tilde{g}(y)K(x,y)\,,\qquad\qquad f(x)=b^{-1}\!\int\!\!dy\,\tilde{f}(y)K(x,y)\,,

(8.36)

where
\tilde{g}(y):=g(y)\,u(y)
and

\tilde{f}(y):=f(y)\,u(y)
, with
u(y)
a
well-behaved function. The parameters

z
and
b
are real numbers, with

b\neq 0,1
, and the limits of integration are
arbitrary. In addition,
the
common
kernel
K(x,y)

satisfies
the symmetry relation
K(x,y)=K(y,x)
. Moreover, let us
further assume that the
value of a constant
\beta
is given by the integral

\beta=z\!\int\!\!dx\,\tilde{f}(x)\,.

(8.37)

Observe now that
the dependence of
\beta
on the parameter

z
may be eliminated in favor of
the function
g(x)
by
resorting
to the system of Eq. (
8.36
). Specifically, one
employs the following sequence of steps

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\!\!dx\,z\,\tilde{f}(x)

(8.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\!\!dx\left[g(x)-\int\!\!dy\,\tilde{g}(y)K(x,y)\right]\tilde{f}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\!\!dx\,g(x)\tilde{f}(x)-\int\!\!dx\!\!\int\!\!dy\,\tilde{g}(y)K(x,y)\tilde{f}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\!\!dx\,\tilde{g}(x)\Bigg[f(x)-\underbrace{\int\!\!dy\,\tilde{f}(y)K(x,y)}_{bf(x)}\Bigg]\,,

where in the second line we used the
first relation in Eq. (
8.36
), while in the last line the
relabelling
x\leftrightarrow y

of the integration variables
was implemented, the symmetry
of the kernel
K(x,y)
was exploited,
and the second relation in
Eq. (
8.36
) was used.
Thus, we find

\beta=(1-b)\int\!\!dx\tilde{f}(x)g(x)\,,

(8.39)

where we used that

\tilde{g}(x)f(x)=\tilde{f}(x)g(x)
.
Clearly, the dependence
of
\beta
on the
parameter
z

has been exchanged in favor of
the dependence
on the function

g(x)
, which was absent from the
original integral
in Eq. (
8.37
).
For a concrete
example, where the
equivalence between
Eqs. (
8.37
) and (
8.39
)
has been worked out explicitly, the reader is
referred to the
Appendix A of
[
100
]
.

Figure 8.2:
Diagrammatic illustration of the renormalization of
I
. Note that the diagrams containing the kernel
{\cal K}
undergo a relabeling of integration momenta,
k\leftrightarrow\ell
, from the second to the third line.

It is now rather
straightforward to repeat the
construction leading to
Eq. (
8.39
) for the system of integral equations
given by Eqs. (
8.25
) and (
8.26
), in conjunction with Eq. (
8.22
); indeed,
all we need is to establish the correspondence

\displaystyle\{g(x),\,f(x),\,u(x),\,K(x,y)\}\,

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\,\{\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2}),\,\mathbb{B}(r^{2}),\,k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2}),\,\alpha_{s}K(r,k)\}\,,\,\,

\displaystyle\{\beta,\,z,\,b,\,dx,\,dy\}\,

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\,\{2I/3C_{\rm A},\,Z_{3},\,\tau,\,d^{4}k,\,d^{4}\ell\}\,.

(8.40)

In fact, with the above identifications
and Eq. (
8.14
), we find that
the analogue of Eq. (
8.39
)
is precisely Eq. (
8.34
), which is
the announced result. The diagrammatic representation of the
steps described in Eq. (
8.38
)
is shown in Fig.
8.2
;
note that, in doing so, we employ
the representation of the BSE
given in Fig.
8.1
,
whose main building block
was introduced in Fig.
7.6
.

The above construction exposes a
remarkable fact: if the parameter

\omega
is set to zero, the cancellation
described in Eq. (
8.38
)
[Fig.
8.2
]
is perfect. Therefore,
even if a non-trivial

\mathbb{B}
is obtained from Eq. (
8.25
), the
renormalized transition amplitude
I
, and with it the gluon mass
m
, vanish.
In that sense, the gluon mass
emerges thanks to the

mismatch
between the kernels in the equations
for
\mathbb{B}
and

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
, [see Eqs. (
8.25
) and (
8.26
)], which is
caused by the
presence of a
t\neq 1

in the former but not in the latter. In diagrammatic terms,
the nonvanishing of the gluon mass becomes possible due to the
difference between the

{\cal T}
appearing in
Fig.
8.1
and the
{\cal K}

in Fig.
7.8
, namely
the component

{\cal M}
[see Fig.
7.2
],
which
carries the vital information about the formation of the Schwinger pole.

8.5  Gluon mass versus Fredholm alternative theorem

It turns out that
the cancellations
described in Eq. (
8.38
) are
not accidental,
but
are rather
enforced by an
underlying
mathematical principle.
In particular,
as we discuss in detail in this subsection, we
have unveiled a rather subtle
application of
the so-called “Fredholm
alternative theorem”.

Consider the inhomogeneous and homogeneous Fredholm equations of the second kind, given by
[
145
,
146
]

f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)+\lambda\int_{a}^{b}K(x,y)f_{1}(y)dy\,,

(8.41)

and

f_{3}(x)=\lambda\int_{a}^{b}K(x,y)f_{3}(y)dy\,,

(8.42)

respectively;
f_{1}
and
f_{3}
are the unknown functions, while
f_{2}
is a known inhomogeneous term. The kernel,
K(x,y)
, is assumed to be continuous and square-integrable in the interval
[a,b]
, in which case it is denominated a “Hilbert-Schmidt integral operator”.

The Fredholm alternative theorem imposes restrictions on the existence of simultaneous solutions for Eqs. (
8.41
) and (
8.42
).
It is sufficient
for our purposes
to consider the special case of the theorem when
K(x,y)
is real and symmetric in
x\leftrightarrow y
; for the generalization to complex and non-symmetric kernels see,
e.g.
,
[
145
,
146
]
.
Under these simplifications, the Fredholm alternative theorem may be stated as follows
[
146
]
:

(
a
)

If
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue of
K(x,y)
,
i.e.
, if the homogeneous equation has only the trivial solution,
f_{3}(x)=0
, then the inhomogeneous Eq. (
8.41
) has a solution for
any
nonzero
f_{2}(x)
.

(
b
)

If
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
K(x,y)
, such that
f_{3}(x)
is nonvanishing, then Eq. (
8.41
) has solutions
if and only if

\int_{a}^{b}f_{2}(y)f_{3}(y)dy=0\,.

(8.43)

In order to expose
the connection between the Fredholm alternative theorem and the system of equations satisfied by
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
and
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
, we need to perform certain transformations that will cast Eqs. (
8.25
) and (
8.26
) into a form similar to Eqs. (
8.41
) and (
8.42
).

The first step is to
rewrite Eqs. (
8.25
) and (
8.26
) in hyperspherical coordinates, using the variables introduced in Eq. (
A.5
). Note that the kernel
K(r,k)
is a function of
x
,
y
, and
\theta
,
i.e.
,
K(r,k)\equiv K(x,y,\theta)
.
Furthermore, we use the integral measure of Eq. (
A.6
), which introduces an ultraviolet regulator to control potential divergences of the integrals. For the sake
of simplicity
we employ a hard
momentum cutoff,
\Lambda
; we have confirmed that exactly the same conclusions are reached when the calculation is carried out using
dimensional regularization. So, the integral measure takes the form

\int_{k}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,y\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!d\theta\,s_{\theta}^{2}\,.

(8.44)

Now, the only dependence of the integrands of Eqs. (
8.25
) and (
8.26
) on the angle is through
K(x,y,\theta)
. Then, we can define an “angle-integrated kernel”,
{\widehat{K}}(x,y)
, by

{\widehat{K}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\!\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!d\theta\,s_{\theta}^{2}\,K(x,y,\theta)\,.

(8.45)

With the above definitions, Eqs. (
8.25
) and (
8.26
) are recast as

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}(x)=

\displaystyle\,\tau^{-1}\alpha_{s}\!\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,{\cal Z}^{2}(y){\widehat{K}}(x,y)\mathbb{B}(y)\,,

\displaystyle\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(x)=

\displaystyle\,Z_{3}+\alpha_{s}\!\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,{\cal Z}^{2}(y){\widehat{K}}(x,y)\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(y)\,,

(8.46)

where we introduced the gluon dressing function,
{\cal Z}(x):=x\Delta(x)
,
first defined in Eq. (
2.23
).

We next note that, since
K(r,k)
is symmetric under the exchange of
r\leftrightarrow k
, then
{\widehat{K}}(x,y)={\widehat{K}}(y,x)
. However, the complete kernel of Eq. (
8.46
), namely
{\cal Z}^{2}(y){\widehat{K}}(x,y)
, is not symmetric under
x\leftrightarrow y
, due to the factor of
{\cal Z}^{2}(y)
. Nonetheless, we can easily transform it into an equivalent system of equations with a symmetric kernel, by
multiplying Eq. (
8.46
) by
{\cal Z}(x)
, and defining

{\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}}(x):={\cal Z}(x)\mathbb{B}(x)\,,\qquad{\widetilde{L}}_{sg}(x):={\cal Z}(x)\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(x)\,,\qquad{\widetilde{K}}(x,y):={\cal Z}(x){\cal Z}(y){\widehat{K}}(x,y)\,.

(8.47)

Then, Eq. (
8.46
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}}(x)=

\displaystyle\,\tau^{-1}\alpha_{s}\!\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,{\widetilde{K}}(x,y){\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}}(y)\,,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{L}}_{sg}(x)=

\displaystyle\,Z_{3}{\cal Z}(x)+\alpha_{s}\!\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,{\widetilde{K}}(x,y){\widetilde{L}}_{sg}(y)\,,

(8.48)

whose kernel
{\widetilde{K}}(x,y)
is indeed symmetric.

Finally, Eq. (
8.22
) for
I
may be re-expressed as

I=\frac{3C_{\rm A}Z_{3}}{32\pi^{2}}\!\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,{\cal Z}(y)\,{\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}}(y)\,.

(8.49)

We are now in position to explore the implications of the Fredholm alternative theorem for Eq. (
8.48
). Specifically, suppose that
\omega=0
, such that
\tau=1
. Then, we have a direct correspondence between Eq. (
8.48
) and the Eqs. (
8.41
) and (
8.42
) through the identification

\displaystyle\{f_{1}(x),\,f_{2}(x),\,f_{3}(x),\,K(x,y)\}\,

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\,\{{\widetilde{L}}_{sg}(x),\,Z_{3}{\cal Z}(x),\,{\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}}(x),\,{\widetilde{K}}(x,y)\}\,,

\displaystyle\{\lambda,\,a,\,b\}\,

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\,\{\alpha_{s},\,0,\,{\Lambda^{2}}\}\,.

(8.50)

Hence, for both
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(x)
and
\mathbb{B}(x)
to be nonzero, the Fredholm alternative theorem implies that

Z_{3}\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,{\cal Z}(y)\,{\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}}(y)=0\,.

(8.51)

Comparison to Eq. (
8.49
) then yields
I=0
, and therefore,
due to Eq. (
7.9
),

m^{2}=0
.

We thus reach the
conclusion that the
generation of a gluon mass scale through the Schwinger mechanism
hinges on
\omega\neq 0
,
\tau\neq 1
, which leads to the
evasion
of
Fredholm alternative theorem, by relaxing the equality of kernels
in Eq. (
8.48
).
Instead, if
\omega=0
,
the theorem imposes the vanishing of the gluon mass, even in the presence
of a
nonvanishing
\mathbb{B}
; this
happens
because, quite remarkably, the
condition of Eq. (
8.43
)
is
precisely
the
equation for the transition amplitude
I
.

We finally address a subtlety related to the applicability
of the Fredholm alternative theorem in the present situation.
Note, in particular,
that the kernel
K(r,k)
depends on
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
, through every
three-gluon appearing in the
defining diagrams of
Fig.
7.9
; in fact, the

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}
enters in
K(r,k)
in
such a way that the symmetry
under
r\longleftrightarrow k

is preserved. As a result,
the integral equation for

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
[second in Eq. (
8.46
)]
is nonlinear
(even when
\omega=0
),
while the Fredholm alternative theorem
applies to a system of
linear
equations.
However, our main
conclusion, namely that for
\omega=0
the gluon mass scale vanishes, persists.

Indeed, suppose that there exists a solution,
L_{0}(r^{2})
and
\mathbb{B}_{0}(r^{2})
, to the full nonlinear system of equations, and let
K_{0}(r,k)
be the value of
K(r,k)
obtained by the substitution of
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}\to L_{0}
in its
expression.
Now,
L_{0}(r^{2})
and
\mathbb{B}_{0}(r^{2})
must also be a solution of

\displaystyle\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})=

\displaystyle\,Z_{3}+\alpha_{s}\!\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\,{K}_{0}(r,k)\,\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(k^{2})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}(r^{2})=

\displaystyle\,\tau^{-1}\alpha_{s}\!\int_{k}k^{2}\Delta^{2}(k^{2})\,{K}_{0}(r,k)\,\mathbb{B}(k^{2})\,,

(8.52)

since setting
\{\mathit{L}_{{sg}},\,\mathbb{B}\}\to\{L_{0},\,\mathbb{B}_{0}\}
in the above equation one recovers the original, nonlinear, system. But the Fredholm alternative theorem applies to Eq. (
8.52
) with
\omega=0
, in which case the arguments of Subsec.
8.5
lead to
m=0
. Consequently, the nonlinear nature of the equations cannot by itself evade the Fredholm alternative theorem when
\omega=0
, and the conclusion of our analysis is unaffected.

8.6  Numerics

The detailed analysis presented in the previous subsections has led us to a set of dynamical equations,
whose solutions will determine the value of the
gluon mass scale that emerges from this approach.
In this subsection we
culminate this exploration
by determining
m
,
as well as the
shape and size of the
displacement function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
,
following the procedure introduced in the recent work of
[
100
]
.
In doing so, we use two
important results as
benchmarks for these quantities.
Specifically, we identify as the optimal value for the gluon mass the inverse of the saturation point of the lattice gluon propagator at the origin; when the lattice curve has been renormalized such that
\Delta^{-1}(\mu^{2})=\mu^{2}
at
\mu=4.3
GeV
, one finds
m_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle lat}}}=354
MeV
[
23
]
. Similarly, the curve shown on the right panel of Fig.
6.3
is used as benchmark for the displacement function; in this subsection we will denote this curve by
{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})
, where the subscript “WI” refers to the WI-based derivation of this result.

The procedure that we adopt
for the computation of
m

and
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
may be summarized as follows:

(
{\it i}
)
The relations given by Eqs. (
8.30
), (
8.21
) and (
8.34
) are written in terms of
hyperspherical coordinates, employing
the variables defined in Eq. (
A.5
).
In particular,

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau^{-1}\frac{\alpha_{s}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!dy\,\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!d\theta\,s_{\theta}^{2}\,{\cal Z}^{2}(y)K(x,y,\theta)\,\mathbb{B}(y)\,,

(8.53)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{3C_{\rm A}}{32\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!dy{\cal Z}^{2}(y)\,\mathbb{B}^{2}(y)\,,

(8.54)

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=
