\displaystyle\frac{3C_{\rm A}}{32\pi^{2}}\omega\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!dy\,{\cal Z}^{2}(y)\,\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(y)\,\mathbb{B}(y)\,.

(8.55)

(
{\it ii}
)
For a given kernel,
K(x,y,\theta)
, the eigenvalue problem of Eq. (
8.53
) can be solved with standard procedures, such as
Nyström
’s method
[
277
]
. This determines the eigenvalue,
\alpha_{\star}
, and
a solution,
\mathbb{B}_{0}(x)
, arbitrarily normalized such that its global maximum is
1
. Then,
we use
Eq. (
8.32
)
to obtain
the value of
\omega

corresponding to this

\alpha_{\star}
.

(
{\it iii}
) Next, we
substitute

\mathbb{B}_{0}(x)

into Eq. (
8.54
),
thus obtaining
the value of
\omega_{0}
.
So,
the physical scale of
\mathbb{B}(x)

is fixed by determining
the value of the constant

\sigma
from Eq. (
8.33
).

(
{\it iv}
)
We may now determine the value of
I
,
by substituting
into Eq. (
8.55
)
the
\mathbb{B}(x)
obtained in
the previous step.

(
{\it v}
)
Finally, with the
I
determined in
(
{\it iv}
),
we can get

m
and
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
using
Eqs. (
8.28
) and (
8.27
), respectively.

The initial form that we will use
for the four-gluon kernel

K(r,k)
entering in the BSE of Eq. (
8.25
)
is its one-gluon exchange
approximation,

K_{\rm oge}(r,k)
,
given by diagram

(b_{1})
of Fig.
7.9
;
we remind the reader
that diagram
(b_{2})

vanishes when inserted in Eq. (
8.24
)
[
96
,
140
]
.

In the Landau gauge,
we obtain
from diagram
(b_{1})
and Eq. (
8.24
)

K_{\rm oge}(r,k)=\frac{2\pi C_{\rm A}}{3}\Delta(u^{2})\left[\frac{(r\cdot k)}{r^{2}k^{2}}\overline{\Gamma}_{\mu\rho\sigma}(r,-k,u)\overline{\Gamma}^{\mu\rho\sigma}(-r,k,u)\right]_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}\,,

(8.56)

where
u:=k-r
, and

\overline{\Gamma}_{\mu\rho\sigma}(r,-k,u):=P^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}(r)P^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(k)P^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(u){\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}(r,-k,u)\,,

(8.57)

is the transversely projected three-gluon vertex
[
212
,
211
,
278
,
116
,
117
,
188
,
250
,
214
,
99
]
.

The
\overline{\Gamma}_{\mu\rho\sigma}
appearing in Eq. (
8.56
) is described in terms of four independent tensor structures and the corresponding form factors, which depend on three kinematic variables. However, as has been shown in numerous studies
[
212
,
211
,
278
,
116
,
117
,
188
,
279
,
250
,
214
,
99
,
251
]
, the classical tensor structure of
\overline{\Gamma}_{\mu\rho\sigma}
is dominant. Moreover, the associated form factor,
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}
, may be accurately described as a function of a single kinematic variable, denoted by
s^{2}
, namely

\overline{\Gamma}^{\mu\rho\sigma}(r,-k,u)=\overline{\Gamma}_{\!0}^{\mu\rho\sigma}(r,-k,u)\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(s^{2})\,,\qquad s^{2}:=\frac{1}{2}[r^{2}+k^{2}+u^{2}]\,,

(8.58)

where
\overline{\Gamma}_{\!0}^{\mu\rho\sigma}
is the tree-level value of
\overline{\Gamma}^{\mu\rho\sigma}
, obtained by substituting the
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma^{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}
in Eq. (
8.57
) by the
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\!0}^{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}
of Eq. (
2.26
).
This special property of the three-gluon vertex is known in the literature as
“
planar degeneracy
”
[
250
]
.

Then, using Eq. (
8.58
) and the variables of Eq. (
A.5
), one obtains

K_{\rm oge}(x,y,\theta)=K_{\rm{kin}}(x,y,\theta){\cal R}_{\rm oge}(u^{2},s^{2})\,,

(8.59)

where we note that
u^{2}=x+y-2c_{\theta}\sqrt{xy}
,
s^{2}=x+y-c_{\theta}\sqrt{xy}
,

K_{\rm{kin}}(x,y,\theta):=\frac{8\pi C_{\rm A}}{3u^{2}\sqrt{xy}}\left\{c_{\theta}s_{\theta}^{2}\left[(c_{\theta}^{2}+8)xy-6c_{\theta}\sqrt{xy}(x+y)+3(x^{2}+y^{2})\right]\right\}\,,

(8.60)

and

{\cal R}_{\rm oge}(u^{2},s^{2}):={\Delta(u^{2})\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{2}(s^{2})}\,.

(8.61)

The ingredients entering in the
K_{\rm oge}(x,y,\theta)
of Eq. (
8.59
) are all accurately known from lattice simulations. In particular, we use for
\Delta(u^{2})
and
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(s^{2})
the fits
to the lattice results of
[
23
]
, given
by Eqs. (C.11) and (C.12) of
[
142
]
, respectively;
the corresponding curves are shown
in Fig.
6.2
.

\Delta(u^{2})
is also shown on the
upper left panel of Fig.
8.3
, for the purpose of comparison
with the modified version,

\Delta^{\prime}(u^{2})
,
introduced later in Eq. (
8.66
).

Note that these ingredients are renormalized within the so-called “asymmetric MOM scheme”
[
184
,
185
,
186
,
187
,
180
,
188
,
23
]
, defined by the renormalization condition [see App.
B
]

\Delta^{-1}(\mu^{2})=\mu^{2}\,,\qquad\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(\mu^{2})=1\,,

(8.62)

with
\mu=4.3
GeV denoting the renormalization point. For this renormalization scheme and point, the corresponding strong charge takes the value
\alpha_{s}=0.27

[
187
]
, which we adopt from now on.

On the upper right panel of Fig.
8.3
we show
as an orange dashed curve
the diagonal slice of the
angle-integrated

{\widehat{K}}(x,y)
[see Eq. (
8.45
)], namely

{\widehat{K}}(k^{2},k^{2})
.

Substituting into Eq. (
8.53
) the

K_{\rm oge}(x,y,\theta)
given by
Eqs. (
8.59
), (
8.60
) and (
8.61
), we
find that
\alpha_{\star}=0.685
;
the corresponding BS amplitude,
to be denoted by

\mathbb{B}_{{\rm oge}}(r^{2})
, is
shown as the orange dashed curve
on the lower left panel of Fig.
8.3
.
Note that its asymptotic ultraviolet behavior is such that the
integrals in Eqs. (
8.54
) and (
8.55
) are perfectly convergent.
In particular, we find that the numerical solution for
\mathbb{B}_{{\rm oge}}(r^{2})
may be accurately approximated by the form

\mathbb{B}_{{\rm oge}}(r^{2})=\frac{a}{r^{2}L_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle UV}}}^{\kappa}(r^{2})}\,,\qquad r>5~{\rm GeV}\,,

(8.63)

where
L_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle UV}}}(r^{2})

is the function introduced in Eq. (
5.42
), while

a=3.93
and
\kappa=0.958

are determined by fitting the data of
\mathbb{B}_{{\rm oge}}(r^{2})
for
r>5~{\rm GeV}
.

Then, following the steps
(
{\it ii}
)-(
{\it iv}
),
we obtain the gluon mass scale,
whose value we denote by
m_{{\rm oge}}
;
in particular we find
m_{{\rm oge}}=1.27
GeV.
Similarly, for the displacement functions, to be denoted by
{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm oge}}(r^{2})
, we find the orange dashed curve shown on the lower right panel of
Fig.
8.3
. Evidently,
both outcomes differ substantially from the benchmark results of
m_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle lat}}}=354
MeV and the
curve corresponding to
{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})
.

Figure 8.3:
Upper left:

The effective gluon propagator, corresponding to
the two choices used in our analysis, namely
\Delta(u^{2})

(orange dashed)
and
\Delta^{\prime}(u^{2})
(blue continuous);
the inset shows the associated dressing functions.
Upper right:
The diagonal slice of the angle-integrated kernel, defined by Eq. (
8.45
); the orange dashed curve corresponds to

{\widehat{K}}_{\rm{oge}}(k^{2},k^{2})
,
while the blue continuous to

{\widehat{K}}^{\prime}(k^{2},k^{2})
.

Lower left:

The absolute value of the BS
amplitude; the orange dashed
curve represents the
|\mathbb{B}_{\rm{oge}}(r^{2})|
,
while the blue continuous curve
denotes the |
\mathbb{B}^{\prime}(r^{2})|
. The black dot-dashed and gray dashed lines represent the corresponding asymptotes, given by Eq. (
8.63
).

Lower right:

Displacement amplitude; the orange dashed curve corresponds to

{\mathbb{C}}_{\rm oge}(r^{2})
, while the
blue continuous denotes the

{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})
.
The black curve surrounded by the green band is displayed for comparison; it corresponds to
the WI-derived result of Subsec.
6.3

[see right panel of Fig.
6.3
], denoted here by

{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})
.

This discrepancy motivates the modification of the kernel
K(x,y,\theta)
, for the purpose of
improving the results; evidently, the underlying idea is to effectively model contributions not captured by the one-gluon exchange approximation.
To that end, we implement the substitution

K(x,y,\theta)\to K^{\prime}(x,y,\theta)=K_{\rm{kin}}(x,y,\theta){\cal R}^{\prime}(u^{2},s^{2})\,,

(8.64)

where the function
{\cal R}^{\prime}(u^{2},s^{2})
is given by

{\cal R}^{\prime}(u^{2},s^{2})=\Delta^{\prime}(u^{2})\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(s^{2})\,,

(8.65)

with

\Delta^{\prime}(u^{2})
parametrized
as

\Delta^{\prime}(u^{2})=\Delta(u^{2})\times\left[1+\frac{c_{0}u^{2}}{1+c_{1}u^{2}+c_{2}u^{4}}\right]\,.

(8.66)

Note that

K_{\rm oge}
is recovered
by setting
c_{0}=0
in Eq. (
8.66
).
Moreover, at large momenta,
\Delta^{\prime}(u^{2})

reduces to
\Delta(u^{2})
,
such that the

K^{\prime}

so constructed reduces
asymptotically
to
K_{\rm oge}
.

We next vary the

c_{i}
in
Eq. (
8.66
)
within certain intervals, and
consider the resulting
values for
m
.
Our analysis reveals
that the “optimal”
set of values is given by

c_{0}=0.503
GeV
-2
,

c_{1}=0.00667
GeV
-2
and
c_{2}=0.0486
GeV
-4
.
The
\Delta^{\prime}(u^{2})

and
\widehat{K}^{\prime}(k^{2},k^{2})

obtained using this
set of
c_{i}
are shown as blue continuous curves on the upper left
and upper right panels of Fig.
8.3
, respectively.
Substituting the

K^{\prime}(x,y,\theta)
into
the BSE of Eq. (
8.53
), we find that

\alpha_{\star}=0.414
. The corresponding solution,
\mathbb{B}^{\prime}(r^{2})
, is shown
as the blue continuous curve on the lower left panel of Fig.
8.3
; its asymptotic form
is given by Eq. (
8.63
), with
\mathbb{B}_{{\rm oge}}\to\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
, and parameters given by

a=3.94
, and
\kappa=2.66
.
The repetition of the steps
(
{\it ii}
)-(
{\it iv}
)
furnishes for the gluon mass scale the value

m^{\prime}=367
MeV, which differs by only

3.6\%
from
m_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle lat}}}=354
MeV.
The corresponding displacement function
{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})
is
shown as the continuous blue curve on the lower right panel of Fig.
8.3
.
Evidently,
the modification of the kernel implemented by
Eq. (
8.64
) has reduced
considerably the discrepancy between

{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})

and

{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})
.

At this point we remind the reader
that the determination of

{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})

in Subsec.
6.3

uses lattice inputs for
all ingredients
except
for
the partial derivative

{\cal W}(r^{2})
, defined in Eq. (
6.14
).
Indeed,
{\cal W}(r^{2})
was obtained from the SDE analysis of
App.
G
, using diagrams
(
h_{1}
) and (
h_{2}
) of Fig.
G.1
,
but omitting diagram (
h_{3}
), on the grounds of furnishing only a

\sim 2\%
effect at the level of the
ghost-gluon vertex, in the symmetric limit
[
280
]
.
However, with hindsight, the partial derivative of a
small form factor may be
considerable, especially if the
kinematic configuration
studied is not the symmetric one.

The above considerations prompt
us to determine the
shape that
{\cal W}(r^{2})
must have in order for
{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})

and
{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})

to coincide,

{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})={\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})
.
Denoting this ideal
{\cal W}(r^{2})

by
{\cal W}^{\,\prime}(r^{2})
,
it is clear that it can be obtained
from Eq. (
6.20
), after substituting in it

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})
,
namely

{\cal W}^{\,\prime}(r^{2})=r^{2}\Delta(r^{2})\left[\frac{\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})-{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})}{F(0)}-{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}\frac{d\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})}{dr^{2}}\right]\,;

(8.67)

the result is shown as a purple dashed curve and band on the left panel of Fig.
8.4
.

Now, given that the ingredients entering the diagrams
(h_{1})
and
(h_{2})
are firmly under control,
this change in the shape of
{\cal W}(r^{2})
can only be attributed to
the contribution of
diagram
(h_{3})
, namely

{\cal W}_{3}(r^{2})={\cal W}^{\,\prime}(r^{2})-{\cal W}_{1}(r^{2})-{\cal W}_{2}(r^{2})\,.

(8.68)

Then, from our results for the
{\cal W}_{1}
,
{\cal W}_{2}
and
{\cal W}^{\,\prime}
, we obtain the
{\cal W}_{3}(r^{2})
that is shown as a blue continuous curve on the right panel of Fig.
8.4
, where the
associated band originates from propagating the statistical error of
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
. Note that this
{\cal W}_{3}(r^{2})
corresponds to a maximum change of
14\%
of
{\cal W}(r^{2})
at
r=2
GeV.

Figure 8.4:
Left:

The

{\cal W}(r^{2})
obtained from the SDE analysis of
App.
G
, using diagrams
(
h_{1}
) and (
h_{2}
) of Fig.
G.1
(blue continuous),
and the
{\cal W}^{\,\prime}(r^{2})
(purple-dashed), obtained from
Eq. (
8.67
), enforcing

{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle WI}}}(r^{2})={\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(r^{2})
.

Right:
The contribution
{\cal W}_{3}(r^{2})

obtained from Eq. (
8.68
),
expected to arise from diagram
(h_{3})
. For each curve in this figure, the band corresponds to the error propagated from the lattice
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
.

9  Discussion and conclusions

The picture that emerges
from the implementation
of the Schwinger mechanism in QCD
is rather appealing,
especially in view of its ability to
expose
deep field-theoretic principles that are at work. In particular,
the tight interplay between
symmetry and dynamics
is revealed by the
multiple role played by the function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
, which is
({\it a})

the residue function of the Schwinger poles, see Eq. (
5.20
);

({\it b})

the displacement function of the Ward identity, see Eq. (
6.16
); and

({\it c})
proportional
to the BS amplitude
that describes the
formation of the massless excitations,
see Eq. (
8.27
).
The definitions
and terminology employed
throughout this article
for the description of

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

are summarized
for convenience in Table
9.1
.

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

Defining

equation

\displaystyle{\lim_{q\to 0}}\frac{V_{1}(q,r,p)}{q^{2}}=\frac{2(q\cdot r)}{q^{2}}{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})=L_{0}(r^{2})+{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})

{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})=-I\mathbb{B}(r^{2})

Terminology

Residue function

Displacement function

Proportional to the BS amplitude

Table 9.1:
Equivalent definitions of the function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
and the corresponding terminology.

In addition,
the combined treatment based on the
functional equations
(standard SDEs, SDEs within the PT-BFM framework, and the BSE for Schwinger pole formation)
proves particularly successful in describing the dynamics, and, most importantly, in
faithfully reflecting
the action of key mathematical principles,
such as the seagull cancellation [Sec.
3
] and
the Fredholm alternative theorem [Subsec.
8.5
].

It is also important to emphasize the fruitful synergy between continuous methods and
lattice QCD, demonstrated throughout this work.
In particular,
lattice-derived inputs, such as gluon propagators and vertices, have been
extensively used in our numerical analysis, both for the determination of the

m
and
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
in Subsec.
8.6
,
as well as the function
{\cal W}(r^{2})

in App.
G
.
In fact, this synergy reaches its culmination in
Subsec.
6.3
, where the lattice-based determination of
the displacement function
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
is carried out.

A particularly noteworthy feature
of our analysis is the exact
implementation of the multiplicative renormalization
of Eq. (
8.22
), described in
Subsec.
8.4
.
This becomes possible due to the precise cancellation captured by Eq. (
8.38
), and represents a rare case where such an exact result may be reached in a nonperturbative context. In fact, the operation of the Fredholm alternative theorem at this level, together with its subsequent
evasion thanks to the inclusion of the pole term proportional to

\omega
[see Subsec.
8.5
], is
exceptionally striking.

The aforementioned major cancellation of Subsec.
8.4

was diagrammatically described
with the aid of Fig.
8.2
.
In order to simplify the analysis,
we have considered the minimum
of diagrams required for
exposing
the gist of this cancellation,
and its connection to the Fredholm alternative theorem,
presented in Subsec.
8.5
.
It is important to stress, however,
that, as we have confirmed,
the omitted contributions undergo themselves
completely analogous cancellations,
proceeding in exactly the same way,
and for precisely the same reason.
In fact, we have confirmed that the construction
pertaining to the renormalization may be extended to include the ghost-gluon and four-gluon vertices,
the renormalization constants associated with them, and the corresponding SDE-BSE systems.
Quite interestingly, and in complete analogy to what we have seen, the only contributions that survive originate from the
nonlinear
components
of the additional BSEs that control the formation of
poles in the ghost-gluon and four-gluon vertices. Therefore, the generalization of
Eq. (
8.34
) will
involve additional terms
proportional to the

\omega
-like parameters
that will emerge exactly as in
Eqs. (
8.11
) and (
8.12
).

It should be emphasized that the analysis
of Sec.
8
is by no means exhaustive, and leaves considerable room for
improvement. (
{\it i}
) To begin with, in the
diagrammatic representation of
the four-gluon kernel
K(r,k)
one must include a graph
that exhibits the Schwinger pole, namely
(
b_{1}
) but
with the internal gluon replaced by
the scalar field
\Phi^{a}
.
This type of contribution was omitted from the
initial analysis, but its impact must be assessed in a more detailed analysis.
In fact, as was shown
in
[
96
]
[see Fig. 20 therein],
the presence of such graphs
is essential for the decoupling of massless excitation from on-shell amplitudes.
(
{\it ii}
) In addition,
the inclusions of the one-loop dressed
diagrams (
b_{3}
)-(
b_{6}
) appearing in the skeleton expansion of Fig.
7.9

must be duly taken into account.
(
{\it iii}
) Furthermore, motivated by the discussion presented at the end of Subsec.
8.6
, it would be important to study the possible numerical impact of graph (
h_{3}
) on the function
{\cal W}(r^{2})
.
Given that this entails a two-loop calculation, the practical implementation of this point
is rather challenging.
(
{\it iv}
) Finally,
as mentioned above,
the inclusion of the ghost-gluon and four-gluon vertices will
modify the expression for
I
given in Eq. (
8.34
), and may affect
the overall numerical picture.

Throughout this article we have focused
exclusively on the implementation of the
Schwinger mechanism within the
Landau gauge,
\xi=0
.
It should be emphasized, however, that
a detailed analysis carried out
in
[
55
]
reveals that the
mechanism persists at least within the interval

\xi\in[0,0.5]
. This picture is compatible
with related studies
[
281
,
282
]

involving the Nielsen identities
[
283
,
284
]
.

As has become clear
from the analysis presented in this work,
the formation of colored massless poles out of the
fusion of two gluons or of a ghost-antighost pair is an indispensable condition
for the generation of a gluon mass
scale through the Schwinger mechanism.
Quite interestingly, colored massless poles are also required
for the nonperturbative realization of the BRST quartet mechanism
[
139
,
285
]
; their
generation is controlled by
appropriate BSEs, exactly as happens
in the case of the Schwinger poles. Despite these similarities,
however, to date no conclusive connection has been established between these two mechanisms.

We end by pointing out that,
among the plethora of states predicted by QCD, the glueballs
[
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
66
,
67
,
68
]
and hybrids
[
286
,
287
,
288
,
289
]
contain “valence gluons”, and are thus expected to be sensitive to the gluon mass scale; the detection and classification of these states are among the primary goals of experiments such as BES III
[
290
,
291
]
and GlueX
[
292
,
293
]
.
In fact, as asserted in
[
294
]
,
their discovery will lead to
a paradigm-shifting reassessment of the distinction between matter
field and force fields, since the
massive gluons will be acting as both.
The gluon mass scale is also likely to be important in understanding the production of heavy mesons,
e.g.
,
J/\psi
and
\phi
, from a proton target, as these processes are generally
considered to be deeply connected to gluon physics
[
295
,
296
,
297
,
298
]
. Finally, the gluon mass scale should leave signals in gluon distribution functions
[
299
,
300
]
, for which data obtained in the planned EIC
[
301
]
and EicC
[
302
]
facilities will provide valuable insights.

Appendix A
Euclidean space conventions

In this Appendix we summarize the rules and conventions adopted throughout this work for transforming expressions from Minkowski to Euclidean space.

To begin with, for our numerical
studies we consider all physical momenta to be space-like; in particular,

r^{2}\to-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}
, where
r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
is an Euclidean momentum with
r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}>0
, and similarly for other momenta. At the level of the integral measure of Eq. (
2.53
), this transformation entails

\int_{k}=i\int_{k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}\,.

(A.1)

Then, for the propagators and their dressing functions, we adopt the conventions

\Delta_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\,-\Delta(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,\qquad D_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\,-D(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,\qquad{\cal Z}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\,{\cal Z}(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,\qquad F_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\,F(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,.

(A.2)

Next, for the pole-free form factor of the three-gluon vertex in the soft-gluon limit, and the function
{\cal W}(r^{2})
, both appearing in the WI displacement of Eq. (
6.20
), we define

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{\!{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,\qquad{\cal W}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})={\cal W}(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,.

(A.3)

Lastly, due to the derivatives with respect to squared momenta in the definitions of the functions
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
,
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
and
\mathbb{B}(r^{2})
[see Eqs. (
5.20
), (
5.22
) and (
7.18
), respectively], they are transformed to Euclidean space as

{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=-{\mathbb{C}}(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,\qquad{\mathcal{C}}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=\,-{\mathcal{C}}(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,,\qquad\mathbb{B}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}(r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})=-\mathbb{B}(-r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2})\,.

(A.4)

In order to evaluate the Euclidean integrals numerically, it is convenient to use hyperspherical coordinates. For the typical integrals encountered in this work,
e.g.
, Eqs. (
8.25
) and (
8.26
), involving a single external momentum,
r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
, and a virtual momentum,
k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
, we introduce the variables

x:=r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}\,,\qquad y:=k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}^{2}\,,\qquad r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}\cdot k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}:=\sqrt{xy}c_{\theta}\,,

(A.5)

where
\theta
is the angle between
r_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
and
k_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle E}}}
, and we write
c_{\theta}:=\cos\theta
and
s_{\theta}:=\sin\theta
. Then, the integral measure becomes

\int_{k}=\frac{i}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,y\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!d\theta s^{2}_{\theta}\,,

(A.6)

with a cutoff
\Lambda
introduced for numerical purposes.

Appendix B
The asymmetric MOM renormalization scheme

In this Appendix we introduce the renormalization scheme adopted throughout this work, namely the asymmetric MOM scheme
[
184
,
185
,
186
,
187
,
180
,
188
,
23
]
.

For the pure Yang-Mills SU(3) that we consider, a renormalization scheme is specified by prescribing values for three of the renormalization constants in Eq. (
2.35
); all others
are determined from the STIs,
i.e.
, through Eq. (
2.36
)
[
181
]
. In the MOM type of renormalization schemes
[
181
,
182
,
183
]
, this is achieved by imposing boundary conditions on the renormalized forms of three chosen Green functions.

Specifically, all MOM schemes require that the renormalized propagators assume tree-level values at the renormalization point
\mu
,
i.e.
,
[
181
,
182
,
183
]

\Delta^{-1}_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle R}}}(\mu^{2})=\mu^{2}\,,\qquad F_{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle R}}}(\mu^{2})=1\,.

(B.1)

Then, the third renormalization constant is specified by imposing that the classical form factor of one of the fundamental vertices also reduces to tree-level at a specified kinematic configuration
[
181
]
.

In Landau gauge studies, it is common to complete the renormalization prescription by adopting the so-called “Taylor scheme”
[
102
,
259
,
271
,
272
,
23
]
. This renormalization scheme takes advantage of the well-known Taylor theorem
[
102
]
, which states that in Landau gauge the fully dressed unrenormalized ghost-gluon vertex reduces to tree-level in the soft-ghost configuration,
i.e.
,

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\nu}(r,0,-r)=r_{\nu}\,.

(B.2)

The Taylor scheme is then defined by requiring that the renormalized ghost-gluon vertex also satisfies Eq. (
B.2
)
[
102
,
259
,
271
,
272
,
23
]
. Within this scheme, Eq. (
2.35
) implies that the ghost-gluon renormalization constant reduces to
Z_{1}=1
.

For the present work, where the soft-gluon form factor of the three-gluon vertex
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
, defined in Eq. (
6.17
), plays a key role, it is more convenient to adopt a renormalization scheme defined by imposing a boundary condition on
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
. Specifically, we impose that
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
reduces to tree-level at
r=\mu
,
i.e.
,

\mathit{L}_{{sg}}^{{\rm{\scriptscriptstyle R}}}(\mu^{2})=1\,.

(B.3)

Together, Eqs. (
B.1
) and (
B.3
) define the asymmetric MOM scheme, which has been extensively employed in nonperturbative studies of
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})

[
186
,
187
,
180
,
188
,
23
,
251
,
214
]
.

In contrast to the Taylor scheme, in the asymmetric MOM scheme the finite ghost-gluon renormalization constant is no longer equal to
1

[
180
,
23
]
.
The value of this
constant in
the asymmetric MOM scheme,
to be denoted by

{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}
,
has been determined through SDE studies to be
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}=0.9333\pm 0.0075

[
144
]
(see also Sec. 8 of
[
99
]
). Moreover, at the same value of the renormalization point, lattice simulations determine the renormalized coupling to be
\alpha_{s}(4.3\text{ GeV})=0.27

[
187
]
.

We point out that other choices of vertices and/or configurations to define MOM-type renormalization schemes have been explored in the literature. Some examples include the “symmetric MOM scheme”, wherein the classical form factor of the three-gluon vertex reduces to tree level at the symmetric point,
q^{2}=r^{2}=p^{2}=\mu^{2}

[
186
,
187
,
188
]
, and MOM type schemes defined by imposing a boundary condition on the four-gluon vertex
[
215
,
216
]
.

Appendix C
The seagull cancellation in the Landau gauge

In the demonstration of
the seagull cancellation in Subsec.
3.6
,
the value of the gauge-fixing parameter was left completely arbitrary; therefore, the main result captured by
Eq. (
3.45
)
is valid for general
\xi_{Q}
. Nevertheless, it is instructive to perform the derivation explicitly in the Landau gauge,
for the purpose of
elucidating a subtlety that arises within the PT-BFM framework. Specifically, as is clear from Eq. (
3.39
), the BQQ vertex,
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, is
ill-defined in the Landau gauge:
due to the presence of terms
proportional to
\xi_{Q}^{-1}
,
the limit
\xi_{Q}\to 0
may not
be taken directly.
The correct procedure is to
take this limit

after

the contraction
\Delta^{\rho\mu}(r)\Delta^{\sigma\nu}(p)\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
has been carried out;
then, one obtains a well-defined
result, as we will now show.

There are two key observations that are relevant for this construction. First, the terms proportional to
\xi_{Q}^{-1}
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
do not receive radiative corrections, retaining their tree-level form
to all orders
[
178
]
. Specifically,
we may write

\widetilde{{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+\xi_{Q}^{-1}(g_{\alpha\nu}r_{\mu}-g_{\alpha\mu}p_{\nu})\,,

(C.1)

where the
\xi_{Q}^{-1}
term is identical to that of
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
in Eq. (
3.39
), while
\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is well-defined at
\xi_{Q}=0
. At tree-level,
\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, and Eq. (
3.39
) is recovered. Moreover, it follows from Eqs. (
2.58
), (
2.19
) and (
C.1
), that the special vertex
\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
satisfies (for any
\xi_{Q}
) the STI
[
178
]

q^{\alpha}\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\Delta^{-1}(p^{2})P_{\mu\nu}(p)-\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})P_{\mu\nu}(r)\,.

(C.2)

Second, the terms proportional to
\xi^{-1}_{Q}
in Eq. (
C.1
) are
longitudinal, namely proportional to
r_{\mu}
and
p_{\nu}
; thus, when contracted with gluon propagators,
\Delta^{\rho\mu}(r)\Delta^{\sigma\nu}(p)
, they trigger the identity,

r_{\mu}\Delta^{\rho\mu}(r)=\xi_{Q}\frac{r^{\rho}}{r^{2}}\,.

(C.3)

As a result,

\Delta^{\rho\mu}(r)\Delta^{\sigma\nu}(p)\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\Delta^{\rho\mu}(r)\Delta^{\sigma\nu}(p)\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+\frac{r^{\rho}}{r^{2}}\Delta^{\sigma}_{\alpha}(p)-\frac{p^{\sigma}}{p^{2}}\Delta^{\rho}_{\alpha}(p)\,,

(C.4)

which is free of
\xi_{Q}^{-1}
terms, and can be safely set to the Landau gauge value, wherein

\lim_{\xi_{Q}\to 0}\Delta^{\rho\mu}(r)\Delta^{\sigma\nu}(p)\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\Delta(r^{2})\Delta(p^{2})P^{\rho\mu}(r)P^{\sigma\nu}(p)\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+\frac{r^{\rho}}{r^{2}}P^{\sigma}_{\alpha}(p)\Delta(p^{2})-\frac{p^{\sigma}}{p^{2}}P^{\rho}_{\alpha}(r)\Delta(r^{2})\,.

(C.5)

Note that the last two terms originate from the product of a term proportional to
\xi_{Q}
,
coming from a gluon propagator, and a term proportional to
\xi_{Q}^{-1}
, originating from
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
.

Armed with these results, we may
now demonstrate the seagull cancellation explicitly in the Landau gauge.
Using Eq. (
C.5
), the self-energy terms
{\tilde{a}}_{1}
and
{\tilde{a}}_{2}
of Eqs. (
3.36
) and (
3.37
) can be expressed at
\xi_{Q}=0
as

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{1}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\left\{\Delta^{2}(k^{2})P^{\alpha\rho}(k)P^{\beta\sigma}(k)\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}(0,k,-k)+\frac{\Delta(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\left[k^{\alpha}P^{\beta\mu}(k)+k^{\beta}P^{\alpha\mu}(k)\right]\right\}\,,

(C.6)

\displaystyle{\tilde{a}}_{2}=

\displaystyle\,-g^{2}C_{\rm A}\frac{(d-1)^{2}}{d}\int_{k}\Delta(k^{2})\,.

(C.7)

Next, the STI of Eq. (
C.2
), together with the assumption that
\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is pole free at
q=0
, imply the WI

\widetilde{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}\vphantom{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}(0,k,-k)=\frac{\partial[\Delta^{-1}(k^{2})P_{\rho\sigma}(k)]}{\partial k_{\mu}}=-2k^{\mu}P_{\rho\sigma}(k)\Delta^{-2}(k^{2})\frac{\partial\Delta(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}-\frac{\Delta^{-1}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\left(g^{\mu}_{\rho}k_{\sigma}+g^{\mu}_{\sigma}k_{\rho}-\frac{2k^{\mu}k_{\rho}k_{\sigma}}{k^{2}}\right)\,.

(C.8)

Note that the term in parenthesis is annihilated by the projectors
P^{\alpha\rho}(k)P^{\beta\sigma}(k)
in Eq. (
C.6
), such that

{\tilde{a}}_{1}=\frac{g^{2}C_{\rm A}}{2d}\int_{k}\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)\left\{-2k^{\mu}P^{\alpha\beta}(k)\frac{\partial\Delta(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{\Delta(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\left[k^{\alpha}P^{\beta\mu}(k)+k^{\beta}P^{\alpha\mu}(k)\right]\right\}\,.

(C.9)

To complete the demonstration, we use that

k^{\mu}P^{\alpha\beta}(k)\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)=2(d-1)k^{2}\,,\qquad\left[k^{\alpha}P^{\beta\mu}(k)+k^{\beta}P^{\alpha\mu}(k)\right]\Gamma_{\!0\,\mu\alpha\beta}(0,k,-k)=-2(d-1)k^{2}\,,

(C.10)

which entail

{\tilde{a}}_{1}=-\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}(d-1)}{d}\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\frac{\partial\Delta(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{k}\Delta(k^{2})\right]\,.

(C.11)

Hence, combining the above with Eq. (
C.7
) yields

{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}(0)={\tilde{a}}_{1}+{\tilde{a}}_{2}=-\frac{2g^{2}C_{\rm A}(d-1)}{d}\underbrace{\left[\int_{k}k^{2}\frac{\partial\Delta(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{d}{2}\int_{k}\Delta(k^{2})\right]}_{\text{seagull identity}}=0\,,

(C.12)

where the seagull identity of Eq. (
3.12
) is once again triggered. We emphasize that the last two terms of Eq. (
C.5
), resulting from the subtle cancellation of the gauge-fixing parameter in the PT-BFM scheme, are crucial for the vanishing of
{\widetilde{\Pi}}^{(1)}(0)
.

Finally, as an alternative to the

\xi_{Q}
-independent demonstration of Subsec.
3.6
,
one may opt for repeating the above procedure
for general
\xi_{Q}
.
In that case, the terms quadratic in
\xi_{Q}
, present in Eq. (
C.4
), vanish when contracted with
\Gamma_{\!0\,\alpha\mu\nu}(0,k,-k)
in Eq. (
C.6
). Then, after some algebra and the use of Eq. (
C.8
), the terms linear in
\xi_{Q}
, appearing in both
{\tilde{a}}_{1}
and
{\tilde{a}}_{2}
, can be shown to be proportional to the integral
in Eq. (
3.1
) with
n=0
,
which is itself a special case of the seagull identity, with
\Delta(k^{2})=1/k^{2}
and
d\neq 2
.

Appendix D
SDE implementation of the seagull cancellation: an example

The implementation of the seagull
cancellation at the level of the SDEs raises certain practical issues that deserve our attention. In particular, while the derivation of the seagull identity relies on the
use of a regularization scheme
that preserves the translational invariance of the integral,
the numerical treatment of the SDEs employs typically a hard ultraviolet cutoff, which breaks this invariance. The way around this
apparent difficulty is to implement the seagull at the level of the SDEs
before

the numerical treatment is initiated
[
95
]
.

To see this in the simple context of scalar QED,
consider the two integrals given in
Eq. (
3.20
),
and use for the vertex

\Gamma_{\nu}(-q,t,-k)
an

Ansatz
that satisfies automatically the WTI of Eq. (
3.21
). In particular, we use
the standard choice
[
303
,
304
]

\Gamma_{\nu}(-q,t,-k)=(t+k)_{\nu}{u}(q,k)\,,\qquad{u}(q,k):=\frac{{\cal D}^{-1}(t^{2})-{\cal D}^{-1}(k^{2})}{t^{2}-k^{2}}\,,

(D.1)

such that

(d_{1})_{\mu\nu}=e^{2}\int_{k}(t+k)_{\mu}(t+k)_{\nu}{\cal D}(k^{2}){\cal D}(t^{2})u(q,k)=-e^{2}\int_{k}(t+k)_{\mu}(t+k)_{\nu}f(q,k)\,,

(D.2)

where

f(q,k):=\frac{{\cal D}(t^{2})-{\cal D}(k^{2})}{t^{2}-k^{2}}\,,

(D.3)

while
(d_{2})_{\mu\nu}

is given by the second
integral in Eq. (
3.20
).

Evidently, at
q=0
,

f(0,k)=\frac{\partial{\cal D}(k^{2})}{\partial k^{2}}\,.

(D.4)

Then, since from
Eq. (
3.22
) we have that

(d-1)\Pi^{(1)}(q^{2})=\Pi^{(1)\mu}_{\mu}(q)=(d_{1})_{\mu}^{\mu}+(d_{2})_{\mu}^{\mu}\,,

(D.5)

we find that

(d-1)\Pi^{(1)}(q^{2})=-e^{2}\left[\int_{k}(t+k)^{2}f(q,k)+2d\int_{k}{\cal D}(k^{2})\right]\,.

(D.6)

Using

(t+k)^{2}=4k^{2}-q^{2}+2(t^{2}-k^{2})
and that

\int_{k}(t^{2}-k^{2})f(q,k)=0
,
we find

(d-1)\Pi^{(1)}(q^{2})=-e^{2}\left[\int_{k}(4k^{2}-q^{2})f(q,k)+2d\int_{k}{\cal D}(k^{2})\right]\,.

(D.7)

Setting
q=0
into
Eq. (
D.7
),
and using
Eq. (
D.4
), we recover immediately the seagull identity of Eq. (
3.27
).

Note at this point that if
the r.h.s. of Eq. (
D.7
) were
to be computed numerically, using
a hard ultraviolet cutoff, the answer would come out quadratically divergent.
In other words, the failure
to impose correctly
\Pi^{(1)}(0)=0
does not simply lead to the erroneous result of a photon with a finite mass; instead,
it gives rise
to a quadratically divergent
contribution,
whose
disposal requires the introduction of a mass counter-term
m^{2}A^{2}_{\mu}
at the level of the Lagrangian, thus violating
gauge invariance.

Instead, the correct way to proceed is to exploit the fact that the properly regulated Eq. (
D.7
) has the result

\Pi^{(1)}(0)=0
built in.
In particular, we may subtract

\Pi^{(1)}(0)=0
from
both sides of Eq. (
D.7
), to
get

(d-1)\Pi^{(1)}(q^{2})=-4e^{2}\int_{k}k^{2}[f(q,k)-f(0,k)]+e^{2}q^{2}\int_{k}f(q,k)\,,

(D.8)

which has the advantage
of vanishing
manifestly

at
q=0
, and being free of
quadratic divergences.
Thus, the remaining divergences are simply those disposed of by means of conventional
renormalization;
once this last step has been successfully implemented and
a finite answer has been reached,
the use of a
hard cutoff may be employed for the final numerical evaluation.

Appendix E
BQIs for the pole amplitudes

In this Appendix, we derive the important identities of Eq. (
5.37
), relating the displacement functions

{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
with their background anologues
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}
, respectively. As a by-product of our analysis, we recover the result captured by Eq. (
5.21
) in the main text.

We begin by deriving the relation between
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})
,
i.e.
, the second line of Eq. (
5.37
). Our starting point is the BQI connecting the conventional and background ghost-gluon vertices,
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\mu}(r,p,q)
and

\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\mu}(r,p,q)
, namely
[
111
]

\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\mu}(r,p,q)=\left\{\left[1+G(q^{2})\right]g^{\nu}_{\mu}+L(q^{2})\frac{q_{\mu}q^{\nu}}{q^{2}}\right\}{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\nu}(r,p,q)+F^{-1}(p^{2})p^{\nu}K_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)+r^{2}F^{-1}(r^{2})K_{\mu}(r,q,p)\,,

(E.1)

where
K_{\mu}
and
K_{\mu\nu}
are the auxiliary functions shown in Fig.
E.1
, whereas
G(q^{2})
and
L(q^{2})
are the scalar form factors of
\Lambda_{\mu\nu}(q)
, defined in Eq. (
2.62
).

Figure E.1:
The auxiliary functions
K_{\mu}(q,r,p)
and
K_{\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
, appearing in the BQI of Eq. (
E.1
).

Now, using Eqs. (
5.2
) and (
5.47
), both
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\mu}(r,p,q)
and
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\mu}(r,p,q)
introduce Schwinger poles of the form
q_{\mu}/q^{2}
into Eq. (
E.1
). On the other hand,
K_{\mu}(r,q,p)
has no external gluon legs,
and hence no Schwinger poles, whereas
K_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)
can have poles at
p=0
, but not at
q=0
. Hence, equating residues of
1/q^{2}
in Eq. (
E.1
) yields

{\widetilde{C}}(r,p,q)=\left[1+G(q^{2})+L(q^{2})\right]C(r,p,q)\,.

(E.2)

Next, we expand Eq. (
E.2
) around
q=0
and equate coefficients of equal orders to relate
C(r,-r,0)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})
with their background counterparts. Specifically, using Eqs. (
2.65
), (
5.22
) and (
5.51
) and
L(0)=0

[
220
]
, we obtain

{\widetilde{C}}(r,-r,0)+2(q\cdot r){\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(r^{2})=F^{-1}(0)\left[C(r,-r,0)+2(q\cdot r){\mathcal{C}}(r^{2})\right]+{\cal O}(q^{2})\,.

(E.3)

Hence, equating zeroth order coefficients in Eq. (
E.3
) yields

C(r,-r,0)=F(0){\widetilde{C}}(r,-r,0)\,,

(E.4)

which combined with Eq. (
5.50
) implies Eq. (
5.21
).

Finally, the linear order terms lead to the result announced in the second line of Eq. (
5.37
).

In order to obtain the first line of Eq. (
5.37
), relating
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
to
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
, we use the BQI that converts between the
QQQ
and
BQQ
vertices, given by
[
111
]

\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)=\left\{\left[1+G(q^{2})\right]g^{\rho}_{\alpha}+L(q^{2})\frac{q_{\alpha}q^{\rho}}{q^{2}}\right\}{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\rho\mu\nu}(q,r,p)+K_{\rho\nu\alpha}(r,q,p)P^{\rho}_{\mu}(r)\Delta^{-1}(r^{2})-K_{\rho\mu\alpha}(p,q,r)P^{\rho}_{\nu}(p)\Delta^{-1}(p^{2})\,,

(E.5)

where
K_{\rho\nu\alpha}(r,q,p)
is the auxiliary function defined in Eq. (
6.1
).

While the vertices
\widetilde{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
and
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
contain Schwinger poles in all channels [see Eq. (
5.12
)], only the simple poles at
q=0
are relevant for deriving Eq. (
5.37
). Then, note that the auxiliary functions
K_{\rho\mu\alpha}(p,q,r)
and
K_{\rho\nu\alpha}(r,q,p)
can only contain Schwinger poles of the form
r_{\mu}/r^{2}
and
p_{\nu}/p^{2}
, respectively. Hence, using Eqs. (
5.1
) and (
5.12
) and their background analogues, and isolating pole structures of the form
q_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}/q^{2}
in Eq. (
E.5
), furnishes

{\widetilde{V}}_{1}(q,r,p)=\left[1+G(q^{2})+L(q^{2})\right]V_{1}(q,r,p)\,.

(E.6)

At this point, we perform a Taylor expansion of Eq. (
E.6
) around
q=0
, to obtain

{\widetilde{V}}_{1}(0,r,-r)+2(q\cdot r)\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})=F(0)\left[V_{1}(0,r,-r)+2(q\cdot r)\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})\right]+{\cal O}(q^{2})\,,

(E.7)

where we used again Eq. (
2.65
),
L(0)=0
, and the definitions of
{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}(r^{2})
through Eq. (
5.20
) and its background equivalent.

Hence, equating zeroth order coefficients in Eq. (
E.7
), and using
Eq. (
5.19
), we get

{\widetilde{V}}_{1}(0,r,-r)=F^{-1}(0)V_{1}(0,r,-r)=0\,,

(E.8)

while the first-order terms lead to the first line in Eq. (
5.37
), which is the main result of this Appendix.

Appendix F
Running versus pole mass

A natural question that arises in the context of gluon mass generation is whether the gluon propagator develops a pole mass,
i.e.
, if the propagator has a pole at a timelike momentum
q_{\star}
, with
q^{2}_{\star}=-M^{2}
(Euclidean space). Such a pole mass would signal that the gluon appears in the physical spectrum, which should not happen in a confined theory as QCD. In this appendix we show with a simple example how such an unwanted feature may be avoided.

In the case of a tree level massive propagator,
\Delta^{-1}(q^{2})=q^{2}+m^{2}
, the pole mass and the value of
\Delta^{-1}(0)
are the same. However, this is not necessarily so if the mass scale is generated dynamically. In the latter case, recalling Eq. (
2.21
), the condition for the gluon propagator to have a pole mass,
q^{2}_{\star}
, is that

\Delta^{-1}(q_{\star}^{2})=q^{2}_{\star}+i\Pi(q^{2}_{\star})=0\,.

(F.1)

Now, since
\Pi(q^{2})
depends on the momentum, the existence or not of a pole mass hinges on the precise momentum dependence of
\Pi(q^{2})
in the timelike region; this is a particularly complicated
technical issue that
is beyond the scope of this work,
see,
e.g.
,
[
237
]
.
Nevertheless, we can illustrate through a physically motivated toy model that it is possible for
\Delta^{-1}(0)\neq 0
, and still Eq. (
F.1
) not have solutions.

Specifically, let us assume for simplicity that
\Pi(q^{2})
is given by the form
i\Pi(q^{2})=m^{2}(q^{2})
, where
m^{2}(q^{2})
is a “running gluon mass”
[
31
,
253
,
56
]
, which goes to zero at large
q^{2}
, so that the propagator reproduces its perturbative behavior in the ultraviolet. Concretely, we take for
m^{2}(q^{2})
a functional form that has been widely explored in the related literature
[
276
,
140
,
56
]
, namely,

m^{2}(q^{2})=\frac{m_{0}^{2}}{1+\rho^{2}q^{2}}\,,

(F.2)

with
m_{0}
and
\rho
positive. Then, the gluon propagator reads explicitly,

\Delta^{-1}(q^{2})=q^{2}+m^{2}(q^{2})=\frac{m_{0}^{2}+q^{2}+\rho^{2}q^{4}}{1+\rho^{2}q^{2}}\,,

(F.3)

and its poles,
i.e.
, the solutions to Eq. (
F.1
), are located at

q^{2}_{\star}=\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}\left(-1\pm\sqrt{1-4\rho^{2}m_{0}^{2}}\right)\,.

(F.4)

For a given
m_{0}
,
i.e.
, for a fixed
\Delta(0)
, the solutions fall in one of the following categories, depending on the value of
\rho
:

(
i
) If
\rho=0
,
i.e.
, for a constant mass, the propagator has a single simple pole at
q^{2}_{\star}=-m^{2}_{0}
;

(
ii
) For
0<\rho<1/(2m_{0})
the propagator has two simple poles;

(
iii
) For
\rho=1/(2m_{0})
, it has a double pole at
q^{2}=-2m_{0}
;

(
iv
) Finally, if
\rho>1/(2m_{0})
,
\Delta(q^{2})
does not have a pole mass, exhibiting instead a pair of complex conjugate poles.

Figure F.1:

Left:
Toy model for the running gluon mass,
m^{2}(q^{2})
, given by Eq. (
F.2
), with fixed
m_{0}=354
MeV, and
\rho=0
(blue continuous),
\rho=0.91\text{ GeV}^{-1}<1/(2m_{0})
(red dashed),
\rho=1/(2m_{0})
(green dot-dashed), and
\rho=2.91\text{ GeV}^{-1}>1/(2m_{0})
(purple dotted).
Right:
Corresponding inverse gluon propagator,
\Delta^{-1}(q^{2})
, given by Eq. (
F.3
).

These different situations are illustrated in Fig.
F.1
, where we show the running gluon mass,
m^{2}(q^{2})
, on the left panel, and the resulting inverse propagator,
\Delta^{-1}(q^{2})
, on the right. For this example, we set
m_{0}=354
MeV, corresponding to the saturation value of the lattice gluon propagator in
[
23
]
, renormalized at
\mu=4.3
GeV, such that
1/(2m_{0})=1.41
GeV
-1
, and consider values of
\rho
in each of the above categories: (
i
)
\rho=0
(blue continuous), (
ii
)
\rho=0.91\text{ GeV}^{-1}<1/(2m_{0})
(red dashed), (
iii
)
\rho=1/(2m_{0})
(green dot-dashed), and (
iv
)
\rho=2.91\text{ GeV}^{-1}>1/(2m_{0})
(purple dotted). Then, at least in the simplified case of Eq. (
F.3
), the gluon propagator can saturate at the origin without having a pole mass, provided that
m^{2}(q^{2})
falls off sufficiently fast at large momenta,
i.e.
, for
\rho>1/(2m_{0})
.

Appendix G
Computing
{\cal W}(r^{2})

An important component of the WI satisfied by the conventional three-gluon vertex, Eq. (
6.20
), is the function
{\cal W}(r^{2})
, originating from the ghost-gluon kernel. In this Appendix we derive the dynamical equation governing its momentum evolution
[
305
,
142
]
, and compute
{\cal W}(r^{2})
numerically using lattice inputs
[
144
]
.

To begin with, from Eq. (
6.14
) follows that
{\cal W}(r^{2})
can be obtained from
K_{\mu\nu\alpha}(r,0,-r)
through the projection

{\cal W}(r^{2})=-\frac{1}{3}r^{\alpha}P^{\mu\nu}(r)K_{\mu\nu\alpha}(r,0,-r)=-\frac{1}{3}r^{\alpha}P^{\mu\nu}(r)\left[\frac{\partial H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)}{\partial q^{\alpha}}\right]_{q=0}\,,

(G.1)

where we use Eq. (
6.13
) to finally express
{\cal W}(r^{2})
directly in terms of the ghost-gluon kernel.

Next, let us recall that
H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)
is related to the ghost-gluon vertex,
\Gamma_{\nu}(r,q,p)
, through the STI
[
102
]

{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\nu}(r,q,p)=r^{\mu}H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)\,.

(G.2)

Then, from Eqs. (
G.1
) and (
G.2
), we conclude that
H_{\nu\mu}(r,q,p)
and
{\cal W}(r^{2})
are renormalized by the same multiplicative constant,
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}
, as
\Gamma_{\nu}(r,q,p)
,
i.e.
,

{\cal W}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R}}(r^{2})={\widetilde{Z}}_{1}{\cal W}(r^{2})\,.

(G.3)

In particular,
{\cal W}(r^{2})
is ultraviolet finite in the Landau gauge, and its renormalization amounts to a finite rescaling.

Figure G.1:
SDE for the ghost-gluon scattering kernel,
H_{\mu\nu}(r,q,p)
.

Now, the ghost-gluon kernel satisfies the SDE given diagrammatically in Fig.
G.1
, where the yellow ellipse is the 1PI ghost-ghost-gluon-gluon vertex,
\Gamma_{\nu\sigma}
; at tree-level
\Gamma_{\nu\sigma}=0
. To date,
\Gamma_{\nu\sigma}
has only been computed nonperturbatively in
[
280
]
, where it was found that it has only a
\sim 2\%
effect when inserted into the SDE of the ghost-gluon vertex, in the symmetric limit. In what follows, we will assume that the impact of
\Gamma_{\nu\sigma}
in the behavior of
{\cal W}(r^{2})
is also small, and will neglect diagram
h_{3}^{\nu\mu}
;
see, however, the related discussion at the end of Subsec.
8.6
.

With this approximation, the dynamical equation determining
{\cal W}(r^{2})
is given by

{\cal W}(r^{2})={\cal W}_{1}(r^{2})+{\cal W}_{2}(r^{2})\,,

(G.4)

where the
{\cal W}_{i}(r^{2})
are, respectively, the results of applying Eq. (
G.1
) to each of the diagrams
(h_{i}^{\mu\nu})
of Fig.
G.1
, for
i=1,\,2
. After renormalization through Eqs. (
2.35
), (
2.36
) and (
G.3
), the
{\cal W}_{i}(r^{2})
read

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{1}(r^{2})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{ig^{2}C_{\rm A}{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}}{6}\int_{k}\Delta(k^{2})D(k^{2})D(v^{2})(r\cdot k)f(k,r)B_{1}(v,-k,-r)B_{1}(k,0,-k)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{2}(r^{2})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{ig^{2}C_{\rm A}{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}}{6}\int_{k}\Delta(k^{2})\Delta(v^{2})D(v^{2})B_{1}(v,0,-v){\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(-r,-k,v)\,,

(G.5)

where
v:=k+r
, while
f(k,r)
is given by

f(k,r)=1-\frac{(r\cdot k)^{2}}{r^{2}k^{2}}\,.

(G.6)

In Eq. (
G.5
) appear contributions from the dressed ghost-gluon and three-gluon vertices present in Fig.
G.1
. Specifically:

(
i
)
B_{1}(r,p,q)
is the classical form factor of the ghost-gluon vertex, whose general tensor structure is given in Eq. (
5.6
); at tree-level
B_{1}^{(0)}=1
. Note that
B_{1}(r,p,q)
can be obtained from
{\rm{I}}\!\Gamma_{\alpha}(r,p,q)
through the transverse projection given by Eqs. (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) [with
C_{1}(r,p,q)=0
]; hence, it is free of Schwinger poles.

Moreover,
B_{1}(r,p,q)
is a lattice observable in Landau gauge. However, for SU(3),
B_{1}(r,p,q)
has so far only been evaluated on the lattice in the soft-gluon limit
[
11
,
244
,
245
]
, while its general kinematics form is necessary for the calculation of
{\cal W}(r^{2})
. Thus, we determine
B_{1}(r,p,q)
instead through its own SDE, truncated so as to preserve the STI of Eq. (
G.2
). The detailed analysis is presented in Sec. 8 of
[
99
]
, and the result is shown on the right panel of Fig. 13 therein. We point out that in the soft-gluon limit this
B_{1}(r,p,q)
reproduces the lattice data of
[
11
,
244
]
(see Fig. 14 of
[
99
]
), and agrees qualitatively with various continuum studies
[
306
,
307
,
230
,
162
,
308
,
213
,
116
,
270
,
117
]
.

(
ii
) The total contribution of the three-gluon vertex to the kernel of
{\cal W}(r^{2})
is denoted by
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(q,r,p)
, and is given by the projection

{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(q,r,p):=\frac{1}{2}(q-r)^{\nu}\overline{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{\alpha\nu}(q,r,p)\,,

(G.7)

where
\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha\mu\nu}(q,r,p)
is the transversely projected three-gluon vertex, defined in Eq. (
8.57
). Evidently,
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(q,r,p)
is free of Schwinger poles.

In order to compute
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(q,r,p)
, we capitalize on the planar degeneracy property of the three-gluon gluon vertex
[
212
,
211
,
278
,
116
,
117
,
188
,
279
,
250
,
214
,
99
,
251
]
. Specifically, substituting Eq. (
8.58
) into Eq. (
G.7
) we obtain a compact and yet accurate expression for
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(q,r,p)
, namely

\displaystyle{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(q,r,p)

\displaystyle\approx{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}^{0}(q,r,p)\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(s^{2})\,,\qquad s^{2}:=\frac{1}{2}(q^{2}+r^{2}+p^{2})\,,

(G.8)

where
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}^{0}(q,r,p)
is the tree-level value of
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}
, given by

{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}^{0}(q,r,p):=\frac{2f(q,r)}{p^{2}}\left[2q^{2}r^{2}-(q^{2}+r^{2})(q\cdot r)-(q\cdot r)^{2}\right]\,,

(G.9)

with
f(q,r)
the function defined in Eq. (
G.6
). Note that Eq. (
G.8
) becomes exact in the limit
p=0

[
144
]
.

Importantly, since
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}(q,r,p)
is a transverse projection it can be simulated directly on the lattice. This was done in general kinematics in
[
144
]
, where it was shown that the error in Eq. (
G.8
) is less than
10\%
for most of the kinematic region probed. Near the diagonal
q^{2}=r^{2}
, which is numerically the most important in the computation of
{\cal W}

[
142
]
, this error falls below
1\%

[
144
]
.

Then, using Eq. (
G.8
) for the
{\cal I}_{{\cal W}}
in Eq. (
G.5
), the expression
{\cal W}_{2}(r^{2})
reduces to

{\cal W}_{2}(r^{2})=\frac{ig^{2}C_{\rm A}{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}}{3}\int_{k}\Delta(k^{2})\frac{\Delta(v^{2})D(v^{2})}{v^{2}}B_{1}(v,0,-v)f(k,r)\left[2r^{2}k^{2}-(r^{2}+k^{2})(r\cdot k)-(r\cdot k)^{2}\right]\mathit{L}_{{sg}}({\hat{s}}^{2})\,,

(G.10)

where we
{\hat{s}}^{2}=r^{2}+k^{2}+(r\cdot k)
.

For the numerical evaluation, we pass Eq. (
G.4
) to Euclidean space, using the rules and conventions of App.
A
, to obtain

{\cal W}(x)={\cal W}_{1}(x)+{\cal W}_{2}(x)\,,

(G.11)

with the
{\cal W}_{i}(x)
given by

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\rm A}{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}}{12\pi^{2}}\!\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,y\sqrt{xy}\Delta(y)F(y)B_{1}(y,y,\pi)\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!d\theta\,\frac{F(z)}{z}B_{1}(z,x,\chi)s_{\theta}^{4}c_{\theta}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\rm A}{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}}{6\pi^{2}}\!\int_{0}^{\Lambda^{2}}\!\!dy\,y^{2}\sqrt{xy}\Delta(y)\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!d\theta\,\frac{\Delta(z)F(z)}{z^{2}}B_{1}(z,z,\pi)\mathit{L}_{{sg}}\left(x+y+\sqrt{xy}c_{\theta}\right)s_{\theta}^{4}\left[\sqrt{xy}(2+c_{\theta}^{2})-zc_{\theta}\right]\,,

(G.12)

where
z:=x+y+2\sqrt{xy}c_{\theta}
and

\chi:=\cos^{-1}\left[-\frac{(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}c_{\theta})}{\sqrt{z}}\right]\,.

(G.13)

At this point, we can evaluate Eq. (
G.11
) to obtain
{\cal W}(r^{2})
. We use the fits to lattice data of
[
23
]
for
\Delta(q^{2})
and
F(q^{2})
, and of
[
188
]
for
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(q^{2})
, shown previously in Fig.
6.2
. All of these ingredients are renormalized in the asymmetric MOM scheme with
\mu=4.3
GeV, for which the corresponding value of the coupling is
\alpha_{s}(4.3\text{ GeV})=0.27

[
187
]
and
{\widetilde{Z}}_{1}=0.933

[
144
]
. Finally, for
B_{1}
we employ the general kinematics SDE result of
[
99
]
.

With the above ingredients, we obtain for
{\cal W}(r^{2})
the blue continuous curve on the left panel of Fig.
8.4
. Propagating the statistical error of the lattice
\mathit{L}_{{sg}}(r^{2})
to the
{\cal W}(r^{2})
yields the error estimate shown as a blue band on the left panel of Fig.
8.4
, whereas the effect of the errors of
\Delta(r^{2})
and
F(q^{2})
are negligible; for the details of this error analysis, see
[
144
]
.
